React Native & Expo: Build and Ship a Mobile App · Answer Key

Part 1: Foundations and Lists

Sessions 1 to 9. Modules 1 to 3: how React Native turns components into native views and what Expo adds, a working toolchain (Node LTS, VS Code with Expo Tools, the shoreline-api mock back end, an Expo SDK 58 project running in Expo Go or the web preview), the shared code that comes over from Shoreline Crew HQ, then the core components and layout (View, Text and StyleSheet, flexbox, images, icons, fonts and safe areas, touch and input, responsive, platform-specific and themed UI) and fast lists (FlatList, SectionList, FlashList). Runs on its own as a short course and ends with the Part 1 project check-in and assessment.

Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 1: How React Native Works and Your Toolchain

Answers and reasoning for every Session 1 item: the diagnostic, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 1 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Diagnostic

Not graded. Each reason also says where the skill comes back in this course. D1 to D3 are real runs (Node), D5 a real Jest run (the test that expects 1 passes); D4 is TypeScript's narrowing rule.

D1
What does this print?
const events = [
  { title: 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', capacity: 18, spotsTaken: 18 },
  { title: 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean', capacity: 10, spotsTaken: 9 },
]
console.log(events.filter((e) => e.spotsTaken < e.capacity).map((e) => e.title))
A) [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup' ]
B) [ 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
C) [ 9 ]
D) [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
Why: filter keeps the items whose test is true (9 is under 10; 18 is not under 18); map turns each kept item into its title. Skill: filter and map. Where it comes back: every list from Lesson 1.3 on (Module 3's FlatList renders exactly this).
D2
What does this print?
const jetty = { title: 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean', capacity: 10, spotsTaken: 9 }
const signedUp = { ...jetty, spotsTaken: jetty.spotsTaken + 1 }
console.log(jetty.spotsTaken, signedUp.spotsTaken)
A) 10 10
B) 9 9
C) 9 10
D) An error: jetty is a const
Why: Spread makes a NEW object; the original keeps 9. React compares objects to see what changed, so state is copied, never edited in place. Skill: copying with one change. Where it comes back: every state update (Lesson 2.4 on) and TanStack Query's cache (Module 5).
D3
What does this print?
const spotsLeft = 0
console.log(spotsLeft || 'unknown', spotsLeft ?? 'unknown')
A) 0 0
B) unknown unknown
C) 0 unknown
D) unknown 0
Why: || replaces every falsy value, 0 included; ?? only null and undefined. A full cleanup has 0 spots left: a real answer, not a missing one. Skill: || and ??. Where it comes back: Lesson 1.3 (the HQ's helpers) and every value that can legitimately be 0.
D4
What type does TypeScript give status on the last line?
type Status = 'scheduled' | 'completed' | 'cancelled'
function label(status: Status) {
  if (status === 'cancelled') return 'Cancelled'
  return status
}
A) Status
B) string
C) 'scheduled' | 'completed'
D) never
Why: After the early return, 'cancelled' is ruled out: TypeScript narrows the union to the two words that are left. Skill: TypeScript narrowing. Where it comes back: Lesson 1.3 (type guards) and every status, role and screen state.
D5
count starts at 0. One press runs setCount(count + 1) twice. What does the screen show after the press?
A) 2 signed up
B) 1 signed up
C) 0 signed up
D) An error: state set twice
Why: Both calls read the same count (0) from that render, so both ask for 1. The kit ran it in Jest: the test that expects 1 passes. setCount((n) => n + 1) twice would give 2. Skill: React state. Where it comes back: Lesson 2.4 (touch and input) and every press handler.
D6
Your terminal is in your home folder; the course folder is shoreline inside it. Which runs the environment check?
A) node check_environment.mjs
B) cd shoreline, then node labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.mjs
C) npm run check_environment
D) npx expo check_environment
Why: A path is looked up from the folder you are in. Every course command starts in the course folder or in one of its projects (the prompt shows which). Skill: folders and the terminal. Where it comes back: Lesson 1.2 (two terminals, the right folder) and every npx command after it.

D1, D2 and D3, run for real:

[ 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
9 10
unknown 0

D5, in Jest with React Native Testing Library (one press, both calls read the same count):

 PASS  src/d5.test.tsx
  ✓ D5
Tests:       1 passed, 1 total

1.1 · How React Native Works: self-check

Q1
On an Android phone, what does the CleanupCard's <Text> become?
A) A <p> element inside a hidden browser
B) A native text view, created by the Fabric renderer
C) A picture of the text that Hermes draws
D) A WebView that shows the card's HTML
Why: React Native renders to the platform's own building blocks: Fabric creates a native view for each component (TextView-like on Android, UILabel-like on iOS). Only the web preview turns <Text> into HTML, a <div dir="auto"> (Lesson 1.1, Native Views, Not a DOM).
A is wrong: There is no browser and no DOM in the app.
C is wrong: Hermes runs your JavaScript; it does not draw.
D is wrong: A WebView is the other approach (a website inside an app); React Native does not use one.
Q2
Predict: this card renders {title} straight inside the <View>. What does a phone (or Jest) say, and does npx tsc --noEmit complain?
<View style={styles.card}>
  {title}
  <Text>{spotsLeft} spots left</Text>
</View>
An error: Text strings must be rendered within a <Text> component. (a red screen on a phone); tsc says nothing
Why: Every piece of text sits in a <Text>. The type checker allows any child in a View, so only running it shows the problem: on a phone a red error screen, in Jest an Invariant Violation with the same words (the kit's demo and Lab 1.1, Task 1).
Q3
You copy onClick={() => setJoined(!joined)} from your web code onto a <Pressable>. What happens?
A) tsc reports that onClick does not exist on Pressable
B) The phone shows a red error screen
C) Nothing happens when a finger presses it, and no tool complains
D) It works exactly like onPress
Why: React Native's touch prop is onPress. onClick type-checks and lints clean, and a press does nothing: a silent bug only a test or a finger finds (the kit's Predict P1 and P2, Lab 1.1 Task 2).
A is wrong: The kit ran it: npx tsc --noEmit printed nothing.
B is wrong: No error at all: that is the danger.
D is wrong: A test that presses it fails: Unable to find an element with role: button, name: You're in!
Q4
Which piece of Shoreline Crew HQ can the phone app use exactly as it is?
A) A CSS Module for the event cards
B) A helper that saves the session in localStorage
C) An <img src> for the site photos
D) A Zod schema that checks an API response
Why: Plain TypeScript and JavaScript libraries with no browser API carry over unchanged (the lesson's "what carries over" table); Lesson 1.3 brings the shared code across.
A is wrong: There is no CSS on a phone: StyleSheet objects (Lesson 2.1).
B is wrong: localStorage is a browser API: AsyncStorage and SecureStore (Lesson 5.3).
C is wrong: <img> becomes an Image component with a source and a size (Lesson 2.3).
Q5
Which part of Expo builds an iPhone app in the cloud, without a Mac?
A) EAS Build
B) Expo Go
C) Metro
D) Expo Router
Why: EAS (Expo Application Services) builds for the stores, iOS included, in the cloud, with a free plan (Lesson 9.2). The course never requires a Mac.
B is wrong: Expo Go runs your project while you develop; it builds nothing.
C is wrong: Metro bundles your JavaScript on your computer.
D is wrong: Expo Router is navigation from files.
Q6
Your code asks for the phone's location. Which parts of the New Architecture are involved?
A) Fabric and the bridge
B) A native module, reached from JavaScript through JSI
C) Hermes alone: JavaScript can read the GPS directly
D) Metro and Expo Go
Why: Native modules (expo-location here) give JavaScript the phone's features, and JSI lets JavaScript call them directly (Lesson 1.1, Exercise 2). Hermes runs the JavaScript that makes the call.
A is wrong: Fabric renders views; the bridge is the old design that the New Architecture replaced.
C is wrong: JavaScript reaches the GPS only through native code.
D is wrong: Those deliver your code to the phone; they are not involved in a location request.

1.1 · How React Native Works: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then run it.
npx tsc --noEmit
(no output: tsc accepts it)
Why: onClick type-checks on a Pressable: nothing tells you it is the wrong prop. Self-check Q3.
P2
Predict, then run it.
npm test -- src/press.test.tsx
● pressing Sign up changes the button
  Unable to find an element with role: button, name: You're in!
...
 FAIL  src/press.test.tsx
  ✕ pressing Sign up changes the button
Tests:       1 failed, 1 total
Why: The press reached the Pressable, and nothing listened: the button still says Sign up. React Native's touch prop is onPress; a test that presses like a finger (or a real finger) is what finds it. Lab 1.1 Task 2.

1.2 · Your Toolchain: self-check

Q7
Expo Go shows Shoreline Crew. You change styles.tagline to styles.tagLine and save. What does the phone show?
A) A red error screen naming index.tsx
B) Nothing changes until you scan the QR code again
C) Metro refuses to bundle the file
D) The tagline, without its size and color
Why: Metro removes the types without checking them, so the file bundles and Fast Refresh shows it: the style is just undefined, and the text silently loses its look. Only npx tsc --noEmit names it (Lesson 1.2, Common Mistakes; Lab 1.2 Task 1).
A is wrong: A red screen comes from code that cannot run (a syntax error, a crash); this runs.
B is wrong: Fast Refresh shows saved changes within a second or two.
C is wrong: Metro transforms TypeScript without type-checking it.
Q8
npx tsc --noEmit prints this in shoreline-crew. Which change fixes it?
src/app/index.tsx:9:27 - error TS2551: Property 'tagLine' does not exist on type 'Readonly<{ … }>'. Did you mean 'tagline'?

9       <Text style={styles.tagLine}>Many hands, cleaner sands.</Text>
                            ~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:9
A) Change styles.tagLine to styles.tagline on line 9
B) Set "strict": false in tsconfig.json
C) Run npx expo start --clear
D) Run npx expo install --fix
Why: Read it from the top: file:line:column (src/app/index.tsx:9:27), the code (TS2551), the message with its Did you mean, then the line with the squiggle. StyleSheet.create is fully typed, so a style name that does not exist is an error.
B is wrong: Switching the checks off hides the bug; the tagline still loses its look.
C is wrong: Metro's cache has nothing to do with a type error.
D is wrong: No package is out of date.
Q9
Phone and laptop are on the same home Wi-Fi, Metro is running, and Expo Go on an iPhone cannot connect. What do you check first?
A) Reinstall Node.js
B) Delete node_modules and run npm install
C) Settings > Privacy & Security > Local Network: Expo Go must be on
D) Press r in the Metro terminal
Why: On iOS, Expo Go needs Local Network access to find your computer (the lesson's checklist). The other common causes: a different network, a firewall, WSL's private 172. address (Lesson 1.2).
A is wrong: Metro is running, so Node works.
B is wrong: The packages are fine: the app bundles in the web preview.
D is wrong: r reloads apps that are already connected.
Q10
Name the two commands the project's AGENTS.md asks for before a task is done, and what each prints when the project is clean.
npx expo lint and npx tsc --noEmit; both print nothing at all
Why: No news is good news: ESLint and tsc speak only when something is wrong. A lint problem ends with a line like ✖ 1 problem (1 error, 0 warnings); a type error starts with file:line:column.
Q11
The very first npx expo lint installs ESLint, says ESLint has been configured, then ends with CommandError: npx expo lint requires ESLint version 8.57.0 or greater. What now?
A) Install a newer ESLint by hand
B) Run npx expo lint again
C) Delete eslint.config.js and start over
D) Run npm audit fix --force
Why: A known first-run hiccup: the CLI checked for ESLint before it had installed it. The second run lints, and prints nothing when the project is clean (Lesson 1.2, Step 7).
A is wrong: ESLint 9 was just installed; it is the version Expo's rules use.
C is wrong: The file it wrote is the one you want.
D is wrong: "force" may install versions that break the SDK's tested set: never.
Q12
On the phone, an app reads http://localhost:4000/api/events. Where does that request go?
A) To your computer, where the API runs
B) To Expo's servers
C) To the Wi-Fi router
D) To the phone itself, where no API runs
Why: On a phone, localhost is the phone. The web preview runs in your computer's browser, so localhost works there; Lesson 5.1 gives the app your computer's address for a real phone.
A is wrong: Only a browser ON the computer reaches the computer as localhost.
B is wrong: Expo's servers never see your API traffic.
C is wrong: The router is not localhost for anyone.

1.2 · Your Toolchain: predict, then check

P3
Predict the status code and the answer, then open it.
/api/sites/seal-rock
HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "not_found",
  "message": "No site with that id."
}
Why: 404 with a JSON body: not_found and a sentence a person can read. Not an empty answer and not a crash: an API says precisely what is wrong, and the app shows it (Module 5).
P4
Predict the status code and the answer, then open it.
/api/events?_status=500
HTTP/1.1 500 Internal Server Error
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "server_error",
  "message": "Simulated 500 response (the _status parameter)."
}
Why: The API's own switch: ?_status=500 makes it answer a server error on purpose, so you can build and test the error screen without breaking anything (Module 5).

Lab Reference Solutions

Every output below is real: the labs run exactly as labs/check_labs.py runs them (Node v24.22.2, in a terminal like a learner's, in a fresh copy of the lab folder; the environment check on a course folder copied from the course downloads, with a private copy of the API). Test durations are left out; everything else is as printed.

Environment check: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.mjs

A tool, not an exercise. Run from a ready course folder (the finished Lesson 1.2 code, lesson-02-end) with the API running, it prints (on a learner's machine the versions differ; without code or git those lines are CHECKs and the verdict READY WITH NOTES):

Environment check for React Native & Expo: Build and Ship a Mobile App
Course folder: shoreline
OK    Node.js v24.22.2 (24 or newer)
OK    npm 11.19.1
OK    Git 2.34.1
OK    VS Code 1.146.0
OK    shoreline-api is in the course folder
OK    shoreline-crew: Expo SDK 58 (expo 58.0.25), React Native 0.88.1, TypeScript 6.0.3
OK    the API answers at http://localhost:4000/api: "Many hands, cleaner sands.", 8 upcoming cleanups
OK    the development server starts (npx expo start) and bundles the app for the web preview
OK    tsc --noEmit: no type errors
OK    npx expo lint: no problems
READY for React Native & Expo: Build and Ship a Mobile App

Three broken machines, as the checker builds them: nothing answering at the address, a course folder with the API but no shoreline-crew, and the ready folder with the lesson's style-name typo in index.tsx. Each ends with NOT READY: fix the FIX lines above, then run this check again and exit code 1:

FIX   nothing answers at http://localhost:4000 (no answer (connection timed out)): start the API in its own terminal: cd shoreline-api, then node server.ts

FIX   no shoreline-crew here: in the course folder run  npx create-expo-app@latest shoreline-crew  (Lesson 1.2, Step 3)

FIX   tsc --noEmit found type errors; the first: src/app/index.tsx(9,27): error TS2551: Property 'tagLine' does not exist on type 'Readonly<{ container: { flex: number; alignItems: "center"; justifyContent: "center"; gap: number; padding: number; }; title: { fontSize: number; fontWeight: "bold"; color: string; }; tagline: { fontSize: number; color: string; }; }>'. Did you mean 'tagline'?

The typo case is the lesson in miniature: tsc fails while the development server still bundles the app (its OK line is in the same run). On the instructor's machine a closed port timed out (connection timed out); on most machines it says ECONNREFUSED. Node 22.18 to 23 gives a CHECK (the API runs on it; the course uses 24), older a FIX; a project that is still the template, a linter that is not set up yet, lint warnings, a missing code or git command or a synced folder give CHECK lines (READY WITH NOTES). The check starts the development server on a spare port for a few seconds and stops it again; it needs no phone.

Diagnostic practice: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/diagnostic_starter.mjs → diagnostic_solution.mjs

As shipped the starter prints JavaScript's answers with the three mistakes in place (the wrong comparison, a second name for the same object instead of a copy, || instead of ??):

D1: [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup' ]
D2: 10 10
D3: unknown

The solution prints (the starter's "Expected when done" comment quotes it):

D1: [ 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean', 'Family Beach Morning' ]
D2: 9 10
D3: 0

Common sticking points: flipping >= to > instead of <; signedUp.spotsTaken++ on the original (D2 prints 10 10: both names point at one object); left || 'unknown' for a full cleanup.

Lab 1.1 (guided): labs/module-01/l1_1-how-react-native-works/starter → solution

As shipped, npm test in starter/ (each failure's first line) and npm run typecheck (it passes: nothing in the starter is a type error):

● Task 1: the site card shows the name as a heading
  Invariant Violation: Text strings must be rendered within a <Text> component. Detected attempt to render "Pelican Point Beach" string within a <View> component.
● Task 1: a beach without step-free access says so
  Invariant Violation: Text strings must be rendered within a <Text> component. Detected attempt to render "Driftwood Cove" string within a <View> component.
● Task 2: a press signs you up, a second press undoes it
  Unable to find an element with role: button, name: You're in!
● Task 3: the label for the spots left, from the real upcoming cleanups
  TODO 3: the label for Tide Pool Morning Cleanup
> shoreline-rn-lab@1.0.0 typecheck
> tsc --noEmit

The solution: npm test; npm run typecheck and npm run lint print nothing after their two > lines:

 PASS  src/lab.test.tsx
  ✓ Task 1: the site card shows the name as a heading
  ✓ Task 1: a beach without step-free access says so
  ✓ Task 2: a press signs you up, a second press undoes it
  ✓ Task 3: the label for the spots left, from the real upcoming cleanups
Tests:       4 passed, 4 total

What each task shows: (1) loose text is a run-time error that no checker sees; the heading role is what <h3> was on the web (screen readers announce it; the test finds it with getByRole('header')); (2) onClick type-checks and does nothing: onPress, and style={({ pressed }) => [...]} for the pressed look (accept a separate pressed && ... variant); (3) spotsLabel is plain TypeScript that would run unchanged in the HQ: watch for left || ... patterns that turn 0 into something else, and for '1 spots left'.

Lab 1.2 (guided): labs/module-01/l1_2-your-toolchain/starter → solution

As shipped: the tests (verdict lines), the type check and the linter:

 FAIL  src/lab.test.tsx
  ✓ Task 1: the banner shows the crew and its tagline
  ✕ Task 1: the tagline keeps its look
  ✓ Task 2: an open desk counts volunteers
  ✓ Task 2: a closed desk says so
Tests:       1 failed, 3 passed, 4 total
src/WelcomeBanner.tsx:10:27 - error TS2551: Property 'tagLine' does not exist on type 'Readonly<{ … }>'. Did you mean 'tagline'?
Found 1 error in src/WelcomeBanner.tsx:10
~/shoreline/labs/module-01/l1_2-your-toolchain/starter/src/SignUpDesk.tsx
  10:29  error  React Hook "useState" is called conditionally. React Hooks must be called in the exact same order in every component render. Did you accidentally call a React Hook after an early return?  react-hooks/rules-of-hooks

✖ 1 problem (1 error, 0 warnings)

The solution: the tests (npm run typecheck and npm run lint print nothing after their two > lines):

 PASS  src/lab.test.tsx
  ✓ Task 1: the banner shows the crew and its tagline
  ✓ Task 1: the tagline keeps its look
  ✓ Task 2: an open desk counts volunteers
  ✓ Task 2: a closed desk says so
Tests:       4 passed, 4 total

Reading it: (1) the style typo runs perfectly and renders every word; only a style test and tsc notice (TS2551 with Did you mean 'tagline'); on a phone the tagline is just plain text; (2) the conditional useState passes every test and fails the linter (react-hooks/rules-of-hooks): hooks first, the early return after them. The tests never change open while the desk is on screen, which is when React's rule matters. Watch for learners who disable the rule with a comment.

Handout: labs/module-01/l1_2-your-toolchain/debug_upcoming.ts (deliberately broken)

As shipped, with the API running, Node stops at once:

triggerUncaughtException(err, true /* fromPromise */);
[TypeError: Failed to parse URL from http://localhost:4000api/events?when=upcoming] {
  [cause]: TypeError: Invalid URL
    code: 'ERR_INVALID_URL',
    input: 'http://localhost:4000api/events?when=upcoming'
  }
}
  1. Bug 1, the address: api + 'api/events?when=upcoming' glues the port to the path (http://localhost:4000api/events?when=upcoming). Fix: ${api}/api/events?when=upcoming . The course writes addresses without a slash at the end and adds /api/... in code.
  2. Bug 2, a missing await: with bug 1 fixed, Node prints 200: undefined upcoming cleanups on the first page and then crashes on events[0].title. response.json() returns a Promise. Fix: const events: Cleanup[] = await response.json().

TypeScript names bug 2 before anything runs: the bug-1-fixed handout, copied into solution/src/ as countUpcoming.ts, then npm run typecheck:

src/countUpcoming.ts:12:9 - error TS2740: Type 'Promise<any>' is missing the following properties from type 'Cleanup[]': length, pop, push, concat, and 35 more.
Found 1 error in src/countUpcoming.ts:12

Fixed, it is solution/src/countUpcoming.ts, which prints (against the course API):

200: 8 upcoming cleanups on the first page
The next one: Tide Pool Morning Cleanup

The lesson in it: the first message is not always the only bug, and Node only finds the second one by running into it; tsc finds it by reading.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 1.1 · How React Native Works
  1. C A native Android view, created by React Native's renderer: React Native renders to the platform's own building blocks. The Fabric renderer creates a native Android view for the View component; there is no DOM on the phone. Only the web preview turns it into a div.
  2. A As JavaScript in Hermes, the engine inside the app: Your TypeScript is turned into JavaScript and runs in Hermes, the JavaScript engine inside the app. Native code (views, modules) runs alongside it, reached through JSI.
  3. B The shoreline-api types in src/api/types.ts: The API types describe the data, not the screen, and the API is the same one. Plain TypeScript with no browser APIs carries over unchanged; Lesson 1.3 copies it.
  4. D A development build of your app: Expo Go contains a fixed set of native modules. A development build is your own version of it, built with exactly the native code your project needs; EAS can build it in the cloud (Lesson 9.2).
  5. B How the camera permission prompt behaves on a phone: The web preview translates components into HTML in a browser. Native-only behavior such as a camera permission prompt, notifications or the platform's own controls needs a phone or an emulator.
Lesson 1.2 · Your Toolchain
  1. B The 172 address is WSL's private address, which the phone cannot reach: By default WSL 2 sits behind its own private address, and Expo prints that address. The phone cannot reach it. Run the commands in PowerShell, switch WSL to mirrored networking and allow the ports, or use the web preview.
  2. D Run npx expo start once, then run tsc again: expo-env.d.ts and the typed routes are generated by the first npx expo start and are not committed. Start Metro once, stop it, and run the type check again.
  3. A n, because files kept in example/ are still checked and their imports break: With y the template moves to an example folder inside the project, where TypeScript and ESLint still check it and its imports no longer resolve. Answering n gives a clean project; you can always create a sample project elsewhere to read the template.
  4. C Fast Refresh shows the new tagline within a second or two, without a reload: Metro sees the saved file, rebuilds that module and Fast Refresh swaps it into the running app, keeping component state. A full reload is only needed when the app gets into a strange state.
  5. A The Android emulator from Android Studio: The Android emulator is a virtual Android phone that runs Expo Go, so it shows native Android views. It runs on Windows, macOS and Linux; the web preview renders HTML instead, and the iOS Simulator needs a Mac.

Opening: Reading the Room

There is no retrieval warm-up in Session 1. The opening's two checks have their answers above: the environment check output (what READY looks like) and the diagnostic D1 to D6. Learners who end the clinic still NOT READY leave with a numbered plan in their workbook's Start Here box.

Exit Ticket

  1. Native views: a native view (an Android view, an iOS UIView) created by React Native's renderer, Fabric; the app is a real app, with no browser inside it, so there is nothing to build a DOM in. (Only the web preview makes HTML.)
  2. The silent typo: npx tsc --noEmit names it (TS2551: StyleSheet.create is typed). Metro removes the types without checking them, and the phone simply ignores a style that is undefined. Running is not checking.
  3. Two terminals: shoreline-api running node server.ts (the API, http://localhost:4000) and shoreline-crew running npx expo start (Metro, port 8081). The API first.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A or D. Still pictures a browser inside the app. Show the demo's outline next to the web preview's HTML.
  • Q2 "tsc catches it". Expects the type checker to know React Native's rules. Run the demo's two commands side by side.
  • Q3 A or D. Assumes web props either fail loudly or work. Run P2: the test presses, nothing happens.
  • Q7 A or C. Expects Metro to check types (the same misconception as Vite's dev server in the web course).
  • Q9 A, B or D. Debugs the computer when the phone's permission is the problem. Walk the lesson's checklist in order.
  • Q12 A. The localhost trap Lesson 5.1 builds on: say it now, solve it then.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 2: From the HQ to the Phone

Answers and reasoning for every Session 2 item: the warm-up, the self-check, the predict-then-check items, the demo outputs, the lab reference outputs, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 2 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Your Toolchain

  1. W1 npx tsc --noEmit and npx expo lint. When clean, tsc prints nothing; from today on, npx expo lint prints only Expo's two env: lines once the project has a .env.local. Metro removes the types without checking them.
  2. W2 To the phone itself, where no API runs. The web preview runs in your computer's browser, so localhost works there (today's project uses it); Lesson 5.1 gives a phone your computer's address.
  3. W3 shoreline-api running node server.ts (the API on :4000) and shoreline-crew running npx expo start (Metro on :8081). The API first.

1.3 · From the HQ to the Phone: self-check

Q1
Which piece of Shoreline Crew HQ is REWRITTEN for the phone, not copied?
A) The type guard isCleanupEvent
B) formatDate with Intl.DateTimeFormat
C) The session kept in sessionStorage
D) The ApiError class
Why: Code that only works with data copies over; code that touches the browser gets rewritten. A phone has no sessionStorage: the session goes to the device's keychain with SecureStore (Lesson 4.3).
A is wrong: A type guard checks unknown data: plain TypeScript, copied.
B is wrong: Intl is part of JavaScript and Hermes has it: copied.
D is wrong: The same API sends the same error bodies: copied.
Q2
This line, copied from a web app, sits in a shoreline-crew file. Does npx tsc --noEmit complain? What happens on a phone?
// The HQ's demo build asked the browser where the site runs:
export const origin = window.location.origin;
export const pageTitle = document.title;
tsc prints nothing (Expo's settings load the DOM types); on a phone it fails while the app runs (the lesson: a TypeError and the red screen)
Why: Expo's tsconfig includes "lib": ["DOM", "ESNext"] for the web preview, so browser APIs type-check. The kit ran it: tsc printed nothing. Search copied code for window, document, Storage and import.meta yourself.
Q3
npx tsc --showConfig in shoreline-crew prints this. Which entry explains why sessionStorage type-checks in phone code?
        "lib": [
            "dom",
            "esnext"
        ],
        "module": "preserve",
A) "dom" in lib
B) "esnext" in lib
C) "module": "preserve"
D) Nothing: it is a bug in TypeScript 6.0
Why: The DOM library is loaded so the same project also type-checks for the web preview, where window and sessionStorage are real. On a phone they are not.
B is wrong: ESNext is the language itself (Promise, Array methods), no browser APIs.
C is wrong: That leaves imports to the bundler (Metro).
D is wrong: It is a deliberate setting, the same in TypeScript 7.
Q4
You copy the HQ's whole client.ts and npx tsc --noEmit reports five errors. Which problem is NOT among them?
A) The missing ../utils/debris.ts
B) import.meta.env.VITE_API_URL
C) The session read and saved with sessionStorage
D) A parameter that implicitly has an any type
Why: TS2307 (debris.ts), two TS7006 that follow from it and two TS2339 (import.meta.env) are all tsc finds. The same file reads, saves and removes the session with sessionStorage and asks window.location for the page's address: tsc accepts every line (the kit's grep found them). Fixing only what tsc reports leaves a client that crashes on a phone.
A is wrong: TS2307, the first error.
B is wrong: TS2339, twice.
D is wrong: TS7006, twice: category has no type once debris.ts is missing.
Q5
The API is not running and the app's request gets no answer at all. What does Expo's fetch on a phone reject with?
A) A TypeError with "Failed to fetch", like a browser
B) A FetchError, a plain Error whose message starts with "fetch failed:"
C) An ApiError with status 503
D) Nothing: the promise never settles
Why: SDK 58 replaces the global fetch on phones with expo/fetch, which throws its own FetchError (node_modules/expo/src/winter/fetch). The HQ's instanceof TypeError branch never matches it, so the phone's request() wraps any failed fetch in a NetworkError (Lab 1.3 Task 2).
A is wrong: That is the browser's (and the web preview's) error.
C is wrong: No server answered, so there is no status at all.
D is wrong: It rejects; the screen then shows describeError's words.
Q6
Why does the phone's request() build the URL BEFORE the try around fetch?
A) So that a missing EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL is not reported as "Could not reach the server"
B) Because fetch accepts only a string
C) TypeScript requires variables outside a try
D) So the request starts faster
Why: apiBaseUrl() throws a plain Error ("EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL is not set") when the variable is missing. Outside the try it stays that error; inside, it would be wrapped as a NetworkError and blamed on the network.
B is wrong: fetch accepts a URL object too; that is not the reason.
C is wrong: No such rule.
D is wrong: It makes no difference to speed.
Q7
const { EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL: url } = process.env; type-checks. npx expo lint prints this. What is the fix?
133:9  error  Unexpected destructuring. Cannot destructure url from process.env  expo/no-env-var-destructuring
A) Rename the variable to VITE_API_URL
B) Add // eslint-disable-next-line above it
C) Move the value into .env.example
D) Write process.env.EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL in full, with a dot
Why: Metro copies the value into the bundle only where it finds the full text process.env.EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL. The kit exported the app both ways: with destructuring the address is not in the bundle at all, and the app says "Something unexpected went wrong". Expo's linter rule catches it (the lesson says it lints cleanly; it does not).
A is wrong: VITE_ is Vite's prefix; Expo reads EXPO_PUBLIC_.
B is wrong: Silencing the rule leaves the address out of the app.
C is wrong: .env.example only documents the variable; .env.local is what Metro reads.
Q8
Which value is fine to keep in an EXPO_PUBLIC_ variable?
A) A paid map service's secret key
B) The shoreline-api's address
C) The database password
D) An organizer's sign-in token
Why: Every EXPO_PUBLIC_ value is copied into the app as plain text: anyone can unpack a downloaded app and read it. An address is public anyway; secrets stay on a server.
A is wrong: Secret keys belong on a server that uses them on the app's behalf.
C is wrong: Never in an app.
D is wrong: A token is per person and changes: SecureStore at run time (Lesson 4.3).
Q9
Pasted from the HQ, export const apiUrl = import.meta.env.VITE_API_URL; gives this. What is the phone's version?
src/vite-habit.ts:2:35 - error TS2339: Property 'env' does not exist on type 'ImportMeta'.
Found 1 error in src/vite-habit.ts:2
A) Add vite/client to the tsconfig's types
B) Put // @ts-ignore above the line
C) Rename the file to .mts
D) process.env.EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, from .env.local
Why: Expo has the same idea with other names: EXPO_PUBLIC_ variables in .env files, read as process.env.EXPO_PUBLIC_…. This one tsc does catch (TS2339), unlike the DOM APIs.
A is wrong: There is no Vite in an Expo project.
B is wrong: The line would still read nothing on a phone.
C is wrong: The file type is not the problem.
Q10
Right after copying the API's types.ts, npx expo lint prints four array-type warnings in it. What is the right move?
A) npx expo lint --fix
B) Add src/api/types.ts to the ignores in eslint.config.js
C) Change each Array<T> to T[] by hand
D) Delete the file and write the types again
Why: types.ts is the API's contract and stays identical to shoreline-api/core/types.ts, so the next copy is a one-line job. The file is not yours to lint: ignore it (Project Step 1).
A is wrong: --fix edits the contract.
C is wrong: The same edit by hand: the copy no longer matches the API.
D is wrong: A second, drifting copy of every type is the mistake the lesson warns about.
Q11
.env.local says http://localhost:4000. The web preview lists the cleanups. What does Expo Go on a phone show today?
A) The same list
B) A red error screen
C) "Could not reach the server. Check that shoreline-api is running, then try again."
D) "Nothing was found at this address. Check the link."
Why: On a phone, localhost is the phone itself: the request gets no answer, request() throws a NetworkError, describeError picks the words. Expected until Lesson 5.1 gives the phone your computer's address.
A is wrong: Only a browser on your computer reaches the API as localhost.
B is wrong: The error is caught and shown as text.
D is wrong: That is a 404 from an API that did answer.
Q12
A teammate tried TypeScript 7, then ran npx expo install typescript (no --dev) to undo it. What did the course measure?
A) TypeScript 6.0 replaced 7.0 in devDependencies
B) npm refused to install an older version
C) Nothing changed: Expo ignores typescript
D) A second typescript entry under dependencies, with 7.0 left in devDependencies
Why: Use npx expo install --dev typescript: it puts the SDK's ~6.0.3 back as a dev dependency. Keep the SDK's version: expo lint's TypeScript support stops at 6.0.
A is wrong: That is what --dev does.
B is wrong: npm installs older versions without complaint.
C is wrong: expo install picks the SDK's version and writes it.

1.3 · From the HQ to the Phone: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then check.
npx tsc --noEmit
src/vite-habit.ts:2:35 - error TS2339: Property 'env' does not exist on type 'ImportMeta'.
Found 1 error in src/vite-habit.ts:2
Why: An error this time: TS2339. Vite's import.meta.env does not exist in an Expo project, and tsc knows it. The phone's version: process.env.EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, from .env.local.
P2
Predict, then check.
http://localhost:4000/api/events/nope
HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "not_found",
  "message": "No cleanup event with that id."
}
Why: 404 with the API's error body. In the app, request() throws an ApiError with status 404, and describeError says "Nothing was found at this address. Check the link." (lesson Exercise 4).
P3
Predict, then check.
http://localhost:4000/api/events?when=upcoming&perPage=3
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
x-total-count: 8
x-total-pages: 3
Why: 8 upcoming cleanups in all, 3 pages of 3. The client's readPage reads these headers into Page.page (lesson Exercise 3 shows the total).

Lab Reference Solutions

Every output below is real: the lab runs exactly as labs/check_labs.py runs it (in a terminal like a learner's, in a fresh copy of the lab folder with the shared install). Test durations are left out.

Lab 1.3 (guided): labs/module-02/l1_3-from-the-hq-to-the-phone/starter → solution

● Task 1: the share message works on a phone (no page address)

  expect(instance).toHaveTextContent()

npm run typecheck prints nothing after its two > lines (the point of Task 1). The search, as TASKS.md gives it:

  1. Task 1, eventText.ts: shareMessage without a web address (Find it in the Shoreline Crew app.); greeting(name: string | null = null) with no storage. spotsText unchanged. Watch for learners who fake window.location or sessionStorage in the test: a phone has neither.
  2. Task 2, client.ts: a NetworkError class; request() builds the URL first, then try { response = await fetch(url) } catch (error) { throw new NetworkError(error) }; describeError checks instanceof NetworkError. Watch for a try around the whole function (it would turn ApiError and the missing-address Error into NetworkErrors) and for error.message.startsWith('fetch failed') (it works on a phone and nowhere else).

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 1.3 · From the HQ to the Phone
  1. C It fails at run time, because a phone has no sessionStorage: Expo's tsconfig loads the DOM types for the web preview, so tsc accepts browser APIs. A phone has no sessionStorage, so the code fails while the app runs. Search copied code for browser APIs yourself; Lesson 4.3 stores the session with SecureStore.
  2. A src/api/types.ts: types.ts is the API's contract, identical to shoreline-api/core/types.ts. It holds only types, so nothing in it depends on the platform, and keeping it identical means a new copy from the API shows every place that has to change.
  3. D The old value was copied into the bundle; restart Metro with --clear: EXPO_PUBLIC_ values are copied into the bundle as text when Metro builds it. Stop Metro and start it with npx expo start --clear so it rebuilds with the new value.
  4. B On a phone, Expo's fetch fails with its own error, not a TypeError: On phones, Expo replaces fetch with its own implementation, which fails with a FetchError rather than the browser's TypeError. Wrapping every failed fetch in one NetworkError gives the same message on Android, iOS and the web preview.
  5. C Keep 6.0: the SDK pins it, and expo lint's TypeScript support stops at 6.0: SDK 58 pins TypeScript 6.0. The code checks the same with 7.0, but npx expo install --check flags it and npx expo lint crashes, because typescript-eslint supports TypeScript only up to 6.0. Keep the SDK's version and let SDK upgrades move it.

Exit Ticket

  1. Copy or rewrite: data code copies (types.ts, the guards, ApiError, describeError, formatDate, getAvailability); browser code is rewritten (the address from import.meta.env and window.location, the session in sessionStorage, components and CSS). Accept any rule that says this in the learner's words.
  2. What tsc accepts: window, document, sessionStorage, localStorage (the DOM types are loaded for the web preview). Find them by searching copied files for window, document, Storage, import.meta.
  3. .env.local changed: Ctrl+C, then npx expo start --clear: the value was copied into the bundle when Metro built it; a reload keeps the old one.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q2 "tsc catches it" / Q4 A, B or D. A clean type check taken as proof: run demo 1's search next to tsc.
  • Q5 A. Expects the browser's error everywhere: the lab's Task 2 test throws the phone's.
  • Q7 B. Silences a linter that is right: export and count the address in the bundle (demo 2).
  • Q8 A or D. "Environment variable" heard as "secret": an EXPO_PUBLIC_ value is plain text in the app.
  • Q10 A or C. Treats the contract as their own file: the next copy from the API would undo it.
  • Q11 A. The localhost trap again: Lesson 5.1 is where it is solved.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 3: View, Text and StyleSheet

Answers and reasoning for every Session 3 item: the warm-up, the self-check, the predict-then-check items, the demo outputs, the lab reference outputs, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 3 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: From the HQ to the Phone

  1. W1 Copied: types.ts, the type guards, ApiError, describeError, formatDate, getAvailability (plain TypeScript, no DOM). Rewritten: the API address (process.env.EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL instead of import.meta.env / window.location) and, later, the session (no sessionStorage). Data code copies; browser code gets rewritten.
  2. W2 No. Expo's tsconfig includes the DOM types for the web preview, so browser APIs type-check: search copied code for window, document and sessionStorage yourself. (On a phone it fails when it runs; Lesson 1.3 hedges the exact wording.)
  3. W3 "Could not reach the server": on a phone, localhost is the phone. The web preview works because it runs on the computer. Lesson 5.1 gives the phone the computer's address.

2.1 · View, Text and StyleSheet: self-check

Q1
Shoreline Crew HQ wrote <p>5 of 12 spots left, <strong>5</strong> …</p>. What is the phone's version of the <strong> inside the <p>?
A) A Text inside a Text, with only fontWeight in its style
B) A View with fontWeight: 'bold' around the number
C) A <b> element: React Native keeps the HTML inline tags
D) A second Text beside the first in a row View
Why: A nested Text is React Native's inline text: it inherits the color, size and line height of the Text around it and changes only what it sets (EventCard's spots number). Lab 2.1 Task 3 builds exactly this.
B is wrong: A View draws no text and passes no text styles on.
C is wrong: There are no HTML elements on a phone.
D is wrong: Two separate Texts lose the shared style, and no single Text holds the sentence (Lab 2.1 Task 3's starter).
Q2
npx tsc --noEmit in shoreline-crew prints this (cut to its first line and the code frame). Which change fixes it?
src/components/AppText.tsx:20:5 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'number'.
20     fontSize: '16px',
       ~~~~~~~~
Found 1 error in src/components/AppText.tsx:20
A) Write fontSize: '16' without the px
B) Set "strict": false in tsconfig.json
C) Write fontSize: 16
D) Move the style into an inline style={{ ... }}
Why: React Native's sizes are plain numbers of density-independent pixels: fontSize is typed number. Read the error from the top: file and line, the type expected (number), the tildes under the property.
A is wrong: Still a string: the same TS2322.
B is wrong: Hiding the check does not make '16px' a size a phone understands.
D is wrong: Inline styles are checked against the same types.
Q3
Predict: the card's style says padding: '16px'. What does npx tsc --noEmit print, what does the web preview show, and what should a phone do?
card: {
  padding: '16px',
  borderRadius: radius.card,
},
tsc prints nothing; the web preview shows 16 pixels of padding; a phone should ignore it (no padding)
Why: React Native 0.88's types allow any string for sizes, so it type-checks; the browser understands px, React Native's native layout code (read for this version) only numbers, 'auto' and percentages. The kit ran tsc: silence. Sizes are numbers, percentages are strings (the lesson's version note).
Q4
<Text style={[styles.line, full && styles.full]}> renders with full set to false. What happens?
A) An error: false is not a style
B) Both styles apply; full wins where they overlap
C) Only styles.full applies
D) Only styles.line applies: React Native skips the false entry
Why: A style array merges left to right, later entries win, and false, null and undefined are skipped. The kit's Jest run of the lesson's Spots printed {"fontSize":16,"color":"#1f2a37"} for an open cleanup.
A is wrong: Skipping falsy entries is what makes conditions this easy.
B is wrong: With full false there is no second style.
C is wrong: The base comes first and always applies.
Q5
AppText draws the banner's title. Which merged style does StyleSheet.flatten([styles.base, styles[variant], style]) give it?
<AppText variant="title" accessibilityRole="header" style={{ color: colors.onBanner }}>
  Shoreline Crew
</AppText>
A) {"color":"#1f2a37","fontSize":32,"lineHeight":40,"fontWeight":"bold"}
B) {"color":"#ffffff","fontSize":32,"lineHeight":40,"fontWeight":"bold"}
C) {"color":"#ffffff"}
D) {"color":"#1f2a37","fontSize":16,"lineHeight":24}
Why: Base (Ink, 16/24), then the title variant (32/40 bold replaces the size and line height), then the screen's style (white replaces Ink). The kit printed this line from Jest with React Native's renderer.
A is wrong: That would be the base's Ink winning: the screen's style comes LAST and wins.
C is wrong: Every layer only says what it changes; the rest comes from the earlier layers.
D is wrong: The title variant replaces the base's size and line height.
Q6
The lesson's Heading sets color: '#0b3954' and fontSize: 20 on the View around <Text>Upcoming cleanups</Text>. What style does the Text itself get?
<View style={styles.box}>
  <Text>Upcoming cleanups</Text>
</View>
A) {}: nothing at all; it shows the default size 14 in the default color
B) Deep Tide at 20, inherited from the View
C) Size 20 only: colors are not inherited
D) A red error screen: color is not allowed on a View
Why: No cascade: a style stays on the component you put it on. The kit's Jest run printed the View's merged style with the color and size, and {} for the Text inside it.
B is wrong: That is the web's cascade; React Native has none (only a Text inside a Text inherits).
C is wrong: Nothing passes from a View to a Text, size included.
D is wrong: It type-checks and runs; it just does nothing.
Q7
AppText's heading variant has fontSize: 20. Which lineHeight matches the lesson's rule?
A) 1.5
B) 20
C) '150%'
D) 28
Why: lineHeight is a size in the same units, about one and a half times the font size: AppText's heading is 20/28, the body 16/24.
A is wrong: 1.5 units: the line squeezes into a sliver (no type error).
B is wrong: Lines touch: no room between them.
C is wrong: A percentage string is not how React Native sizes a line height.
Q8
Exercise 3: you added 'small' to TextVariant and ran npx tsc --noEmit. What does this error mean?
src/components/AppText.tsx:14:37 - error TS7053: Element implicitly has an 'any' type because expression of type 'TextVariant' can't be used to index type 'Readonly<{ base: { color: "#1f2a37"; fontSize: number; lineHeight: number; }; title: { fontSize: number; lineHeight: number; fontWeight: "bold"; }; heading: { color: "#0b3954"; fontSize: number; lineHeight: number; fontWeight: "600"; }; body: {
Found 1 error in src/components/AppText.tsx:14
A) variant needs a default value
B) StyleSheet objects can never be indexed with a variable
C) The type allows 'small', but styles has no small style yet
D) tsconfig.json must turn strict off
Why: styles[variant] is checked: every name in the union must be a key of styles. Add small: { fontSize: 14, lineHeight: 20 } and the error is gone. The type and the styles cannot drift apart (Lab 2.1 Task 2 has the same guard).
A is wrong: It has one ('body').
B is wrong: They can: it works for the five names that have a style.
D is wrong: The check is the point; the fix is the missing style.
Q9
In your shoreline-crew project (after your first npx expo start), you type <View className="card">. What does npx tsc --noEmit print?
A) TS2322: Property 'className' does not exist
B) Nothing: Expo's generated types add className for the web preview, and a phone ignores it
C) TS2551 with Did you mean 'style'?
D) A red error screen, before anything runs
Why: The kit ran it: nothing. expo-env.d.ts (written by the first npx expo start, Lesson 1.2) loads expo/types, which adds className to ViewProps and TextProps for react-native-web. So className is one of the SILENT habits; use style={styles.card}. (Only a project that was never started, without the generated file, prints TS2322.)
A is wrong: Only without expo-env.d.ts, which every learner's project has after Lesson 1.2.
C is wrong: TS2551 is for a misspelled property that exists under a similar name.
D is wrong: tsc checks; it never draws a screen.
Q10
Why can a theme typo such as colors.pgae not slip through the way var(--pgae) did in CSS?
A) The theme is TypeScript code: a property that does not exist is a type error
B) React Native refuses to start with an unknown color
C) StyleSheet.create checks the hex codes
D) Metro replaces unknown colors with black
Why: colors is an as const object exported from src/theme/colors.ts, so tsc knows every key. A misspelled CSS variable just silently fell back.
B is wrong: Nothing at run time checks it: tsc does.
C is wrong: It checks style properties, not your theme's keys.
D is wrong: Metro does not type-check or replace anything.
Q11
The Cleanups screen's ScrollView takes two style props. Which one gets the Sand background, which one the padding and gap, and why?
<ScrollView style={styles.screen} contentContainerStyle={styles.content}>
style = the scrolling window (Sand fills the screen); contentContainerStyle = the content that scrolls (padding and gap travel with the cards)
Why: Padding on the window would stay put while the cards slide under it; on the content strip it scrolls with them (Lesson 2.1, Step 4).
Q12
A volunteer has made all text larger in the phone's settings. Which habit breaks the Cleanups screen for them?
A) Leaving allowFontScaling at its default, true
B) Giving every AppText variant a lineHeight
C) A fixed height on a box that holds text
D) Using the system font
Why: React Native follows the person's text size by default, so a 16 can become 20 or much more; a fixed height clips the text. Let text grow and wrap (Lesson 8.1 tests the extremes).
A is wrong: That is what you want: never turn it off.
B is wrong: A line height in proportion grows with the text.
D is wrong: System fonts follow the setting best of all.

2.1 · View, Text and StyleSheet: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then check.
npx tsc --noEmit
(no output: tsc accepts it)
Why: React Native 0.88's types allow any string for sizes. The web preview shows 16 pixels of padding (the browser understands px); React Native's native layout code, read for this version, treats it as not set, so a phone should show no padding (the lesson's version note). Sizes are numbers.
P2
Predict, then check.
npx tsc --noEmit
(no output: tsc accepts it)
Why: Silence in a project with expo-env.d.ts (every learner's after Lesson 1.2's first npx expo start): Expo's types add className to View and Text for react-native-web. Only a project without that file prints TS2322. A phone ignores className: use style={styles.card}.
P3
Predict, then check.
npm test -- src/styles.test.tsx
Heading, the View: {"padding":16,"color":"#0b3954","fontSize":20}
Heading, the Text inside it: {}
Why: No cascade: the View keeps its color and size, the Text inside it gets nothing and shows the default size 14 in the default color (the lesson measured that in the web preview).

Demo 2 files (for your Jest copy of the lab's solution)

src/colors.ts is the app's src/theme/colors.ts unchanged; src/AppText.tsx is the app's AppText with one import changed to ./colors; src/Heading.tsx and src/Spots.tsx are the lesson's two snippets (No Cascade, Style Arrays). The test:

import { render, screen } from '@testing-library/react-native';
import { StyleSheet } from 'react-native';

import { AppText } from './AppText';
import { colors } from './colors';
import { Heading } from './Heading';
import { Spots } from './Spots';

// One line per component: the style React Native really gives it, merged (StyleSheet.flatten)
const lines: string[] = [];
function show(label: string, style: unknown) {
  lines.push(`${label}: ${JSON.stringify(StyleSheet.flatten(style as never) ?? {})}`);
}

test('what each component really gets', async () => {
  await render(<Heading />);
  show('Heading, the View', (screen.toJSON() as { props: Record<string, unknown> }).props.style);
  show('Heading, the Text inside it', screen.getByText('Upcoming cleanups').props.style);
  await render(<Spots full={false} label="5 of 12 spots left" />);
  show('Spots, open', screen.getByText('5 of 12 spots left').props.style);
  await render(<Spots full label="Full: new sign-ups join the waiting list" />);
  show('Spots, full', screen.getByText('Full: new sign-ups join the waiting list').props.style);
  await render(
    <AppText variant="title" accessibilityRole="header" style={{ color: colors.onBanner }}>
      Shoreline Crew
    </AppText>,
  );
  show('AppText title on the banner', screen.getByText('Shoreline Crew').props.style);
  const fs = require('fs') as { writeFileSync(file: string, text: string): void }; // Node's, inside Jest
  fs.writeFileSync('styles.txt', lines.join('\n') + '\n');
});

Its run and the file it writes:

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 2.1 · View, Text and StyleSheet
  1. B The text in the default color, because a View passes no styles on: Styles stay on the component you put them on, and a View has no text to color. The Text uses the default style. Put the color on the Text, or use AppText with the heading variant.
  2. D White: A style array is merged from left to right and later entries win, so the screen's own style, which comes last, sets the color. That is why AppText puts the style prop at the end.
  3. A Every line is squeezed into a sliver 1.5 units tall: lineHeight is a size in the same units as fontSize, not a multiplier. 1.5 units is far less than one line of 16-unit text, so the lines collapse. tsc cannot know you meant a multiplier. Use 24 for 16-unit text.
  4. C From a Text to a Text nested inside it: A nested Text inherits the text styles of the Text around it and changes only what it sets, which is how EventCard makes just the spots number bold. Everywhere else there is no inheritance, which is why the app has AppText.
  5. B So one file can change what each job looks like, for example a dark palette in Lesson 2.5: A role says what a color is for, so Lesson 2.5 can give each role a dark-mode value in one file and every screen follows. It also keeps the app to one set of colors.

Exit Ticket

  1. Loud and silent: loud: fontSize: '16px' (TS2322), border: '1px solid …' (TS2345); silent: padding: '16px', a color or font size on a View, lineHeight: 1.5, className in a started project. Any two of each.
  2. Inheritance: only from a Text to a Text nested inside it; a View passes nothing on.
  3. AppText's order: later entries in a style array win, so the screen's style, last, can change anything the base or the variant set (the banner's white title over the base's Ink).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B or D. Still thinks in HTML layout: show Lab 2.1 Task 3's starter, where the row of pieces loses the line's style.
  • Q3 "tsc catches it". Expects the types to know what a phone accepts: the lesson's version note, then the demo's silence.
  • Q5 A. Reads the array right to left. Point at AppText's comment: the screen's style comes last and wins.
  • Q6 B. The web's cascade: run the demo's Jest file and read the {}.
  • Q9 A. Habit from older React Native. Run the className step in a started project: nothing; only a project without expo-env.d.ts prints TS2322.
  • Q12 A. Turning font scaling off to protect a layout: the fix is a layout that grows (Lesson 8.1).
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 4: Flexbox the Native Way

Answers and reasoning for every Session 4 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, Checkpoint 1's model answer, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 4 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Lesson 2.1 From Memory

  1. W1: React Native 0.88's types accept the string; the web preview obeys it; a phone should ignore it (Lesson 2.1 read React Native's source and hedges the phone). Write padding: 16.
  2. W2: only a Text inside a Text; a View passes nothing to its Texts (no cascade).
  3. W3: the last entry (style): later wins; false, null and undefined are skipped.

2.2 · Flexbox the Native Way: self-check

Q1
A new View holds a badge (a View with one Text) and nothing else is styled. The badge has a background color. How does it look on the phone?
A) A bar of color as wide as its parent
B) A pill just wider than its word
C) A square as tall as it is wide
D) Nothing at all: a View without a size is not drawn
Why: Every View is a flex column with alignItems: 'stretch', so a child fills the column's width: Lesson 2.2 measured the Scheduled badge at 340 instead of 93 in the web preview. In a row, or with alignSelf: 'flex-start', it keeps the width of its word.
B is wrong: That is the badge inside a row, or with alignSelf: 'flex-start'.
C is wrong: Nothing makes it square: its height still comes from its content.
D is wrong: A View is as tall as its content and, in a column, as wide as its parent.
Q2
You write the CSS shorthand in Screen's style. What does npx tsc --noEmit say, and what do you write instead?
screen: {
  flex: '1 1 0%',
  backgroundColor: colors.page,
},
error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'number'. Write flex: 1 (or flexGrow, flexShrink and flexBasis as three properties)
Why: In React Native flex is ONE number: a share of the free space (Yoga turns a positive flex into flexGrow with a basis of 0). The kit ran it on the stage app: the lesson's card, line for line.
Q3
In the Cleanups screen's header row someone writes alignItems: 'space-between'. Which change fixes what npx tsc --noEmit reports?
src/app/index.tsx:81:5 - error TS2322: Type '"space-between"' is not assignable to type '"center" | "flex-start" | "flex-end" | "stretch" | "baseline" | undefined'.
Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:81
A) Change 'space-between' to "space-between"
B) Add display: 'flex' to the row
C) Move space-between to justifyContent and keep alignItems: 'center'
D) Change flexDirection to 'column'
Why: space-between distributes children along the main axis: that is justifyContent's job. alignItems works across it and accepts only flex-start, flex-end, center, stretch and baseline (the type the message lists). The lesson's quiz Q5 is the same row.
A is wrong: The quotes do not matter: the value is not allowed in alignItems.
B is wrong: Every View is already a flex container; display: 'flex' changes nothing.
D is wrong: A column swaps the axes but alignItems still cannot take space-between.
Q4
EventCard's Detail row: the label has width: 72. Why does the value have flex: 1?
A) To make the value line up with the card's title
B) So it takes the space the label leaves and wraps inside it, instead of running past the card on a phone
C) Because a Text in a row is invisible without a flex value
D) To make the value as tall as the label
Why: Nothing shrinks by default (flexShrink: 0), so a long Text in a row keeps its natural width. flex: 1 gives it exactly the rest of the row and lets it wrap there (the lesson's quiz Q2).
A is wrong: The fixed label width lines the values up, not the value's flex.
C is wrong: The Text is visible either way; the question is where it ends.
D is wrong: In a row, both stretch to the row's height anyway (alignItems: 'stretch').
Q5
Lesson 2.2, Step 5: you delete flex: 1 from detailValue and the web preview barely changes. What should you conclude?
A) flex: 1 was never needed: delete it for good
B) Metro's cache is stale: restart with --clear
C) The value's text is short enough on every phone
D) Nothing yet: the web preview lets a Text shrink like CSS, while a phone's Yoga does not
Why: react-native-web gives Views React Native's defaults but renders a Text as an element that keeps the browser's flex-shrink: 1, so the preview can hide a row that would run off a phone's screen. The lesson measured the preview and hedges the phone ("should run past the card's edge"): give text in a row flex: 1 or flexShrink: 1 anyway, and look at rows on a phone.
A is wrong: It is exactly the bug the preview hides.
B is wrong: Nothing is cached: the preview is just more forgiving.
C is wrong: Meeting points are long, phones are narrow, and people turn up their text size.
Q6
In the header row, "Upcoming cleanups" gets flexShrink: 1, not flex: 1. Why?
A) flexShrink is the only flex property a Text accepts
B) With flex: 1 the heading would take all the free space, leaving space-between nothing to push the count with
C) flex: 1 would make the heading bold
D) flexShrink: 1 centers the heading vertically
Why: flexShrink: 1 keeps the heading's natural width (so space-between has free space to distribute) and lets it shrink and wrap only when both no longer fit, as with a very large text size. flex: 1 means "take the rest".
A is wrong: A Text accepts flex, flexGrow, flexShrink and flexBasis like a View.
C is wrong: flex has nothing to do with the font.
D is wrong: alignItems: 'center' on the row does that.

2.2 · Flexbox the Native Way: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then run it.
npx tsc --noEmit
src/app/index.tsx:81:5 - error TS2322: Type '"space-between"' is not assignable to type '"center" | "flex-start" | "flex-end" | "stretch" | "baseline" | undefined'.

81     alignItems: 'space-between',
       ~~~~~~~~~~

  node_modules/react-native/types_generated/Libraries/StyleSheet/StyleSheetTypes.d.ts:471:3
    471   alignItems?: "flex-start" | "flex-end" | "center" | "stretch" | "baseline" | undefined;
          ~~~~~~~~~~

Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:81
Why: Refused: alignItems accepts only flex-start, flex-end, center, stretch and baseline, and the message lists them. space-between belongs to justifyContent (the main axis). A valid value on the wrong axis, such as justifyContent: 'center' after switching to a row, is never an error: only looking finds it.
P2
Predict, then run it.
npx tsc --noEmit
src/components/Badge.tsx:16:33 - error TS7053: Element implicitly has an 'any' type because expression of type 'BadgeTone' can't be used to index type 'Readonly<{ info: { backgroundColor: "#bfe3df"; }; success: { backgroundColor: "#2d6a4f"; }; neutral: { borderWidth: number; borderColor: "#5b6573"; }; danger: { backgroundColor: "#b23a22"; }; attention: { ...; }; }>'.
src/components/Badge.tsx:17:38 - error TS7053: Element implicitly has an 'any' type because expression of type 'BadgeTone' can't be used to index type 'Readonly<{ info: { color: "#1f2a37"; }; success: { color: "#ffffff"; }; neutral: { color: "#1f2a37"; }; danger: { color: "#ffffff"; }; attention: { color: "#1f2a37"; }; }>'.
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/components/Badge.tsx:16
Why: Noticed twice: boxes[tone] and labels[tone] index tables that have no warning entry (TS7053, with the missing property named). Typed lookup tables make "add a case, forget its style" a type error, the same idea as StatusBadge's Record<EventStatus, …> (self-check Q7).

and the web export (npx expo export --platform web --output-dir dist --clear) ends with Exported: dist.

  • Task 1: flexDirection: 'row', justifyContent: 'space-between', alignItems: 'center' on the header; flexShrink: 1 on the name (with flex: 1 the name would take all the free space and the town would sit against it).
  • Task 2: width: 88 on the label, flex: 1 on the value. The web preview may show no difference without it: a Text shrinks there like CSS; on a phone it should run past the edge (Lesson 2.2's table).
  • Task 3: siteTags from a Record<Site['terrain'], string> plus two conditions (accept .toLowerCase().includes('free') as well as the solution's /\bfree\b/i); the row gets flexDirection: 'row', flexWrap: 'wrap'. Stretch: a pill straight in the card stretches; alignSelf: 'flex-start' or a row fixes it.

labs/module-04/l2_2-flexbox-the-native-way/solution/src/TagRow.tsx
import { StyleSheet, Text, View } from 'react-native';

import type { Site } from './types';

const terrainWords: Record<Site['terrain'], string> = {
  sand: 'Sand',
  rocky: 'Rocky',
  mixed: 'Mixed',
  marsh: 'Marsh',
};

// Plain TypeScript: the same function would work in a web app
export function siteTags(site: Site): string[] {
  const tags = [terrainWords[site.terrain]];
  if (site.accessible) tags.push('Step-free access');
  if (/\bfree\b/i.test(site.parking)) tags.push('Free parking');
  return tags;
}

// Wrapping chips: a row (so the pills keep their own widths), wrapping, with a gap between them
export function TagRow({ tags }: { tags: string[] }) {
  return (
    <View testID="tag-row" style={styles.tags}>
      {tags.map((tag) => (
        <View key={tag} style={styles.tag}>
          <Text style={styles.tagText}>{tag}</Text>
        </View>
      ))}
    </View>
  );
}

const styles = StyleSheet.create({
  tags: {
    flexDirection: 'row',
    flexWrap: 'wrap',
    gap: 8,
  },
  tag: {
    paddingVertical: 2,
    paddingHorizontal: 10,
    borderRadius: 999,
    backgroundColor: '#bfe3df',
  },
  tagText: {
    color: '#1f2a37',
  },
});
