labs/module-04/checkpoint-1/solution/src/site.ts
import type { Site } from './types';

const terrains: readonly string[] = ['sand', 'rocky', 'mixed', 'marsh'];

function isRecord(value: unknown): value is Record<string, unknown> {
  return typeof value === 'object' && value !== null;
}

// A type guard: after `if (isSite(json))`, TypeScript treats the value as a Site
export function isSite(value: unknown): value is Site {
  return (
    isRecord(value) &&
    typeof value.id === 'string' &&
    typeof value.name === 'string' &&
    typeof value.town === 'string' &&
    typeof value.terrain === 'string' &&
    terrains.includes(value.terrain) &&
    typeof value.accessible === 'boolean' &&
    typeof value.parking === 'string'
  );
}
labs/module-04/checkpoint-1/solution/src/BeachCard.tsx
import { useState } from 'react';
import { Pressable, StyleSheet, Text, View } from 'react-native';

import type { Site } from './types';

// The beach card for a phone: native building blocks (1.1), StyleSheet and a style array (2.1), flexbox (2.2)
export function BeachCard({ site }: { site: Site }) {
  const [saved, setSaved] = useState(false);
  return (
    <View style={styles.card}>
      <View testID="beach-header" style={styles.header}>
        <Text accessibilityRole="header" style={styles.name}>
          {site.name}
        </Text>
        <Text>{site.town}</Text>
      </View>
      {site.accessible && (
        <View testID="access-badge" style={styles.badge}>
          <Text>Step-free access</Text>
        </View>
      )}
      <View style={styles.detail}>
        <Text style={styles.label}>Parking</Text>
        <Text testID="parking-value" style={styles.value}>
          {site.parking}
        </Text>
      </View>
      <Pressable
        accessibilityRole="button"
        onPress={() => setSaved(!saved)}
        style={[styles.button, saved && styles.buttonSaved]}>
        <Text style={styles.buttonText}>{saved ? 'Saved' : 'Save this beach'}</Text>
      </Pressable>
    </View>
  );
}

const styles = StyleSheet.create({
  card: { gap: 8, padding: 16, borderRadius: 12, backgroundColor: '#ffffff' },
  header: { flexDirection: 'row', justifyContent: 'space-between', alignItems: 'center', gap: 8 },
  name: { flexShrink: 1, fontSize: 20, fontWeight: '600', color: '#0b3954' },
  badge: { alignSelf: 'flex-start', paddingVertical: 2, paddingHorizontal: 10, borderRadius: 999, backgroundColor: '#bfe3df' },
  detail: { flexDirection: 'row', gap: 8 },
  label: { width: 72, color: '#5b6573' },
  value: { flex: 1 },
  button: { alignSelf: 'flex-start', paddingVertical: 10, paddingHorizontal: 16, borderRadius: 8, backgroundColor: '#1f6f8b' },
  buttonSaved: { backgroundColor: '#2d6a4f' },
  buttonText: { color: '#ffffff', fontWeight: 'bold' },
});
  • The two sentences (model): "npm run typecheck would have caught none of them: loose text in a View and onClick on a Pressable both type-check (only running them shows it). The web preview would have hidden the parking value without flex: 1, because there a Text shrinks like CSS while a phone's does not."
  • Common partial answers: a guard that checks typeof value.terrain === 'string' only (row 4: 1 point); style={saved ? styles.buttonSaved : styles.button} with the base style copied (row 2: 1 point); flex: 1 on the name (the town is no longer pushed to the end: row 3 partly).

Modules 1 and 2 review: self-check

Q7
Someone deletes the Draft line from StatusBadge's table (copied from the HQ in Lesson 2.2). npx tsc --noEmit prints this. What does it protect you from?
src/components/StatusBadge.tsx:5:7 - error TS2741: Property 'draft' is missing in type '{ scheduled: { label: string; tone: "info"; }; completed: { label: string; tone: "success"; }; cancelled: { label: string; tone: "danger"; }; }'
  but required in type 'Record<EventStatus, { label: string; tone: BadgeTone; }>'.
Found 1 error in src/components/StatusBadge.tsx:5
A) A status the API can send that the badge would have no label or tone for
B) A badge that is too wide for its row
C) A Draft event appearing in a volunteer's list
D) An import path written without @/
Why: Record<EventStatus, ...> asks for one entry per status the API's types allow. Plain data like this table carries over from the HQ unchanged (Lesson 1.3's rule), and the type checker makes it complete.
B is wrong: Width is layout; no type knows it.
C is wrong: The table only decides how a status looks.
D is wrong: Imports are fine; the message is about a missing property.
Q8
Checkpoint 1's starter, as shipped: npm test fails with this, while npm run typecheck and npm run lint print nothing. What is wrong in the card?
Invariant Violation: Text strings must be rendered within a <Text> component. Detected attempt to render "Parking" string within a <View> component.
A) The card has no StyleSheet
B) A Pressable cannot hold a Text
C) The fixture's JSON has the wrong shape
D) A string ("Parking") sits straight inside a View, outside a Text
Why: Every piece of text sits in a <Text> (Lesson 1.1). The type checker accepts any child in a View, so only running the code (on a phone: the red error screen; here: Jest) shows it.
A is wrong: It has one; styles never cause this message.
B is wrong: A Text inside a Pressable is exactly how a button gets its label.
C is wrong: The data is fine; the message is about rendering.
Q9
The checkpoint card's button uses onClick={() => setSaved(!saved)}. What do the tools say?
A) tsc reports that onClick does not exist on Pressable
B) The linter reports an unknown prop
C) Nothing: tsc and the linter are silent, and a finger's press does nothing
D) Jest crashes before rendering
Why: React Native's touch prop is onPress. onClick type-checks and lints clean (the kit's runs of the starter: no error TS, no problem), so only a test that presses it, or a finger, finds it (Lesson 1.1).
A is wrong: The kit's typecheck of the starter printed nothing.
B is wrong: The kit's lint of the starter printed nothing.
D is wrong: It renders; the press simply has nobody listening.
Q10
Why does Shoreline Crew check the API's JSON with type guards such as isCleanupEvent(value: unknown): value is CleanupEvent instead of writing as CleanupEvent?
response.json() gives a value nothing has checked; a guard checks it at run time and narrows the type, while as only tells TypeScript to trust it
Why: Lesson 1.3 copied the HQ's guards unchanged (plain TypeScript, no DOM). A server that changes a field fails the guard and becomes an error you can show, instead of a crash three screens later. Checkpoint Part A writes one.
Q11
AppText renders <Text style={[styles.base, styles[variant], style]} />. A caller passes style={{ color: '#b23a22' }} to a muted AppText. Which color does the text get?
A) The muted gray: the variant always wins
B) #b23a22: in a style array, later entries win
C) The base color: the first entry wins
D) A mix of the two
Why: Style arrays apply left to right, later wins, and false/null/undefined entries are skipped (Lesson 2.1). That is why the caller's style goes last.
A is wrong: The variant sits in the middle: anything after it overrides it.
C is wrong: The first entry is the base that the others override.
D is wrong: There is no blending: one value wins.
Q12
A teammate gives the card's View color: '#b23a22' so all its text turns coral. What does the text inside show?
A) Coral, as in CSS
B) Coral, but only on Android
C) A type error from tsc
D) Its own color: on a phone only a Text inside a Text inherits text styles
Why: There is no cascade from a View to its Texts (Lesson 2.1's tide pools: each Text keeps its own water); nested Text inherits from its parent Text. Lesson 2.1 measured it in the web preview; that is why AppText exists.
A is wrong: That is the web's cascade, which React Native does not have.
B is wrong: Both platforms behave the same here.
C is wrong: The lesson notes that tsc accepts some text styles on a View; nothing is reported, nothing is colored.

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 2.2 · Flexbox the Native Way
  1. A Stacked from top to bottom, each as wide as the View: Every View is a flex container whose default flexDirection is column, and the default alignItems is stretch, so the three stack from top to bottom across the full width.
  2. D So it takes the space the label leaves and wraps inside it instead of running past the card: Nothing shrinks by default on a phone (flexShrink is 0), so a long Text in a row could run past the card. flex: 1 makes the value take exactly the space the 72-wide label leaves, and wrap inside it.
  3. B The card is a column and its children stretch across it by default: The card is a column with the default alignItems: stretch, so every child fills its width. In a row, or with alignSelf: flex-start, the badge keeps the width of its word.
  4. C The ScrollView grows to the height of all its rows, so it cannot scroll and the last rows are out of reach: A View without flex: 1 is as tall as its content, so the ScrollView grows to the height of all sixty rows and has nothing to scroll; the last rows stay below the screen. The course measured exactly this in the web preview.
  5. A justifyContent: 'space-between' and alignItems: 'center': In a row the main axis runs across, so justifyContent: space-between pushes the two to opposite ends, and alignItems: center centers them across the row, which is now up and down.

Exit Ticket

  1. Defaults: column (children stack top to bottom), flexShrink 0 (nothing shrinks: long text can run off), alignContent flex-start (wrapped lines stay at the start), flex is one number (a share of the free space; strings are a type error). Any two.
  2. The preview: react-native-web renders a Text that keeps CSS's flex-shrink 1, so it wraps there; on a phone Yoga's flexShrink 0 should keep its width and push it past the card (the lesson's hedge). flex: 1 makes both agree.
  3. Header row: justifyContent: 'space-between' and alignItems: 'center' on a row (the heading flexShrink: 1).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B. Thinks of a badge as inline: the column's stretch is not yet a reflex. Show the badge-bar demo again.
  • Q3 A or D. Treats the type error as syntax, or the axes as fixed. Draw the two arrows.
  • Q5 A. Trusts the web preview as a phone: the habit the lesson's table builds.
  • Q8 or Q9 "tsc". Expects the type checker to know React Native's rules (Session 1's lesson): run the checkpoint starter's three commands side by side.
  • Q12 A. The web's cascade: Lesson 2.1's tide pools.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 5: Images, Icons, Fonts and Safe Areas

Answers and reasoning for every Session 5 item: the warm-up, the self-check, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the demo files, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 5 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Lesson 2.2, Retrieved

  1. A View holds a title and a badge, with no style. Do they sit side by side or one under the other, and why? Answer: One under the other: every View is a flex container whose default flexDirection is 'column' (CSS's is row).
  2. In a row, a long title pushes the badge off the right edge on a phone (the web preview looked fine). Which one style fixes it, and on which element? Answer: flex: 1 (or flexShrink: 1) on the Text: text in a row gets flex: 1 so it wraps; React Native's flexShrink defaults to 0.
  3. Why does Shoreline Crew's Screen give its outermost View flex: 1? Answer: So the screen fills the space the navigator gives it, not just its content's height (the page color reaches the bottom).

2.3 · Images, Icons, Fonts and Safe Areas: self-check

Q1
Exercise 2's logo: <Image source={require('@/assets/images/splash-icon.png')} accessibilityLabel="" /> from expo-image, with no style. The file is 400 by 400. What shows?
A) The logo at 400 by 400, the file's own size
B) Nothing: the picture is 0 by 0
C) A red error screen: a required picture needs a size
D) The logo at 24 by 24, the default icon size
Why: expo-image never sizes itself from the file, even for a require()d one: the course measured 0 by 0 (React Native's own Image would have used the file's 400 by 400). No error, no warning. Every expo-image gets a size: a width and height, or a width from its parent plus an aspectRatio (Lesson 2.3, the size rule).
A is wrong: That is React Native's own Image with a required file; expo-image does not do it.
C is wrong: Nothing complains: that is why it is a silent mistake.
D is wrong: 24 is expo-symbols' default icon size, not a picture size.
Q2
Predict: the icons table gets date: 'calendar', (a plain SF Symbol name). What does npx tsc --noEmit print, and what does an Android phone show next to When?
const icons = {
  date: 'calendar',
  place: { ios: 'mappin.and.ellipse', android: 'location_on', web: 'location_on' },
  meet: { ios: 'person.2', android: 'group', web: 'group' },
} satisfies Record<string, SymbolViewProps['name']>;
tsc prints nothing (a plain SF Symbol name is a valid name); Android and the web preview show no icon at all
Why: name accepts a plain SF Symbol too, so the table type-checks (the kit ran it on the finished app: no output). Only iOS can draw it; on Android and the web there is no name for the platform and no fallback, so nothing. Give all three names (Lab 2.3, Task 2).
Q3
npx tsc --noEmit prints this first (the type is shortened), then a second error at line 26. What do you change?
src/components/Icon.tsx:9:28 - error TS2820: Type '"calender_today"' is not assignable to type '"function" | "draft" | "email" | "phone" | ... 4039 more ...'. Did you mean '"calendar_today"'?

9   date: { ios: 'calendar', android: 'calender_today', web: 'calendar_today' },
                             ~~~~~~~
A) 'calender_today' to 'calendar_today' on line 9, and both errors go
B) Line 26: name={icons[name] as SymbolViewProps['name']}
C) Add 'calender_today' to the Material Symbols with a declaration file
D) Replace the object with date: 'calendar'
Why: Read the end of the first line: Did you mean '"calendar_today"'? The second error is the same mistake seen from where Icon uses the table; fix the first and both go (the kit's run is the lesson's card).
B is wrong: A cast hides the problem; the Android icon would still be missing.
C is wrong: The name really does not exist: Material Symbols has calendar_today.
D is wrong: That silences tsc and loses the icon on Android and the web (Q2).
Q4
Why does Shoreline Crew describe each site picture with accessibilityLabel instead of expo-image's alt?
A) alt is only for local pictures
B) Screen readers on phones ignore alt
C) With alt alone, the web preview's picture had no alt attribute (expo-image 58); accessibilityLabel works everywhere
D) accessibilityLabel makes the picture load faster
Why: expo-image's docs list alt as another name, and on phones it is (the kit's Jest run on iOS turned alt into the label). In the web preview, alt alone gave the loaded <img> no alt at all, which cost the course a failed accessibility check; accessibilityLabel became the alt. One prop that works everywhere.
A is wrong: Both kinds of source take a description.
B is wrong: On phones alt is read; the web preview is the problem.
D is wrong: A description has nothing to do with loading.
Q5
The card's picture gets { aspectRatio: site.image.width / site.image.height } and no width. What gives it its width?
A) expo-image measures the file when it arrives
B) The card: a column stretches its children across
C) aspectRatio sets both width and height
D) Nothing: it needs width: '100%' as well
Why: Lesson 2.2's flexbox: a column stretches its children across (alignItems: 'stretch'), so the picture is as wide as the card, and aspectRatio turns that width into a height before the picture arrives. That is the noticeboard frame: pinned up first, the right shape.
A is wrong: Layout happens before the picture arrives; expo-image never sizes itself.
C is wrong: aspectRatio only relates the two; one of them must come from somewhere.
D is wrong: The stretch already gives the width (the lesson's snippet uses width: '100%' for a picture outside a column).
Q6
_layout.tsx says const ready = fontsLoaded || fontError !== null;. What would happen with const ready = fontsLoaded; on a phone where Outfit fails to load?
A) The headings use the system font
B) The app shows a red error screen
C) useFonts tries again every second
D) The splash screen never goes away
Why: useFonts returns [loaded, error]. If loading fails, loaded stays false; with ready = fontsLoaded the layout keeps returning null and never calls SplashScreen.hideAsync(). "A volunteer with a slightly plainer heading is fine; a volunteer stuck on a splash screen is not."
A is wrong: That is what the lesson's version does: it treats an error as ready.
B is wrong: A failed font is an error value, not a crash.
C is wrong: useFonts does not retry.
Q7
npx tsc --noEmit prints this after a change to typography.ts. What was the change, and what would a phone have shown if fonts were plain strings without satisfies?
src/theme/typography.ts:10:3 - error TS2820: Type '"Outfit_700bold"' is not assignable to type '"Outfit_600SemiBold" | "Outfit_700Bold"'. Did you mean '"Outfit_700Bold"'?

10   title: 'Outfit_700bold',
     ~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/theme/typography.ts:10
title: 'Outfit_700bold' (a lowercase b); without the typed names the titles would silently use the system font
Why: A misspelled fontFamily is not an error anywhere: the web preview drew the browser's default font, iOS only logs "Unrecognized font family". typography.ts's as const satisfies Record<string, keyof typeof fontFiles> turns the typo into TS2820 with a Did you mean (the kit's run is the lesson's card).
Q8
A heading style says fontFamily: 'Outfit_600SemiBold', fontWeight: 'bold'. It looks bold in the web preview. On a phone?
A) Maybe bold, maybe semibold, maybe the system font: do not depend on it
B) Always bold: React Native fakes a bold weight
C) A tsc error: fontWeight is not allowed with a fontFamily
D) The phone downloads Outfit's bold weight
Why: Each weight of a loaded font is its own family. A browser fakes a bold version; React Native's fontFamily documentation does not promise that on phones. Pick the weight by its family name (Outfit_700Bold) and add the weight to fontFiles.
B is wrong: That is the browser's behavior, not a promise on phones.
C is wrong: Both props type-check.
D is wrong: Only the weights you import ship in the app; nothing downloads.
Q9
The lesson's Screen, run headless with a notched phone's insets (47 at the top, 34 at the bottom). Which line is a detail screen under a header bar (Lesson 4.1)?
notch, all four edges (Cleanups)     frame paddingTop 47 · content paddingBottom 50
notch, edges={['bottom','left','right']} frame paddingTop 0 · content paddingBottom 50
web preview (every inset is 0)       frame paddingTop 0 · content paddingBottom 16
A) The first: every screen should add every inset
B) The third: a header removes every inset
C) None: a header bar needs SafeAreaView from react-native
D) The second: the header already pads the top, so the frame adds 0 there
Why: A header bar already sits below the status bar and pads itself; adding insets.top again gives an empty band. So a screen under a header leaves 'top' out of its edges, and keeps the bottom inset inside the scrolling content (the lesson's quiz Q4).
A is wrong: Under a header that doubles the top inset (Common Mistakes).
B is wrong: The third line is the web preview, where every inset is 0.
C is wrong: React Native's SafeAreaView is deprecated and never handled Android.
Q10
A detail screen ends with a bar pinned to the bottom (it does not scroll) holding a Sign up button. Where does the bottom inset go?
A) Nowhere: the home indicator is transparent
B) On the ScrollView's contentContainerStyle above the bar
C) On the bar's own paddingBottom, so its background reaches the glass and the button stays above the indicator
D) On the Sign up button's marginBottom
Why: Only scrolling content carries the bottom inset inside its contentContainerStyle. A bar that does not scroll pads itself: paddingBottom = its own padding + insets.bottom (Lesson 2.3, Scrolling screens; Lab 2.3 Task 3).
A is wrong: The indicator is drawn on top of your app: a button under it is hard to press.
B is wrong: The bar is below the scroll view; the inset belongs to whatever touches the bottom edge.
D is wrong: A margin leaves a band of page color under the bar instead of the bar's background.
Q11
An old tutorial's screen starts with this import. What does React Native 0.88 print the first time it renders, and what do you change?
import { SafeAreaView, Text } from 'react-native';

Why: The kit rendered it once in Jest: React Native printed that warning (with a link to the library). React Native's own version only ever worked on iOS. The library's SafeAreaView takes edges; the hook gives the numbers.
Q12

A is wrong: That needs your own build.

Demo files (Live demo 2, "What Screen does with a notch")

In a copy of labs/module-05/l2_3-images-icons-fonts-and-safe-areas/solution/: the stage's Screen.tsx, theme/colors.ts and theme/spacing.ts copied into src/ (Screen's two @/theme/ imports changed to ./theme/), and this test as src/insets.test.tsx. It renders Screen inside a SafeAreaProvider with a notched phone's insets and writes what Screen hands the native scroll view:

import { render, screen } from '@testing-library/react-native';
import { Image } from 'expo-image';
import { StyleSheet, Text } from 'react-native';
import { SafeAreaProvider } from 'react-native-safe-area-context';

import sites from '../fixtures/sites.json';
import { Screen } from './Screen';
import { SitePicture } from './SitePicture';
import type { Edge } from 'react-native-safe-area-context';
import type { Site } from './types';

type Host = { type: string; props: Record<string, unknown>; children: Host[] | null };
const lines: string[] = [];
const phone = (top: number, bottom: number) => ({
  frame: { x: 0, y: 0, width: 390, height: 844 },
  insets: { top, right: 0, bottom, left: 0 },
});

// What Screen hands the native scroll view: the frame's style and the content's style, flattened
async function screenWith(name: string, top: number, bottom: number, edges?: Edge[]) {
  await render(
    <SafeAreaProvider initialMetrics={phone(top, bottom)}>
      <Screen scroll edges={edges}>
        <Text>Upcoming cleanups</Text>
      </Screen>
    </SafeAreaProvider>,
  );
  const scroll = (screen.toJSON() as Host).children![0];
  const frame = StyleSheet.flatten(scroll.props.style as object) as { paddingTop: number };
  const content = StyleSheet.flatten(scroll.props.contentContainerStyle as object) as { paddingBottom: number };
  lines.push(`${name.padEnd(36)} frame paddingTop ${frame.paddingTop} · content paddingBottom ${content.paddingBottom}`);
}

test('Screen and the safe area, headless', async () => {
  await screenWith('notch, all four edges (Cleanups)', 47, 34);
  await screenWith("notch, edges={['bottom','left','right']}", 47, 34, ['bottom', 'left', 'right']);
  await screenWith('web preview (every inset is 0)', 0, 0);
  await render(<Image source={(sites as Site[])[0].image.url} />);
  lines.push(`${'expo-image with no style'.padEnd(36)} style ${JSON.stringify((screen.toJSON() as Host).props.style)}`);
  await render(<SitePicture site={(sites as Site[])[0]} />);
  const style = StyleSheet.flatten((screen.toJSON() as Host).props.style as object) as { aspectRatio: number };
  lines.push(`${'the lab card picture'.padEnd(36)} aspectRatio ${style.aspectRatio.toFixed(3)} (1200 / 675)`);
  const fs = require('fs') as { writeFileSync(file: string, text: string): void }; // Node's, inside Jest
  fs.writeFileSync('insets.txt', lines.join('\n') + '\n');
});

npm test -- src/insets.test.tsx passes, and cat insets.txt prints (the last two lines are the size rule: an expo-image with no style has an empty style, the lab's card picture an aspectRatio):

notch, all four edges (Cleanups)     frame paddingTop 47 · content paddingBottom 50
notch, edges={['bottom','left','right']} frame paddingTop 0 · content paddingBottom 50
web preview (every inset is 0)       frame paddingTop 0 · content paddingBottom 16
expo-image with no style             style {}
the lab card picture                 aspectRatio 1.778 (1200 / 675)

npx tsc --noEmit in the same folder prints nothing (the copies type-check). The kit's runs on the finished app, for the record: npx tsc --noEmit prints nothing, and npx expo lint prints only the two env: lines of the .env.local from Lesson 1.3:

env: load .env.local
env: export EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 2.3 · Images, Icons, Fonts and Safe Areas
  1. D Nothing: the picture is as wide as the card but 0 tall: A picture from a server has no size when the screen is laid out, and expo-image never sizes itself from the file, so the image stretches across the card but is 0 tall. Give it a width and height, or let the parent set the width and add aspectRatio.
  2. B The iPhone shows a calendar; Android and the web preview show no icon at all: A plain SF Symbol name only works on iOS. On Android and the web, expo-symbols finds no name for the platform and draws the fallback, which is nothing. Give the object with ios, android and web names.
  3. A So the first screen appears only once Outfit is ready, instead of jumping from the system font: Until useFonts has registered Outfit, headings would draw in the system font and then jump. Keeping the splash screen up until the fonts are loaded (or have failed) means the first screen appears in its final font.
  4. C ['bottom', 'left', 'right']: The header bar covers the top edge and pads itself below the status bar, so the screen adds only the edges nothing else covers. Adding the top inset again would leave an empty band under the header.
  5. B The installed app is Expo Go; your icon only appears in your own build: The app installed on the phone is Expo Go, so the home screen shows Expo Go's icon. Your own icon and native splash screen are built into your own app: a development build in Module 9.

Exit Ticket

  1. Three silent mistakes: an expo-image with no size (0 tall); a plain SF Symbol name (no icon on Android or the web); a misspelled fontFamily (the system font). Each made loud: the size rule, the three-name table, typed family names.
  2. Typed family names: a misspelled family becomes TS2820 with a Did you mean, instead of a silently wrong font on every platform.
  3. Under a header: ['bottom', 'left', 'right']: the header pads itself below the status bar; adding the top inset again leaves an empty band.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Expects expo-image to behave like React Native's Image (or a browser). Show the lesson's measured table.
  • Q2 "tsc catches it". Expects a type to promise a platform; a plain SF name is a valid name. Run P1.
  • Q5 A or D. Has not connected flexbox's stretch with the picture's width: back to Lesson 2.2's column default.
  • Q6 A. Reads the lesson's version instead of the question's: point at the || fontError !== null.
  • Q9 A. "Every inset everywhere, to be safe" is the double-inset bug of Lesson 4.1. Draw the header over the status bar.
  • Q12 B. Expects Expo Go to become your app; it is the installed app. Module 9 makes your own.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 6: Touch and Input

Answers and reasoning for every Session 6 item: the warm-up, the self-check, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the demo files, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 6 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Lesson 2.3, Retrieved

  1. An expo-image from the API has source and contentFit but no size. What do volunteers see, and what fixes it? Answer: Nothing (0 tall, no error); a width from the parent plus an aspectRatio from the API's image.width / image.height.
  2. Icon's table has date: 'calendar'. tsc prints nothing. What is wrong? Answer: A plain SF Symbol name draws only on iOS: Android and the web preview show no icon. Give ios, android and web names.
  3. A detail screen sits under a header bar. Which edges does its Screen touch? Answer: ['bottom', 'left', 'right']: the header already pads itself below the status bar.

2.4 · Touch and Input: self-check

Q1
The lesson's PressLog: a volunteer holds the Try me button for a full second, then lifts. What does the log show?
A) pressIn → press → longPress
B) pressIn → pressOut → press
C) pressIn → longPress → pressOut
D) longPress only
Why: Holding gives pressIn, then longPress at 500 ms, then pressOut when the finger lifts, and no press: when you handle a long press, the tap does not count as well. The kit ran the lesson's PressLog in Jest with a real hold: long press: pressIn → longPress → pressOut (the lesson measured the same in the web preview).
A is wrong: There is no press after a handled long press.
B is wrong: That is a quick tap.
D is wrong: pressIn always comes first: it starts the pressed look.
Q2
Predict: the search field's clear button gets onClick={() => onChangeText('')} instead of onPress. What do npx tsc --noEmit and npx expo lint print, and what happens when a finger taps it?
<Pressable
  onClick={() => onChangeText('')}
  accessibilityRole="button"
  accessibilityLabel="Clear search"
  hitSlop={8}
>
tsc prints nothing; lint prints only its two env: lines (no problem); the tap does nothing
Why: React Native 0.88's types accept onClick on any view (keyboards and other pointing devices), so nothing complains. Pressable puts its own click handler on top of yours: taps go to onPress. The kit ran both checks on the finished app with this change.
Q3
npx tsc --noEmit prints this after a change to SearchField.tsx. What is the fix?
src/components/SearchField.tsx:22:43 - error TS18048: 'event.target' is possibly 'undefined'.

22         onChange={(event) => onChangeText(event.target.value)}
                                             ~~~~~~~~~~~~

src/components/SearchField.tsx:22:56 - error TS2551: Property 'value' does not exist on type 'number | ReactNativeElement'. Did you mean 'valueOf'?
  Property 'value' does not exist on type 'number'.

22         onChange={(event) => onChangeText(event.target.value)}
                                                          ~~~~~


Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/components/SearchField.tsx:22
A) onChange={(event) => onChangeText(event.target?.value ?? '')}
B) onChangeText={onChangeText}
C) onChange={(event) => onChangeText(String(event.target))}
D) Add // @ts-expect-error above the line
Why: In React Native an event's target is a reference to a native view, not an element with a value. onChangeText hands you the new text as a string, so the setter goes straight in (the kit's run is the lesson's card).
A is wrong: There is no value to read: the field would always be ''.
C is wrong: That is the view's number or object, not the text.
D is wrong: Silencing the checker leaves a field that never updates.
Q4
Shoreline Crew's Button has minHeight: 48. Why not height: 48?
A) height is not allowed on a Pressable
B) minHeight is faster to lay out
C) With larger text the button can still grow instead of cutting its words
D) Android ignores height
Why: 48 is the finger-sized minimum (Apple asks 44 pt, Android 48 dp; the course uses 48 for both). minHeight keeps that minimum and still lets the button grow when a volunteer has made their text larger (Lesson 2.1).
A is wrong: It is allowed; it just stops growing.
B is wrong: Layout speed is not the reason.
D is wrong: Both platforms honor height.
Q5
The Show details button tells screen readers whether the details are open. Which prop does Shoreline Crew use, and why?
A) aria-expanded: the web preview passed it on, and React Native reads it first on phones
B) accessibilityState={{ expanded }}: the only one phones understand
C) accessibilityHint: hints carry state
D) None: the words Show and Hide are enough
Why: React Native accepts both names; its Pressable reads aria-expanded first. In the web preview only aria-expanded arrived (react-native-web ignored accessibilityState), so the course writes state with aria-* names and keeps accessibilityRole and accessibilityLabel.
B is wrong: Phones read both; the web preview only aria-expanded.
C is wrong: A hint describes what happens, not a state.
D is wrong: Words change, but a screen reader announces state from the state prop.
Q6
Which keyboardShouldPersistTaps value lets a tap on the clear button reach the button while the keyboard is open, and still closes the keyboard on a tap on empty space?
A) 'never' (the default)
B) 'always'
C) Leave it out and add hitSlop
D) 'handled'
Why: With 'never' the first tap outside the field only closes the keyboard, and "children will not receive the tap" (React Native's description). 'handled' gives a tap that a button handles to the button; empty space still closes the keyboard. Screen sets it once for every scrolling screen. Only a phone shows this (the web preview has no on-screen keyboard).
A is wrong: That swallows the first tap: volunteers tap twice and think the app is slow.
B is wrong: Then a tap on empty space no longer closes the keyboard.
C is wrong: hitSlop makes the target bigger; the tap is still swallowed.
Q7
npx tsc --noEmit prints this for SwitchRow.tsx (first lines). Which words tell the whole story, and what is the fix?
src/components/SwitchRow.tsx:20:9 - error TS2322: Type '(value: boolean) => void' is not assignable to type '(event: Readonly<{ bubbles: boolean | undefined; cancelable: boolean | undefined; currentTarget: number | ReactNativeElement; defaultPrevented: boolean | undefined; ... 11 more ...; type: string | undefined; }>) => void | Promise<...>'.
  Types of parameters 'value' and 'event' are incompatible.
"Types of parameters 'value' and 'event' are incompatible": onChange gets an event, not the new value; use onValueChange={onValueChange}
Why: Switch also has onChange, and it hands you an event. onValueChange hands you the new boolean, like a checkbox's checked in React (the kit's run matches the lesson's shortened card).
Q8
After an editor auto-import, index.tsx prints this. Which word in the type shows WHICH Button TypeScript is looking at?
src/app/index.tsx:90:19 - error TS2322: Type '{ label: string; variant: string; onPress: () => void; }' is not assignable to type 'IntrinsicAttributes & Omit<ButtonProps, "ref"> & { ref?: Ref<ReactNativeElement | TouchableNativeFeedback> | undefined; }'.
  Property 'label' does not exist on type 'IntrinsicAttributes & Omit<ButtonProps, "ref"> & { ref?: Ref<ReactNativeElement | TouchableNativeFeedback> | undefined; }'.

90           <Button label="Show all cleanups" variant="primary" onPress={showAll} />
                     ~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:90
A) IntrinsicAttributes
B) TouchableNativeFeedback: React Native's own Button, which takes a title
C) label: Shoreline Crew's Button
D) Omit: a Button with props removed
Why: React Native's core Button is built from the old touchables and takes a title; the type after ref? names TouchableNativeFeedback. Fix the import: import { Button } from '@/components/Button'; (Exercise 3; the kit's run is the lesson's card).
A is wrong: Every JSX element's props type has it.
C is wrong: label is the prop it rejects.
D is wrong: Omit is how React Native's Button type is written; it does not say which Button.
Q9
A field has inputMode="numeric". A volunteer types 12. What does onChangeText hand you?
A) The number 12
B) The string '12'
C) NaN until the field loses focus
D) An event with a value property
Why: The number keyboard only changes the keys: the value is still a string. Turn it into a number yourself (Lab 2.4's parseBags) or with Zod (Lesson 5.4).
A is wrong: No input type converts it.
C is wrong: Nothing is converted.
D is wrong: That is onChange, and it has no value either.
Q10
matchesSearch(event, site, '') (an empty search box). What does it return, and why?
A) true: an empty query has no words, and every() on an empty list is true
B) false: there is nothing to match
C) It throws: split of an empty string
D) true only for the first cleanup
Why: ''.split(/\s+/).filter(Boolean) is [], and [].every(...) is true: an empty search shows every cleanup, which is what a volunteer expects.
B is wrong: every on an empty list is true, not false.
C is wrong: Splitting an empty string is fine.
D is wrong: The same rule runs for every cleanup.
Q11
Every card's button has accessibilityLabel={${showDetails ? 'Hide' : 'Show'} details for ${event.title}}. Give two reasons for this label.
eight buttons all called "Show details" are indistinguishable to a screen reader; starting with the visible words lets voice control ("tap Show details") still find it
Why: On a phone the label replaces what is read, so it names the cleanup (the HQ used hidden text for the same job), and it begins with the words on the screen.
Q12
The clear button is 32 by 32 with hitSlop={8}. In the web preview you click 4 pixels outside its circle. What happens, and on a phone?
A) It clears in both
B) Nothing in the web preview (it ignores hitSlop); on a phone the finger's 48 by 48 area clears it
C) It clears in the web preview only
D) A red screen: hitSlop needs a number per side
Why: The course measured it: react-native-web ignores hitSlop (a click 10 px outside a hitSlop of 20 missed). On a phone React Native's Pressable uses it. A mouse is precise; fingers are the problem hitSlop solves.
A is wrong: The web preview ignores hitSlop.
C is wrong: It is the other way round.
D is wrong: A single number means the same on every side.

In a copy of labs/module-06/l2_4-touch-and-input/solution/: the lesson's PressLog as src/PressLog.tsx (copied from the lesson page) and this test as src/presslog.test.tsx. npm test -- src/presslog.test.tsx passes and writes presslog.txt:

import { render, screen, userEvent } from '@testing-library/react-native';

import { PressLog } from './PressLog';

// The lesson's PressLog, pressed the way a finger presses it: a tap, then a hold (800 ms; a long press needs 500)
test('which callbacks run, and in which order', async () => {
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  const lines: string[] = [];
  await render(<PressLog />);
  await user.press(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Try me' }));
  lines.push('tap:        ' + screen.getByText(/pressIn/).props.children);
  await render(<PressLog />);
  await user.longPress(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Try me' }), { duration: 800 });
  lines.push('long press: ' + screen.getByText(/pressIn/).props.children);
  const fs = require('fs') as { writeFileSync(file: string, text: string): void }; // Node's, inside Jest
  fs.writeFileSync('presslog.txt', lines.join('\n') + '\n');
}, 20000);
tap:        pressIn → pressOut → press
long press: pressIn → longPress → pressOut

npx tsc --noEmit in that folder prints nothing. On the finished app, npx tsc --noEmit prints nothing and npx expo lint prints only the .env.local lines:

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 2.4 · Touch and Input
  1. B The button looks pressed, then not; the details stay closed: onPressIn and onPressOut run (the button shows and then drops its pressed look), but onPress only runs when the finger lifts while still on the control. Sliding off is how people cancel a tap.
  2. D tsc reports errors; the fix is onChangeText={setQuery}: tsc reports that event.target may be undefined and that value does not exist on it: a native event's target is a reference to a view, not an element with a value. onChangeText={setQuery} hands you the text directly.
  3. A The keyboard closes and the text stays; a second tap clears it: With the default, never, a tap outside the field while the keyboard is open only closes the keyboard, and the button never receives it. keyboardShouldPersistTaps handled lets the button handle the tap.
  4. C accessibilityLabel="Clear search" on the Pressable: An icon-only control has no words of its own, so the Pressable carries them in accessibilityLabel (with accessibilityRole button). In the web preview it became aria-label.
  5. A hitSlop={8}: hitSlop extends the area that reacts to touches beyond the drawn box: 32 plus 8 on each side is 48. On a phone, that is; the web preview ignores hitSlop, which is fine for a precise mouse pointer.

Exit Ticket

  1. Web habits: onClick → onPress; onChange + event.target.value → onChangeText; a Switch's onChange → onValueChange; a 36-high target → minHeight 48 (and hitSlop); nothing → keyboardShouldPersistTaps="handled". Any two.
  2. A hold: pressIn, onLongPress (when there is one), pressOut; onPress does not run.
  3. "handled": a tap that a button handles reaches the button while the keyboard is open; a tap on empty space still closes the keyboard.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A or B. Thinks a long press is a slow tap. Run the PressLog demo again.
  • Q2 "tsc catches it". Trusts types to know the platform. Show P1.
  • Q3 A. Patches the web habit instead of dropping it. Ask what target is.
  • Q6 A or B. Has not met the swallowed tap; it only shows on a phone: pair them with a phone owner.
  • Q9 A. Expects the keyboard to convert values: back to Zod in 5.4.
  • Q12 A or C. Treats the web preview as a phone (Lesson 1.1's warning).
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 7: Responsive, Platform-Specific and Themed UI

Answers and reasoning for every Session 7 item: the warm-up, the self-check, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the demo files, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 7 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Lesson 2.4, Retrieved

  1. A Pressable has onClick={signUp}. What do tsc and lint say, and what does a tap do? Answer: Nothing and nothing: onClick type-checks and lints clean; a tap does nothing. Taps go to onPress.
  2. The HQ's onChange={(e) => setQuery(e.target.value)} on a TextInput: what replaces it? Answer: onChangeText={setQuery}: React Native hands you the new text as a string.
  3. On a phone, a volunteer types in the search field and taps the clear button. What makes the first tap reach the button? Answer: keyboardShouldPersistTaps="handled" on the scroll view (Screen sets it once).

2.5 · Responsive, Platform-Specific and Themed UI: self-check

Q1
The kit ran the lesson's Dimensions.get snippet in Jest, where every test first sets a 390-wide phone. It printed this. Why 2?
columns from Dimensions.get:   2 (read when the file loaded)
A) The file was loaded (and read the width) before the test set the phone's size
B) Jest has no window, so it guesses
C) Dimensions.get rounds 390 up to 700
D) Two columns is the default
Why: A top-level read runs once, when the file is first imported: under Jest that was the default window, before the test's 390. On a phone it is the size at launch, and it never follows a tablet turning or a split screen widening. Inside components, use useWindowDimensions (the lesson's quiz Q1).
B is wrong: Jest has a window: the same run printed 390 by 844 from the hook.
C is wrong: Nothing rounds.
D is wrong: The snippet computes it from the width it read.
Q2
An iPad runs Shoreline Crew in split screen, in a third of the screen. What does useWindowDimensions().width report?
A) The iPad's full screen width
B) The width of the app's window: a third of the screen
C) 0 until the app is full screen
D) The width the app had when it launched
Why: It measures the window your app draws in, not the device, and re-renders when it changes. In split screen the window is what the layout has to fit (Dimensions.get('screen') gives the whole screen; rarely what you want).
A is wrong: That is the screen, not the window.
C is wrong: The window always has a size.
D is wrong: That is the stale top-level read.
Q3
The kit rendered the lesson's PlatformNote in Jest and got this line. What does it tell you about the web preview and the phones?
PlatformNote:                  ios: Swipe from the left edge to go back
A) Every platform says the same thing
B) Jest is broken: the web preview should be the default
C) This Jest run renders as iOS; the web preview shows the default branch, an Android phone the android one
D) Platform.select only works on iOS
Why: jest-expo's preset renders as iOS, so Platform.OS is 'ios' there. The lesson ran the snippet in the web preview and got the default hint and "0.0.0" as the version. Each phone runs its own branch: the only way to see both is to test on both (or ask a friend with the other kind).
A is wrong: Platform.select picks one value per platform.
B is wrong: Each environment runs its own branch.
D is wrong: It picks ios, android, web or default.
Q4
Why does the lesson's snippet check Platform.OS === 'android' before comparing Platform.Version >= 33?
const newAndroid = Platform.OS === 'android' && Platform.Version >= 33;
Platform.Version is a number (the API level) only on Android; on iOS it is a string (and "0.0.0" on the web), so TypeScript narrows it to a number only after the OS check
Why: React Native's types narrow Version by OS. Comparing first would compare a string on iOS.
Q5
Shoreline Crew has src/hooks/useColorScheme.ts and src/hooks/useColorScheme.web.ts. Code imports @/hooks/useColorScheme. Which file does npx tsc --noEmit check against?
A) The .web.ts file
B) Both, merged
C) Neither: platform files are not type-checked
D) The plain .ts file: keep the .web.ts version exporting the same names with the same types
Why: Metro picks the file for the platform it bundles; TypeScript checks the plain file. A .web.ts that drifts can break one platform without tsc noticing, so platform files stay tiny and same-typed.
A is wrong: TypeScript resolves the plain name.
B is wrong: There is no merging.
C is wrong: The plain file is checked; the variant is checked only on its own.
Q6
Exercise 2: trackOff is deleted from darkColors. npx tsc --noEmit prints this first (shortened). What does satisfies ThemeColors buy you?
src/theme/colors.ts:74:3 - error TS1360: Type '{ page: "#0f1b26"; surface: "#16283a"; border: "#2b4257"; text: "#e9eef3"; textMuted: "#a9b4c0"; heading: "#bfe3df"; error: "#ff9b85"; banner: "#0b3954"; onBanner: "#ffffff"; onBannerAccent: "#bfe3df"; ... 7 more ...; knob: "#ffffff"; }' does not satisfy the expected type 'ThemeColors'.
  Property 'trackOff' is missing in type '{ page: "#0f1b26"; surface: "#16283a"; border: "#2b4257"; text: "#e9eef3"; textMuted: "#a9b4c0"; heading: "#bfe3df"; error: "#ff9b85"; banner: "#0b3954"; onBanner: "#ffffff"; onBannerAccent: "#bfe3df"; ... 7 more ...; knob: "#ffffff"; }' but required in type 'ThemeColors'.
A) Nothing: theme.ts would report the same
B) The error points at the palette itself, the cause, with "Property 'trackOff' is missing"
C) It makes darkColors a ThemeColors at run time
D) It fills the missing role with the light value
Why: Without satisfies you only get theme.ts's error, further from the cause. With it, tsc stops you where the palette is written (the kit's run matches the lesson's card: 2 errors in 2 files).
A is wrong: That is the second error, further away.
C is wrong: satisfies is a check only; it changes nothing at run time.
D is wrong: Nothing is filled in: it is an error.
Q7
npx expo lint prints this for Screen.tsx after const { colors } = useTheme(); was put at the top of the file. Even if it ran, why would it be wrong?
52:20  error  React Hook "useTheme" cannot be called at the top level. React Hooks must be called in a React function component or a custom React Hook function  react-hooks/rules-of-hooks
it would run once, when the file loads: the same baked-in (daytime) colors; use createThemedStyles outside the component and call useStyles() inside
Why: Hooks only work inside components and other hooks (react-hooks/rules-of-hooks). And top-level code runs once: the phone can switch to dark mode and nothing follows. The kit's run matches the lesson's card (except the path, which is yours).
Q8
SwitchRow.tsx: npx tsc --noEmit prints four of these. What happened?
src/components/SwitchRow.tsx:25:37 - error TS2339: Property 'trackOff' does not exist on type '{ primary: ____ColorValue_Internal; background: ____ColorValue_Internal; card: ____ColorValue_Internal; text: ____ColorValue_Internal; border: ____ColorValue_Internal; notification: ____ColorValue_Internal; }'.
A) The editor imported expo-router's useTheme: the navigator's six colors; import it from @/hooks/useTheme
B) darkColors is missing trackOff
C) SwitchRow needs a ThemeProvider
D) ColorValue cannot hold a string
Why: The type lists primary, background, card, text, border, notification: the navigator's theme. Expo Router exports a useTheme too, and editors auto-import it.
B is wrong: That is TS1360 in colors.ts (Q6).
C is wrong: The provider is for the navigator; it does not change our hook.
D is wrong: The roles are strings; the property does not exist on that type.
Q9
Why does createThemedStyles build both style sets when the file loads, instead of calling StyleSheet.create inside each component?
A) StyleSheet.create cannot run inside a component
B) Hooks cannot return objects
C) It would work, but build new style objects on every render; two ready-made sets cost nothing at run time
D) The dark set must exist before the phone switches
Why: The lesson's answer: inside a component StyleSheet.create would run on every render. createThemedStyles makes the light and the dark set once and returns a tiny hook that picks one.
A is wrong: It can; it is just wasteful.
B is wrong: They can.
D is wrong: Nothing requires that; it is about render cost.
Q10
Sea Teal (#1f6f8b) reads 2.4:1 on the night page. What does the dark set do, and what follows for a filled button's text?
A) Keeps Sea Teal and makes the button bigger
B) Uses pure black for the page
C) Inverts every color
D) A lighter teal (#7cc6d6, 9:1), so onAction flips to dark text (the night page color)
Why: Dark mode is designed, not inverted: lighter, softer accents, the same 4.5:1 and 3:1 rules, no pure black. A light teal button then needs dark words.
A is wrong: Size does not fix contrast.
B is wrong: No pure black: a deep blue-black page, lighter raised surfaces.
C is wrong: Inverting breaks the brand and the badges.
Q11
With React Native's plain useColorScheme, the web preview in dark mode showed a Sand page under dark cards, with no error. Why, and what fixed it?
the page is drawn on a server first (light, it cannot know the browser's setting) and React kept the server's look while hydrating; useColorScheme.web.ts starts as 'light' like the server (useSyncExternalStore's third argument) and switches right after
Why: web.output: server (the template's setting) draws each page on a server; production React silently keeps mismatched class names. Phones do not have this problem (the lesson's quiz Q5).
Q12
On a 1280-wide browser window the cards should stop at 960 and sit in the middle. What does that need?
A) maxWidth: 960, alignSelf: 'center' (and width: '100%') on the content: no window size at all
B) useWindowDimensions and a margin computed from it
C) Platform.select({ web: 960 })
D) A third breakpoint in useColumns
Why: Flex, percentages and maxWidth first: maxWidth with alignSelf: 'center' is max-width + margin: 0 auto. Use the window's size only when the structure changes (one column to two).
B is wrong: Works, but the plain style tools do it without a hook.
C is wrong: Width, not platform, decides layout.
D is wrong: Columns are a structure change; this is only a limit.

Demo files (Live demo 1, "Size is a number from a hook")

In a copy of labs/module-07/l2_5-responsive-platform-specific-and-themed-ui/solution/: the lesson's WindowSize, Dimensions.get snippet (as staleColumns.ts) and PlatformNote copied from the lesson page into src/, their AppText import pointed at this stand-in src/AppText.tsx:

import { Text, type TextProps } from 'react-native';

// A stand-in for Shoreline Crew's AppText: a plain Text
export function AppText(props: TextProps) {
  return <Text {...props} />;
}

and this test as src/window.test.tsx:

import { act, render, screen } from '@testing-library/react-native';
import { Dimensions } from 'react-native';

import { PlatformNote } from './PlatformNote';
import { columns } from './staleColumns';
import { WindowSize } from './WindowSize';

// The words a Text shows (its children without false and null, the way React draws them)
const shown = (children: unknown[]) => children.filter((c) => typeof c === 'string' || typeof c === 'number').join('');
const text = () => shown(screen.getByText(/ by /).props.children);

test('the window, a stale read and a platform, headless', async () => {
  const lines: string[] = [];
  await render(<WindowSize />);
  lines.push('WindowSize, a phone:           ' + text());
  const tablet = { width: 1024, height: 768, scale: 2, fontScale: 1 };
  await act(async () => Dimensions.set({ window: tablet, screen: tablet }));
  lines.push('WindowSize, after the resize:  ' + text());
  lines.push('columns from Dimensions.get:   ' + columns + ' (read when the file loaded)');
  await render(<PlatformNote />);
  lines.push('PlatformNote:                  ' + shown(screen.getByText(/back/).props.children));
  const fs = require('fs') as { writeFileSync(file: string, text: string): void }; // Node's, inside Jest
  fs.writeFileSync('window.txt', lines.join('\n') + '\n');
});

npm test -- src/window.test.tsx passes and cat window.txt prints:

WindowSize, a phone:           390 by 844, text size ×1, 3 pixels per point
WindowSize, after the resize:  1024 by 768, text size ×1, 2 pixels per point
columns from Dimensions.get:   2 (read when the file loaded)
PlatformNote:                  ios: Swipe from the left edge to go back

On the finished app, npx tsc --noEmit prints nothing and npx expo lint prints only the .env.local lines:

env: load .env.local
env: export EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 2.5 · Responsive, Platform-Specific and Themed UI
  1. C The layout stays as it was when the file loaded: The width was read once, when the file loaded, and never again. useWindowDimensions inside the component re-renders it whenever the window changes, which is what a split screen, a rotation or a resized browser needs.
  2. A The color was fixed when the file loaded; the screen needs themed styles: StyleSheet.create at the top level runs once, when the file loads, with whatever color it was given. Colors in styles have to come through createThemedStyles (one sheet per theme, picked by a hook), and colors for props from useTheme().
  3. D The window's width from useWindowDimensions, compared with 700: Layouts follow the room the window has right now, in density-independent pixels, whatever the device or platform. useColumns reads useWindowDimensions and compares the width with layout.twoColumns.
  4. B tsc reports that trackOff, action and knob do not exist on the navigator's colors: Expo Router's useTheme returns the navigator's theme, whose colors are primary, background, card, text, border and notification. tsc reports that trackOff, action and knob do not exist on that type. Fix the import.
  5. C A useColorScheme.web.ts that starts as 'light', like the server, then switches: The server cannot know the browser's setting and draws the light page. useColorScheme.web.ts starts as light, like the server (the third argument of useSyncExternalStore), then switches after hydration. Phones keep React Native's hook.

Exit Ticket

  1. Stale at load: a StyleSheet color → createThemedStyles; Dimensions.get → useWindowDimensions; a hook at the top of a file → inside the component. Any one.
  2. The window: width decides structure; an Android tablet, a split-screen iPad and a wide browser all need two columns, and a platform check gets them wrong.
  3. A designed night: contrast must still pass (lighter accents, dark text on them), no pure black, raised surfaces lighter; badges and the banner stay on purpose.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 B or D. Has not separated "when the file loads" from "when the component renders". Show line 3 of the demo again.
  • Q3 B. Expects Jest to be the web preview: each environment runs its own branch.
  • Q5 A. Thinks TypeScript knows the platform; it checks the plain file.
  • Q6 A. Does not see the value of an error at the cause: compare the two error locations.
  • Q9 A or B. Believes the rule is technical; it is about render cost.
  • Q12 B. Reaches for the window first: flex, percentages and maxWidth come first.

Session 8: FlatList, SectionList and Big Lists

Answers and reasoning for every Session 8 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes, the Part 1 project as handed out, and the exit ticket.

Session 8 12 self-check items Confidential

Opening: Warm-up Answers

  1. A color baked into a style sheet stays light in dark mode: it was worked out once, when the file loaded. Colors in style sheets come through createThemedStyles, colors for props from useTheme() (Lesson 2.5).
  2. Platform.OS for a real platform difference: today, the web preview has no pull-to-refresh gesture, so Shoreline Crew shows a Refresh button on the web only.
  3. Two columns come from useColumns(): useWindowDimensions().width >= layout.twoColumns (700).

3.1 · FlatList and SectionList: self-check

Q1
The course opened two screens of 1,000 rows at phone size and counted the rows in the page. What did it find when they opened?
A) Both drew about 20 rows
B) ScrollView 1,000; FlatList 358, about ten screens above and below what shows
C) ScrollView 358; FlatList 1,000
D) FlatList drew only the rows on screen, about 15
Why: A ScrollView draws every child at once; a FlatList draws what is near the screen (its windowSize is 21 screens) and more as you scroll: virtualization. A row thrown away is drawn again from data (Lesson 3.1, Why a List Component).
A is wrong: A ScrollView never skips a child.
C is wrong: The other way round.
D is wrong: FlatList draws generously: ten screens' worth each way.
Q2
The kit ran the lesson's "header made inside the screen" in Jest: ListHeaderComponent={() => <TextInput ... />}, then typed lo. Why does the field hold only l, and what do you write instead?
expect(instance).toHaveDisplayValue()

Expected instance to have display value:
  lo
Received:
  l
Each render makes a new function, so React sees a new component and builds a new text field after the first letter; pass an ELEMENT (const header = (...))
Why: On a phone the keyboard closes after every letter. tsc and npx expo lint say nothing (the kit ran both: silent); only running it shows it (Lesson 3.1, Common Mistakes; Lab 3.1 Task 1).
Q3
npx tsc --noEmit prints this in shoreline-crew. Which change fixes it?
src/app/index.tsx:178:7 - error TS2322: Type '(card: Card) => number' is not assignable to type '(item: Card, index: number) => string'.
Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:178
A) keyExtractor={(card) => String(card.event.capacity)}
B) Delete keyExtractor: FlatList finds a key itself
C) keyExtractor={(card) => card.event.id}
D) Add // @ts-expect-error above the line
Why: Keys are strings, and they come from the data's identity: the API's id is unique and never changes. Capacity is not unique even as a string (Lesson 3.1, Exercise 2).
A is wrong: It type-checks, but two cleanups can have the same capacity.
B is wrong: Without one, React Native 0.88 quietly falls back to the item's position: the index-key bug.
D is wrong: Silences the check and keeps a wrong key.
Q4
A volunteer pulls the list to refresh with no signal. The spinner never stops. Which line is missing?
A) onRefresh={refresh}
B) refreshing={refreshing}
C) key={columns}
D) setRefreshing(false) in the .catch, not only in the .then
Why: The list shows the platform's spinner for as long as refreshing is true: switch it off when the data arrives AND when the request fails (Lesson 3.1, Step 5).
A is wrong: Then nothing would refresh at all.
B is wrong: Then no spinner would show.
C is wrong: The number of columns has nothing to do with refreshing.
Q5
In the web preview, a learner drags the Cleanups list down and nothing refreshes. What is going on?
A) A browser has no pull gesture for a list, and react-native-web's RefreshControl draws a plain View: test the pull in Expo Go
B) onRefresh is spelled wrong
C) The list needs a ScrollView around it
D) The API is down
Why: A real platform difference: Shoreline Crew shows a Refresh button only on the web (Platform.OS === 'web') and leaves the gesture to phones (Lesson 3.1, the web preview has no pull to refresh).
B is wrong: The same code refreshes on a phone.
C is wrong: A list inside a ScrollView breaks virtualization.
D is wrong: With the API down, the Refresh button would show the error view.
Q6
Group by date on Android: the headings scroll away instead of staying on top. What is missing?
A) numColumns on the SectionList
B) stickySectionHeadersEnabled, set explicitly, with a background on the heading
C) A key on each section
D) renderSectionHeader must return a View
Why: React Native turns sticky headings on for iOS only; Shoreline Crew sets the prop so every platform behaves the same, and gives the heading the page color so cards do not show through it.
A is wrong: SectionList has no numColumns (the lesson's quiz Q5).
C is wrong: Sections have titles, not keys.
D is wrong: Any element works; the AppText heading is fine.

3.1 · FlatList and SectionList: predict, then check

Both are one run of the kit's demo test (src/mistakes.test.tsx in a copy of Lab 3.1's solution: the lesson's InlineHeader and NoKey with plain React Native components). The test file:

import { render, screen, userEvent } from '@testing-library/react-native';
import { useState } from 'react';
import { FlatList, Text, TextInput } from 'react-native';

const beaches = ['Driftwood Cove', 'Otter Creek Mouth', 'Long Strand', 'Jetty Rocks'];
jest.setTimeout(30_000); // a first render on a busy laptop can take seconds

// Don't: a new header component on every render (Lesson 3.1, Common Mistakes)
function InlineHeader() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  return (
    <FlatList
      data={beaches.filter((beach) => beach.toLowerCase().includes(query.toLowerCase()))}
      keyExtractor={(beach) => beach}
      renderItem={({ item }) => <Text>{item}</Text>}
      ListHeaderComponent={() => (
        <TextInput accessibilityLabel="Search beaches" value={query} onChangeText={setQuery} />
      )}
    />
  );
}

// Don't: the number of columns changes, but the FlatList is the same one
function NoKey({ columns }: { columns: number }) {
  return (
    <FlatList
      data={beaches}
      keyExtractor={(beach) => beach}
      numColumns={columns}
      renderItem={({ item }) => <Text>{item}</Text>}
    />
  );
}

test('an inline header: type "lo"', async () => {
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  await render(<InlineHeader />);
  await user.type(screen.getByLabelText('Search beaches'), 'lo');
  expect(screen.getByLabelText('Search beaches')).toHaveDisplayValue('lo');
});

test('numColumns from 1 to 2, no key', async () => {
  await render(<NoKey columns={1} />);
  await screen.rerender(<NoKey columns={2} />);
});
P1
Predict, then run it.
npm test -- src/mistakes.test.tsx
expect(instance).toHaveDisplayValue()

Expected instance to have display value:
  lo
Received:
  l
Why: l: a new header component on every render, so React builds a new text field after the first letter; the rest of the typing goes nowhere. On a phone the keyboard closes after every letter. tsc and the linter print nothing. Self-check Q2, Lab 3.1 Task 1.
P2
Predict, then run it.
npm test -- src/mistakes.test.tsx
Invariant Violation: Changing numColumns on the fly is not supported. Change the key prop on FlatList when changing the number of columns to force a fresh render of the component.
Why: tsc says nothing; the run stops with React Native's invariant, the words the lesson's browser console showed (on a phone, the red screen). key={columns}. Lab 3.1 Task 1.

3.2 · Big Lists, Fast: self-check

Q7
In the course's list lab, which number shows FlashList's big idea most clearly?
A) Rows in the page when it opens
B) Frames per second at full speed
C) The time the first row takes to appear
D) Row components created during the scroll test: thousands for FlatList, a few dozen for FlashList
Why: FlatList unmounts rows that leave its window and creates new ones; FlashList keeps them in pools and draws them again with new data: recycling. At full speed both stayed near 60 fps; the gap opened with the CPU slowed six times (Lesson 3.2, Measure, Don't Guess).
A is wrong: That shows the drawn area, not the reuse.
B is wrong: Both stayed close to 60 on a desktop at full speed.
C is wrong: The lesson did not measure that.
Q8
Moving the list to FlashList, a learner left columnWrapperStyle={styles.cell} on it. tsc prints this (its long first line shortened). Which line names the problem, and where does the spacing go instead?
src/app/index.tsx:221:9 - error TS2322: Type '{ data: …; … 16 more …; }' is not assignable to type 'IntrinsicAttributes & FlashListProps<SectionRow<Card>> & { ref?: Ref<FlashListRef<SectionRow<Card>>> | undefined; }'.
Found 1 error in src/app/index.tsx:221
Property 'columnWrapperStyle' does not exist on type 'IntrinsicAttributes & FlashListProps<SectionRow<Card>> & { ref?: Ref<FlashListRef<SectionRow<Card>>> | undefined; }'.
The line after it: Property 'columnWrapperStyle' does not exist; FlashList has no row style, so the spacing lives inside each cell (half the gap on each side)
Why: With long tsc errors, read the last indented line first: it is the specific reason. FlashList places every cell itself (Lesson 3.2, Exercise 3).
Q9
useScreenScroll() puts the safe-area padding in style. Why does the Cleanups screen put that part on a View around the FlashList?
A) tsc rejects padding on FlashList
B) FlashList's documentation says padding in style is not supported; margin is fine
C) A View is faster than a list
D) The safe areas only work on a View
Why: The type checker accepts it (style is an ordinary view style), so this is one only the documentation tells you: const { style: frame, ...scroll } = useScreenScroll(); and <View style={frame}><FlashList {...scroll} ... (Lesson 3.2, Step 4).
A is wrong: It type-checks: that is the trap.
C is wrong: Speed has nothing to do with it.
D is wrong: Padding works on any view; FlashList's own style is the exception.
Q10
The lab screen without its two casts: tsc prints this. What is the honest fix?
src/app/list-lab.tsx:39:34 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'RefObject<FlashListRef<string> | null>' is not assignable to parameter of type 'RefObject<FlashListRef<any>>'.
A) useBenchmark(flashRef as RefObject<FlashListRef<string>>, ...): the ref is only used after the button is pressed, when the list exists
B) useRef<FlashListRef<string>>() without null
C) // @ts-ignore on both lines
D) Turn off strict in tsconfig.json
Why: The hooks were typed before React 19 changed what useRef(null) returns. A cast tells TypeScript what you know; use one only when you can say that sentence (Lesson 3.2, Step 2).
B is wrong: React 19's useRef wants an initial value.
C is wrong: Hides every other error on those lines too.
D is wrong: Switches off checks for the whole project.
Q11
Page 2 failed and the volunteer is still at the end of the list. Why must onEndReached NOT call loadNextPage now, and what asks again?
Every failure changes the footer, FlashList checks the end again and would ask again and again (a busy radio, a drained battery); only when more is idle; after a failure only the footer's Try again asks
Why: Ask once, stop after a failure, never show a cleanup twice: the three details that make infinite scroll shippable (Lesson 3.2; Lab 3.2 Task 2 counts the requests).
Q12
While a volunteer scrolls, a new cleanup is added before the ones already loaded. Page 2 then starts with a cleanup the list already has. What does Shoreline Crew do?
A) Asks for page 2 again
B) Shows it twice: the API's order is the truth
C) Keeps only the items of page 2 whose id it does not have yet
D) Reloads every page from 1
Why: Pages are counted from the start of the list, so items shift by one. A Set of the known ids makes "never show it twice" one line, and keeps the list's keys unique (Lesson 3.2, Step 6).
A is wrong: Page 2 would still start with the same item.
B is wrong: A repeated cleanup repeats a key: the list's bookkeeping breaks.
D is wrong: Throws away what the volunteer already sees.

  // Above the beaches, in every state. An element, not a component: the field keeps its text and its focus.
  const header = (

The solution:

 PASS  src/lab.test.tsx
  ✓ Task 1: a recycled row does not keep the last cleanup's report open
  ✓ Task 2: near the end twice while page 2 is on its way: page 2 is asked for once
  ✓ Task 2: page 2 fails: the list stops asking until Try again

Tests:       6 passed, 6 total

What each task shows: (1) rerender with another cleanup is what recycling does, and useRecyclingState(false, [cleanup.id]) closes the report for the new cleanup; (2) the idle check (more.status === 'idle') and the Set of known ids, exactly Lesson 3.2's two details, with MSW holding page 2 "on its way" so a second call really happens while it waits; (3) the flattened month rows, getItemType and stickyHeaderIndices (the test counts each month once: FlashList draws a pinned copy of the current heading). The solution's hook:

  // The next page, added after the cleanups the list already has
  function loadNextPage() {
    if (load.status !== 'loaded' || !hasMore) {
      return;
    }
    setMore({ status: 'loading' });
    getPastPage(api, load.page.page + 1)
      .then((next) => {
        setLoad((current) => {
          if (current.status !== 'loaded') {
            return current;
          }

          // A cleanup can move to the next page while you scroll: never show it twice
          const known = new Set(current.cleanups.map((cleanup) => cleanup.id));
          const added = next.items.filter((cleanup) => !known.has(cleanup.id));
          return { ...current, cleanups: [...current.cleanups, ...added], page: next.page };
        });
        setMore({ status: 'idle' });
      })
      .catch((error: unknown) => setMore({ status: 'failed', message: message(error) }));
  }

  // The list is near its end. Only ask when nothing is on its way and the last page did not fail:
  // after a failure, Try again (loadNextPage) is the only way to ask again
  function onEndReached() {
    if (more.status === 'idle') {
      loadNextPage();
    }
  }

  return { load, more, hasMore, loadNextPage, onEndReached };
}

npm run typecheck and npm run lint print nothing after their > lines; the solution also bundles for the web (npx expo export --platform web: Exported: dist).

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 3.1 · FlatList and SectionList
  1. D The keyboard closes after every letter: The arrow function is a new component on every render, so React replaces the header, and its text field, after each letter. Focus is lost and the keyboard closes. Pass an element (const header = ...) instead.
  2. B An error: changing numColumns on the fly is not supported: FlatList cannot change numColumns in place: it stops with Changing numColumns on the fly is not supported. A key that changes with the number of columns, key={columns}, makes React build a new list instead.
  3. C (card) => card.event.id: The API's id is unique and never changes, so each drawn card stays with its cleanup while the list is filtered, refreshed or regrouped. Positions and titles can both change.
  4. A In the list's ListEmptyComponent, under the banner and the search field: The failed load leaves the list with no cards, so FlatList shows ListEmptyComponent, which is the ErrorView in the failed state. The header (banner, search, switches) stays above it.
  5. B SectionList has no numColumns: SectionList has no numColumns, so the grouped view is one card per line at any width. Getting columns there means putting rows of cards in each section yourself.
Lesson 3.2 · Big Lists, Fast
  1. B They are kept and reused to draw the cards coming into view: FlashList keeps them in a pool of spare rows of the same type and draws them again with the next items' data. That is why the lab counted a few dozen rows created for 1,000 items.
  2. D useRecyclingState(false, [event.id]): useRecyclingState resets to its initial value when a dependency changes. With the cleanup's id as the dependency, a recycled card starts closed for its new cleanup.
  3. B The footer shows the error and Try again; nothing is asked until the volunteer presses it: After a failure the next page is failed, not idle, so onEndReached does nothing. The footer shows describeError's message and Try again, which is the only thing that asks again.
  4. C The short list is near its end, so it loads page 2, where the cleanup is found: With no cards, the list is shorter than the screen, so it is near its end and calls onEndReached. Page 2 arrives, the filter runs again, and Night Heron Walk and Clean appears: 1 of 8 cleanups.
  5. C Half the gap of padding inside each cell, on both sides: FlashList positions every cell itself, so each cell carries half the gap on each side. The list's own side padding is halved, and the header, empty list and footer get the other half, so everything lines up.

Exit Ticket

  1. Keys from the data: the key keeps each drawn row with its item; a position points at another item after filtering, so state (an open Show details) moves to the wrong card. An id from the API never changes.
  2. Infinite scroll: ask once (only when the next page is idle), stop after a failure (only Try again asks), never show an item twice (drop the ids you have). And stop at the API's X-Total-Pages.
  3. Fix-It Tables: their own; check it against the milestones (TODO B and C before Session 9).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 D. Thinks virtualization draws only what is visible. The lesson's 358: ten screens each way.
  • Q3 A. Fixes the type and keeps a non-unique key. Ask: can two cleanups have the same capacity?
  • Q5 B or C. Debugs the code when the platform is the difference. Show the web-only Refresh button.
  • Q7 B. Reads frame rates on a desktop as proof; full speed hides the gap. The 6x line and the rows created.
  • Q9 A. Expects tsc to know FlashList's documentation. It type-checks: that is why it is a trap.
  • Q12 A or B. Trusts page numbers as stable. Pages count from the start of the list, which can move.

Session 9: Part 1 Project Check-in and Assessment

Answers and marking for Session 9: the warm-up, the Part 1 assessment's written part and practical, the Part 1 review self-check, and the project model's outputs.

Session 9 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up Answers

  1. A style-name typo: npx tsc --noEmit (TS2551 with a Did you mean); on a phone the text just loses its look.
  2. The count: derived from the tickets, the search and the switch on every render (filterTickets(...).length), never stored.
  3. key={columns}: a FlatList cannot change its number of columns; a new key builds a new list.

Part 1 Assessment: Written Part (model answers and marking)

10 points. W2 and W3 show real tsc output from a copy of the project's model solution with the change made.

W1
Metro bundles a file with a type error and the phone shows the app. Why does nothing complain, and which command finds the error? (1)
Metro removes the types and bundles the JavaScript that is left: it never checks them. npx tsc --noEmit (here npm run typecheck) reads the types and reports the error.
Marking: The point needs both halves: running is not checking, and a tsc command.
W2
A learner writes this line in the ticket card and npm run typecheck prints the error below. What does it mean, and how do you fix it? (2)
src/components/TicketCard.tsx:37:56 - error TS18048: 'ticket.fixer' is possibly 'undefined'.
Found 1 error in src/components/TicketCard.tsx:37
fixer is optional in Ticket (fixer?: string), so its type is string | undefined, and checking the status does not tell TypeScript that a fixer is there. Fix: check the property itself, ticket.fixer !== undefined && ... (or ticket.fixer?.toUpperCase()).
Marking: 1 point for "possibly undefined because it is optional", 1 for a fix that narrows. A non-null assertion ! or as string scores 0 for the fix.
W3
The café adds a fifth status, 'collected', to TicketStatus. What does npm run typecheck do next, and why is that useful? (1)
src/utils/tickets.ts:12:14 - error TS2741: Property 'collected' is missing in type '{ waiting: string; 'in-repair': string; fixed: string; 'not-fixable': string; }' but required in type 'Record<TicketStatus, string>'.
src/utils/tickets.ts:19:14 - error TS2741: Property 'collected' is missing in type '{ waiting: "neutral"; 'in-repair': "info"; fixed: "success"; 'not-fixable': "danger"; }' but required in type 'Record<TicketStatus, BadgeTone>'.
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/utils/tickets.ts:12
It reports every Record<TicketStatus, ...> table that has no entry for 'collected' (here statusLabels and statusTones, TS2741). The compiler lists every place the new status needs a decision before anything runs.
Marking: The point needs "it lists the tables missing the new key".
W4
On a phone, this card shows the text Fixer: and nothing else when there is no fixer, but a string straight inside a <View> stops the app. Which rule is behind the second, and which tool finds it? (1)
Every piece of text sits in a <Text> (AppText here); text straight in a View is an error when it runs (the red screen; in Jest Text strings must be rendered within a <Text> component). tsc does not find it: only running it (a test, the app).
Marking: The point needs the rule and "only running it".
W5
Why does the queue use keyExtractor={(ticket) => ticket.id} and not the index? (1)
A key tells the list which drawn row belongs to which ticket. When the search or Hide finished removes tickets, an index would point at a different ticket, so an open Show problem would jump to the wrong card. An id stays with its ticket.
Marking: Accept any answer that ties the key to the item's identity when the list changes.
W6
A teammate writes ListHeaderComponent={() => <SearchField ... />}. What does a volunteer notice on a phone, why, and what do you write instead? (2)
The keyboard closes after every letter (the field loses what is typed). A new function on every render is a new component type, so React throws the old header away and builds a new text field. Build the header as an element (const header = (...)) and pass that.
Marking: 1 point for the symptom and the reason, 1 for the element.
W7
The window gets wider than 700 and the queue goes from one column to two. Without key={columns} the screen stops. Why does the key fix it? (1)
A FlatList cannot change its number of columns (Changing numColumns on the fly is not supported). A new key tells React it is a different list, so it builds a new one with the new number of columns.
Marking: The point needs "a new key builds a new list".
W8
Name two things a phone's dark mode needs from the code, as the course does it. (1)
Any two of: colors by job in a light and a dark set (ThemeColors); styles made per theme (createThemedStyles), never a color baked in when the file loads; colors handed to props from useTheme(); a sticky heading with a background from the theme.
Marking: Half a point each, rounded down.
