The solution: npm test (npm run typecheck and npm run lint print nothing after their two > lines):

 PASS  src/lab.test.ts
  ✓ Task 1: nothing saved yet gives the defaults
  ✓ Task 1: a change is saved and comes back
  ✓ Task 1: a unit this version does not know falls back to kg
  ✓ Task 1: text that is not JSON gives the defaults, with a warning
  ✓ Task 2: a note with an apostrophe is saved as written

  ✓ Task 3: a new phone gets version 2 of notes.db
  ✓ Task 3: a phone with version 1 keeps its notes and gets the tide column

What each task shows: (1) saved data checked like API data (the stage's fromSaved pattern), JSON.parse inside the try so a bad save gives the defaults and a warning, never a rejection. (2) ? values in runAsync and getAllAsync, ORDER BY written_at DESC, columns renamed with AS to match the type. (3) PRAGMA user_version, a version 1 step that is never changed and a version 2 step ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN tide TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'unknown', both in withTransactionAsync; the test opens a real version 1 file first. Common sticking points: '?' in quotes; a new column NOT NULL without a DEFAULT (SQLite refuses it on a table with rows); changing the version 1 CREATE TABLE instead of adding a step.

labs/module-13/l5_3-storing-data-on-the-device/solution/src/state/prefs.ts
// Task 1: the crew leader's preferences, kept on the phone with AsyncStorage: one key, one small JSON object.
import AsyncStorage from '@react-native-async-storage/async-storage';

export type WeightUnit = 'kg' | 'lb';

export type Prefs = {
  unit: WeightUnit;
  tipsSeen: boolean;
};

export const prefsKey = 'crew-lab-prefs';
export const defaultPrefs: Prefs = { unit: 'kg', tipsSeen: false };

const units: WeightUnit[] = ['kg', 'lb'];

// What was saved is checked like API data: anything this version does not know becomes the default
function fromSaved(saved: string | null): Prefs {
  const value: unknown = saved === null ? null : JSON.parse(saved);
  if (typeof value !== 'object' || value === null) {
    return defaultPrefs;
  }
  const unit = 'unit' in value ? units.find((known) => known === value.unit) : undefined;
  const tipsSeen = 'tipsSeen' in value && typeof value.tipsSeen === 'boolean' ? value.tipsSeen : undefined;
  return { unit: unit ?? defaultPrefs.unit, tipsSeen: tipsSeen ?? defaultPrefs.tipsSeen };
}

/** The saved preferences, checked. It never rejects: a broken save gives the defaults and a warning. */
export async function loadPrefs(): Promise<Prefs> {
  try {
    return fromSaved(await AsyncStorage.getItem(prefsKey));
  } catch (error) {
    console.warn('Could not read the saved preferences', error);
    return defaultPrefs;
  }
}

/** Change some preferences: merged with what is saved, saved again, and returned */
export async function changePrefs(changes: Partial<Prefs>): Promise<Prefs> {
  const next = { ...(await loadPrefs()), ...changes };
  await AsyncStorage.setItem(prefsKey, JSON.stringify(next));
  return next;
}
labs/module-13/l5_3-storing-data-on-the-device/solution/src/storage/notes.ts
// Tasks 2 and 3: the crew leader's notes about each beach, in a SQLite database on the phone. SQLite, not
// AsyncStorage: many records with the same fields, read one site at a time, newest first.
import * as SQLite from 'expo-sqlite';

export type Tide = 'low' | 'high' | 'unknown';

export type SiteNote = {
  siteId: string;
  text: string;
  tide: Tide;
  writtenAt: number; // milliseconds, as Date.now() gives them
};

// The version of the tables below. Version 2 added the tide column.
const schemaVersion = 2;

let opening: Promise<SQLite.SQLiteDatabase> | null = null;

// One connection for the whole app, opened and brought up to date the first time it is needed
function database(): Promise<SQLite.SQLiteDatabase> {
  opening ??= openNotes().catch((error: unknown) => {
    opening = null; // try again next time instead of keeping the failure
    throw error;
  });
  return opening;
}

async function openNotes() {
  const db = await SQLite.openDatabaseAsync('notes.db');
  await migrate(db);
  return db;
}

/** Close the connection; the next call opens the file again (the tests use it: a new phone for every test) */
export async function closeNotes(): Promise<void> {
  const db = await opening;
  opening = null;
  await db?.closeAsync();
}

// SQLite keeps a number in every database file, user_version (0 in a new one). Each step brings an older file
// one version further; all of them and the new number succeed or fail together.
async function migrate(db: SQLite.SQLiteDatabase) {
  const row = await db.getFirstAsync<{ user_version: number }>('PRAGMA user_version');
  const version = row?.user_version ?? 0;
  if (version >= schemaVersion) {
    return;
  }
  await db.withTransactionAsync(async () => {
    if (version < 1) {
      await db.execAsync(`
        CREATE TABLE site_notes (
          site_id TEXT NOT NULL,
          text TEXT NOT NULL,
          written_at INTEGER NOT NULL
        );
      `);
    }
    if (version < 2) {
      // The tide when the note was written. Notes from version 1 get 'unknown' (NOT NULL needs a default).
      await db.execAsync("ALTER TABLE site_notes ADD COLUMN tide TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'unknown'");
    }
    // A PRAGMA takes no ? values; schemaVersion is our own number, never user input
    await db.execAsync(`PRAGMA user_version = ${schemaVersion}`);
  });
}

/** Save one note. Every ? is filled in by SQLite with the value in the same place: never build SQL from data. */
export async function addNote(note: SiteNote): Promise<void> {
  const db = await database();
  await db.runAsync(
    'INSERT INTO site_notes (site_id, text, tide, written_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?)',
    note.siteId,
    note.text,
    note.tide,
    note.writtenAt,
  );
}

/** One site's notes, the newest first */
export async function notesFor(siteId: string): Promise<SiteNote[]> {
  const db = await database();
  return db.getAllAsync<SiteNote>(
    `SELECT site_id AS siteId, text, tide, written_at AS writtenAt
       FROM site_notes
      WHERE site_id = ?
      ORDER BY written_at DESC`,
    siteId,
  );
}

labs/module-13/l5_4-mobile-forms/solution/src/ControlledInput.tsx
import type { ComponentProps } from 'react';
import { type Control, type FieldPath, type FieldValues, useController } from 'react-hook-form';
import { StyleSheet, Text, TextInput, View } from 'react-native';

// TextInput's props, minus the ones React Hook Form provides. T is what the fields hold; Out is what the form
// gives back after the schema's checks (numbers instead of text).
type ControlledInputProps<T extends FieldValues, Out> = Omit<
  ComponentProps<typeof TextInput>,
  'value' | 'onChangeText' | 'onBlur' | 'ref'
> & {
  control: Control<T, unknown, Out>;
  name: FieldPath<T>;
  label: string;
};

// A labeled text field that belongs to a React Hook Form form. A phone has no HTML input for register() to
// find, so useController hands over the value, the change and blur handlers, the field's message and a ref:
// with it, setFocus('volunteers') and "focus the first field with a problem" work.
export function ControlledInput<T extends FieldValues, Out = T>({
  control,
  name,
  label,
  ...input
}: ControlledInputProps<T, Out>) {
  // Taken apart at once: the lint rules treat an object with a ref inside as a ref
  const {
    field: { ref, value, onChange, onBlur },
    fieldState: { error },
  } = useController({ control, name });
  return (
    <View style={styles.field}>
      <Text style={styles.label}>{label}</Text>
      <TextInput
        {...input}
        ref={ref}
        accessibilityLabel={label}
        value={value}
        onChangeText={onChange}
        onBlur={onBlur}
        style={styles.input}
      />
      {error?.message !== undefined && <Text style={styles.error}>{error.message}</Text>}
    </View>
  );
}

const styles = StyleSheet.create({
  field: { gap: 4 },
  label: { fontWeight: '600', color: '#2a1257' },
  input: { minHeight: 48, paddingHorizontal: 12, borderWidth: 1, borderRadius: 8, borderColor: '#6b5a8e', fontSize: 16 },
  error: { color: '#b3261e' },
});
labs/module-13/l5_4-mobile-forms/solution/src/schema.ts
// The shift report a crew leader sends at the end of a cleanup: the form's type AND its checks (Zod 4)
import { z } from 'zod';

// A count typed into a box: digits only, so an empty box is a message, not a silent 0 (the HQ's wholeNumber)
function wholeNumber(min: number, max: number, message: string) {
  return z
    .string()
    .trim()
    .regex(/^\d+$/, message)
    .transform(Number)
    .refine((count) => count >= min && count <= max, message);
}

const hoursRule = 'Hours must be a number from 0 to 12.';

export const shiftReportSchema = z.object({
  leader: z.string().trim().min(1, "Enter the crew leader's name."),
  volunteers: wholeNumber(1, 200, 'Volunteers must be a whole number from 1 to 200.'),
  hours: z
    .string()
    .trim()
    // Many phones set to other languages offer a comma as the decimal key: 2,5 means 2.5
    .transform((typed) => typed.replace(',', '.'))
    .pipe(z.string().regex(/^\d+(\.\d+)?$/, hoursRule))
    .transform(Number)
    .refine((hours) => hours <= 12, hoursRule),
});

/** What the fields hold: text, as typed */
export type ShiftReportValues = z.input<typeof shiftReportSchema>;

/** What a successful check gives back: numbers, ready to send */
export type ShiftReport = z.output<typeof shiftReportSchema>;
labs/module-13/l5_4-mobile-forms/solution/src/ShiftReportForm.tsx
import { zodResolver } from '@hookform/resolvers/zod';
import { useForm } from 'react-hook-form';
import { Pressable, StyleSheet, Text, View } from 'react-native';

import { ControlledInput } from './ControlledInput';
import { type ShiftReport, shiftReportSchema, type ShiftReportValues } from './schema';

const emptyReport: ShiftReportValues = { leader: '', volunteers: '', hours: '' };

type ShiftReportFormProps = {
  onSend: (report: ShiftReport) => void;
};

// The crew leader's shift report: who led, how many volunteers came, how many hours the crew worked.
export function ShiftReportForm({ onSend }: ShiftReportFormProps) {
  const { control, handleSubmit, setFocus } = useForm({
    resolver: zodResolver(shiftReportSchema),
    defaultValues: emptyReport,
    mode: 'onTouched',
  });

  // Check, then send. Called with no arguments: handleSubmit expects a web form event, and a phone's press and
  // return key send different events
  const send = handleSubmit(onSend);

  return (
    <View style={styles.form}>
      <ControlledInput
        control={control}
        name="leader"
        label="Crew leader"
        autoCapitalize="words"
        autoComplete="name"
        enterKeyHint="next" // the return key says Next...
        submitBehavior="submit" // ...keeps the keyboard up...
        onSubmitEditing={() => setFocus('volunteers')} // ...and moves on
      />
      <ControlledInput
        control={control}
        name="volunteers"
        label="Volunteers"
        inputMode="numeric" // the number pad (on iPhone it has no return key: the button below sends)
        enterKeyHint="next"
        submitBehavior="submit"
        onSubmitEditing={() => setFocus('hours')}
      />
      <ControlledInput
        control={control}
        name="hours"
        label="Hours"
        inputMode="decimal" // digits and a decimal point, or a comma
        placeholder="For example 2.5"
        enterKeyHint="go" // the last field: the return key sends
        onSubmitEditing={() => send()}
      />
      <Pressable accessibilityRole="button" onPress={() => send()} style={styles.button}>
        <Text style={styles.buttonText}>Send report</Text>
      </Pressable>
    </View>
  );
}

const styles = StyleSheet.create({
  form: { gap: 16, padding: 16 },
  button: { minHeight: 48, justifyContent: 'center', alignItems: 'center', borderRadius: 8, backgroundColor: '#6534c4' },
  buttonText: { color: '#ffffff', fontWeight: 'bold' },
});

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 5.3 · Storing Data on the Device
  1. C SecureStore: Tokens are secrets. SecureStore keeps them in the iOS Keychain or encrypted with the Android Keystore; AsyncStorage and SQLite files are not encrypted.
  2. A So the first screen is drawn in the chosen theme, with no light flash: Reading AsyncStorage is async. If the app drew first, a volunteer who chose Dark would see a light screen until the setting arrived. Waiting behind the splash screen means the first screen is already in the right colors.
  3. D His logs and totals, read from the database on the phone: The logbook query reads SQLite on the phone with networkMode always, so TanStack Query does not pause it offline. The rows saved earlier are there; only bringing in new logs waits for a connection.
  4. B See user_version 1 and run only the step that adds the column, then set version 2: PRAGMA user_version says the file is at version 1, so migrate runs only the new step, an ALTER TABLE that adds the column, and sets version 2 in the same transaction. The rows stay.
  5. C db.runAsync('INSERT INTO notes (note) VALUES (?)', note): The question mark is a placeholder: SQLite receives the note as a value, never as SQL, so apostrophes and anything else in it are stored as written.
Lesson 5.4 · Mobile Forms
  1. B It type-checks, but the form never gets what the volunteer types: register is built around HTML inputs: it reads the value from the change event's target and the element itself. A TextInput reports its text differently, so the form never gets it. useController or Controller hands the field value, onChange, onBlur and a ref instead.
  2. D The iOS decimal pad has no return key at all: According to React Native's documentation, the iOS decimal pad and number pad have no return key, so there is nothing to press. Volunteers tap the next field or drag the form to close the keyboard, which is one reason the counts use steppers.
  3. A Pass the header's height as keyboardVerticalOffset: KeyboardAvoidingView measures the keyboard from the top of the window, but the screen starts under the header. keyboardVerticalOffset with the header height (useHeaderHeight) makes the padding come out right.
  4. C Answers 200 with the log it already has, because the clientId is the same: The form made the clientId once, so both requests carry the same one. shoreline-api finds the log it already saved for that volunteer and clientId and answers 200 with it: a resend, never a duplicate.
  5. B "Sign in to log what you collected", with a Sign in button; after signing in she stays on the log screen: log/[id] is not guarded: it has a guest face. Amara signs in on top of it; the sign-in closes and the screen underneath now knows who she is (and, since she has no spot at that cleanup, it says so in the API's words).

Exit Ticket

  1. Three places: the theme choice in AsyncStorage (small, not secret, read once at startup), the session token in SecureStore (a secret: the Keychain or the Android Keystore), the logbook in SQLite (many records the screen sorts and totals; a copy of what the server has).
  2. networkMode 'always': the logbook's queryFn reads only the phone. With the default mode, TanStack Query pauses it offline ("Opening your logbook…" for ever, measured in the lesson); the logbook must open with no signal.
  3. const send: handleSubmit(fn) returns a function typed for a web form event; React Native's press and return-key events are other types, so tsc rejects passing it straight in (TS2322). Made once and called with no arguments, it type-checks and both the button and the Go key use it.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. "Everything on the phone should be encrypted": SecureStore is for secrets and is small. Back to the van: clipboard, lockbox, ledger.
  • Q2 A. Pictures the logbook as a list that only grows: the primary key is the API's own idempotency. Show P1's run.
  • Q4 A or B. Thinks the query failed. It never ran: paused. Show the lesson's networkMode card.
  • Q5 A. Believes IF NOT EXISTS rebuilds tables. Lab 5.3 Task 3's failing test shows the old file.
  • Q7 A or B. Trusts the checkers with behavior: register is the session's quietest mistake.
  • Q11 A. Has not met idempotency: one clientId, one log, however often it is sent.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 14: Offline First and Permissions

Answers and reasoning for every Session 14 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the demo scripts, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 14 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Warm-up

  1. W1: the session token = SecureStore (Lesson 4.3); the theme choice = AsyncStorage (Lesson 5.3's settings); the logbook of sent logs = SQLite (Lesson 5.3); a log sent with no signal = memory, TanStack Query's paused mutation (Lesson 5.4). Tonight a fifth place appears: the queue in AsyncStorage.
  2. W2: the log disappeared. A paused mutation waits in memory, and force-quitting ends the memory; Lesson 5.4 said so on the screen ("Keep the app open until then").
  3. W3: once per form visit, on the phone (randomUUID() in a useState initializer), and sent with the log, so a resend of the same log is recognized by the server instead of saved twice.

5.5 · Offline First: self-check

Q1
With Lesson 5.4's code, Theo sends a debris log in airplane mode, then force-quits Expo Go. What happens to the log?
A) It is sent the next time Theo opens the app
B) It waits in AsyncStorage until the phone is online
C) It is gone: a paused mutation waits in memory, and memory ends with the app
D) The server receives it twice
Why: TanStack Query's paused mutation waits in its mutation cache, in memory (Lesson 5.2, 5.4's "Keep the app open until then"). Lesson 5.5's queue writes the log to AsyncStorage the moment Send is pressed, before the network is tried at all.
A is wrong: Nothing was saved anywhere that outlives the app.
B is wrong: That is today's queue, not Lesson 5.4's mutation.
D is wrong: Nothing reached the server; and the clientId would make a resend harmless anyway.
Q2
The web course's persisted store, copied with no storage option, run in Node with a window that has no localStorage (as on a phone). What does it print? (The kit ran it.)
// The web course's persisted store, copied as it was: no storage option
globalThis.window = {}; // a window without localStorage, as on a phone
const { create } = await import('zustand');
const { persist } = await import('zustand/middleware');

const useLogQueue = create()(
  persist((set) => ({ logs: [], add: (log) => set(({ logs }) => ({ logs: [...logs, log] })) }), {
    name: 'shoreline-crew-log-queue',
  }),
);
try {
  useLogQueue.getState().add({ clientId: 'c3a0f6b2' });
  console.log('add worked');
} catch (error) {
  console.log('add threw: ' + error);
}
A) add worked
B) add threw: TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'setItem')
C) Nothing: tsc stops it with a missing-option error first
D) add threw: ReferenceError: AsyncStorage is not defined
Why: persist's default storage is the browser's localStorage. On a phone window exists and window.localStorage does not, so the first set fails (Hermes words it "Cannot read property 'setItem' of undefined"). The web preview HAS localStorage, which is why the mistake ships. Fix: storage: createJSONStorage(() => AsyncStorage).
A is wrong: The kit's run printed the TypeError, exactly as the lesson's card does.
C is wrong: storage is optional in persist's types: tsc and the linter are silent.
D is wrong: Nothing names AsyncStorage here; that is the point: nobody told persist about it.
Q3
Why does the queue save its state with partialize: ({ logs }) => ({ logs }) instead of everything?
A) So sending always starts as false: an app killed during a send would otherwise never send again
B) Because AsyncStorage cannot store booleans
C) To make the saved JSON smaller than 1 KB
D) Because persist cannot save functions without it
Why: sending means "a send is running right now"; it must end with the app. Saved, it would come back true, and the sender's first guard (if (queue.sending) return) would stop every future send. persist already skips the action functions.
B is wrong: JSON holds booleans; AsyncStorage stores the string.
C is wrong: A log is well under a kilobyte; size is not the reason.
D is wrong: Functions are dropped by JSON anyway; partialize is about sending.
Q4
Theo's first POST reached shoreline-api and was saved, but the answer never reached the phone. The queue sends the same log again with the same clientId. What does the API answer? (The kit ran it against the course API.)
A) 201 and a second saved log
B) 409, so the log is marked refused
C) 400: the clientId is already used
D) 200 with the log it already has, and the server holds one log
Why: The kit sent one log twice: Theo, first try: 201, then Theo, same clientId again: 200, same log: true, and Theo's logs with this clientId on the server: 1. The device-made clientId (Lesson 5.4) is what makes "no answer: send again" safe.
A is wrong: The API looks the clientId up first: no duplicate.
B is wrong: 409 is "this cleanup has not happened yet", not a resend.
C is wrong: A resend is expected; the API answers it with the saved log.
Q5
Sort these answers to a queued log into what the queue does: 201, 200, 403, 401, no answer, 503.
201 and 200 = done (logbook, then out of the queue); 403 = refused, kept with the server's words, never sent again by itself; 401 = keep everything, "Your sign-in has ended", stop; no answer and 503 = keep waiting, count the try, stop for now
Why: The question for every failure: would asking again, unchanged, ever get a different answer? A dead network or a busy server: yes, later. A 403 "no spot": no. Treating them the same either loses data or retries forever (Common Mistakes).
Q6
A screen selects useLogQueue((state) => state.logs.filter((item) => item.volunteerId === viewer)). What happens, and what is the fix?
A) It works: Zustand compares arrays by their items
B) tsc reports that filter returns a new array
C) It renders forever (Maximum update depth exceeded): wrap the selector in useShallow
D) It shows every volunteer's logs: add a second filter
Why: filter builds a new array every time Zustand asks, so Zustand sees a new answer, renders, asks again... The lesson measured a blank page and React error #185 in the web preview; Lab 5.5's Task 3 fails in Jest with the same words. useShallow compares the items; a selector that returns a number (the tab badge's .length) needs nothing.
A is wrong: That is what useShallow adds; plain selectors compare with Object.is.
B is wrong: Types are fine: the bug is at run time.
D is wrong: The filter by volunteerId is right; the new array each call is the problem.

5.5 · Offline First: predict, then check

P1
The same debris log is sent to shoreline-api four times: as Theo (who has a spot), as Theo again with the same clientId (the first answer was lost), as Amara (no spot at this cleanup), and with no sign-in. Predict each status code and what the queue would do with it.
// One debris log, sent the ways a queue meets: what does shoreline-api answer each time?
const api = process.env.API_URL ?? 'http://localhost:4000';
const log = {
  clientId: 'c3a0f6b2-5d1e-4c7a-9b8f-2e6d1a4c9f70', // made on the phone, saved with the log
  items: [{ category: 'glass', count: 2 }],
  bags: 1,
  weightKg: 1.5,
  recordedAt: new Date().toISOString(),
};

async function send(token) {
  const response = await fetch(`${api}/api/events/e17/logs`, {
    method: 'POST',
    headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json', ...(token && { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` }) },
    body: JSON.stringify(log),
  });
  return { status: response.status, body: await response.json() };
}

const first = await send('dev-volunteer'); // Theo, who has a spot
console.log(`Theo, first try: ${first.status}`);
const again = await send('dev-volunteer'); // the answer was lost: the queue sends it again
console.log(`Theo, same clientId again: ${again.status}, same log: ${again.body.id === first.body.id}`);

const signIn = await fetch(`${api}/api/auth/sign-in`, {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
  body: JSON.stringify({ email: 'amara.diallo@example.com', password: 'shoreline-demo' }),
});
const amara = await send((await signIn.json()).token); // Amara has no spot at this cleanup
console.log(`Amara: ${amara.status} ${amara.body.message}`);
const nobody = await send(null); // a sign-in that has ended
console.log(`No sign-in: ${nobody.status} ${nobody.body.message}`);

const logs = await fetch(`${api}/api/events/e17/logs`, { headers: { Authorization: 'Bearer dev-volunteer' } });
const saved = (await logs.json()).filter((item) => item.clientId === log.clientId);
console.log(`Theo's logs with this clientId on the server: ${saved.length}`);
Theo, first try: 201
Theo, same clientId again: 200, same log: true
Amara: 403 Only volunteers with a spot at this cleanup can log for it.
No sign-in: 401 Sign in first.
Theo's logs with this clientId on the server: 1
Why: Run against the course API (a private copy, sample data built around one fixed day). 201: a new log. 200 and the same log: the server already had this clientId (the queue then removes it: done). 403: refused, kept with these exact words, never sent again by itself. 401: keep everything, show that the sign-in has ended, stop. And the server holds ONE log from Theo, whatever the queue resent.

6.1 · Permissions Done Right: self-check

Q7
A "Photo of the haul" feature needs the camera. Where should the app call requestCameraPermissionsAsync()?
A) In the root layout's useEffect, so it is out of the way
B) Right after the volunteer signs in
C) In a useEffect of the photo screen, the moment it appears
D) From Continue, on a one-sentence explanation shown after the volunteer presses Take a photo
Why: Ask in context, explain first: the press is the moment the question answers itself, and the explanation's button leads to the phone's own question (Continue, not "Allow"). On an iPhone a "Don't Allow" given to a question nobody understood is final.
A is wrong: Asking at launch: the lesson measured a request on the Cleanups list before anyone pressed anything.
B is wrong: Still no context: nothing the volunteer did needs the camera.
C is wrong: Mounting is not a press: still asking at the door, with no reason.
Q8
On an Android phone, the volunteer tapped "Don't allow" once. The Hook's answer is { status: 'denied', canAskAgain: true, granted: false }. Which step does permissionStep return, and what does the gate show?
A) 'blocked': Open Settings
B) 'denied': "that is fine", the reason again, and Ask me again
C) 'not-asked': the explanation with Continue
D) 'checking': Checking…
Why: Android allows one more question after the first no; canAskAgain: true says so. After a second no (or on an iPhone after the first) canAskAgain is false: 'blocked'. In the web preview a no is always 'blocked' (the web answer always says canAskAgain: true).
A is wrong: Only when canAskAgain is false (or on the web).
C is wrong: That is status 'undetermined'.
D is wrong: That is a null answer, while the Hook reads.
Q9
npx tsc --noEmit prints this in shoreline-crew after an edit to DistancePanel.tsx. Which change fixes it?
src/components/DistancePanel.tsx:43:34 - error TS2345: Argument of type '(options?: PermissionHookOptions<object> | undefined) => [LocationPermissionResponse | null, () => Promise<LocationPermissionResponse>, () => Promise<...>]' is not assignable to parameter of type 'PermissionHookResult'.

43   const location = usePermission(Location.useForegroundPermissions);
                                    ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

  src/components/DistancePanel.tsx:43:34
    43   const location = usePermission(Location.useForegroundPermissions);
                                        ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
    Did you mean to call this expression?


Found 1 error in src/components/DistancePanel.tsx:43
A) Call the Hook: usePermission(Location.useForegroundPermissions())
B) Widen PermissionHookResult to accept a function
C) Add as PermissionHookResult after the argument
D) Run npx expo install expo-location again
Why: Read it from the bottom: "Did you mean to call this expression?" is the whole answer. The long type in the first line is the Hook itself (a function that takes options and returns the tuple); usePermission takes what the Hook RETURNS. The kit's run is the lesson's card, word for word.
B is wrong: Then the function would reach the destructuring and fail at run time (Lab 6.1: "Invalid attempt to destructure non-iterable instance").
C is wrong: A cast silences the checker and keeps the bug.
D is wrong: The package is installed; the call is missing.
Q10
You remove the whole expo-location entry from app.json's plugins and run npx expo config --type introspect. What do the iPhone's location strings look like? (The kit ran it.)
A) There are none: no entry, no plugin
B) Only Shoreline Crew's own sentence
C) Five location and motion strings in the plugin's default words, Always location included
D) The command fails: the plugin is missing
Why: expo-location's plugin is applied automatically when the package is installed, with default words ("Allow $(PRODUCT_NAME) to access your location", Always, motion, temporary precise location). That is why npx expo install adds no entry: the entry exists only to pass your words and false for what you do not use.
A is wrong: The plugin still runs: it is on Expo's list of auto-applied plugins.
B is wrong: That is WITH the entry.
D is wrong: It runs and prints the config; nothing is missing.
Q11
Name two things Expo's permission Hook does NOT do for you, and how usePermission or the gate covers each.
It never asks by itself (unless { request: true }): the gate's Continue asks. It never reads the answer again after Settings: usePermission calls check() when focusManager says the app is back in the foreground. (Also: on the web canAskAgain is always true: permissionStep treats a web no as blocked.)
Why: The lesson read PermissionsHook.ts (about 80 lines): it reads once on mount and after each request. Lab 6.1 Task 3 fails until the focus subscription is there.
Q12
Which of these needs NO runtime permission on a phone?
A) The share sheet (Lesson 4.4)
B) The camera
C) Notifications on an iPhone
D) The phone's location
Why: Network requests, the app's own storage, the share sheet and haptics need none; the photo picker generally none either. The camera and location do; notifications do on iPhone and recent Android (Lesson 6.4).
B is wrong: The camera needs one (Lesson 6.2 puts it through the same gate).
C is wrong: iPhone asks before an app may notify.
D is wrong: Location is today's permission.

6.1 · Permissions Done Right: predict, then check

P2

Demo Scripts (for the projector)

The two scripts of the 5.5 demo, exactly as the kit ran them (Node 24). Save them in the copy of the finished Lesson 5.5 code (lesson-19-end, with npm ci done) and run them from its shoreline-crew folder with node no-storage.mjs and node answers.mjs; the second needs the API running at http://localhost:4000 (or the address in API_URL). The 401 line needs nobody signed in: it sends no token at all.

no-storage.mjs (the lesson's Common Mistakes measurement)
// The web course's persisted store, copied as it was: no storage option
globalThis.window = {}; // a window without localStorage, as on a phone
const { create } = await import('zustand');
const { persist } = await import('zustand/middleware');

const useLogQueue = create()(
  persist((set) => ({ logs: [], add: (log) => set(({ logs }) => ({ logs: [...logs, log] })) }), {
    name: 'shoreline-crew-log-queue',
  }),
);
try {
  useLogQueue.getState().add({ clientId: 'c3a0f6b2' });
  console.log('add worked');
} catch (error) {
  console.log('add threw: ' + error);
}
add threw: TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'setItem')
answers.mjs (one log, sent four ways)
// One debris log, sent the ways a queue meets: what does shoreline-api answer each time?
const api = process.env.API_URL ?? 'http://localhost:4000';
const log = {
  clientId: 'c3a0f6b2-5d1e-4c7a-9b8f-2e6d1a4c9f70', // made on the phone, saved with the log
  items: [{ category: 'glass', count: 2 }],
  bags: 1,
  weightKg: 1.5,
  recordedAt: new Date().toISOString(),
};

async function send(token) {
  const response = await fetch(`${api}/api/events/e17/logs`, {
    method: 'POST',
    headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json', ...(token && { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` }) },
    body: JSON.stringify(log),
  });
  return { status: response.status, body: await response.json() };
}

const first = await send('dev-volunteer'); // Theo, who has a spot
console.log(`Theo, first try: ${first.status}`);
const again = await send('dev-volunteer'); // the answer was lost: the queue sends it again
console.log(`Theo, same clientId again: ${again.status}, same log: ${again.body.id === first.body.id}`);

const signIn = await fetch(`${api}/api/auth/sign-in`, {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
  body: JSON.stringify({ email: 'amara.diallo@example.com', password: 'shoreline-demo' }),
});
const amara = await send((await signIn.json()).token); // Amara has no spot at this cleanup
console.log(`Amara: ${amara.status} ${amara.body.message}`);
const nobody = await send(null); // a sign-in that has ended
console.log(`No sign-in: ${nobody.status} ${nobody.body.message}`);

const logs = await fetch(`${api}/api/events/e17/logs`, { headers: { Authorization: 'Bearer dev-volunteer' } });
const saved = (await logs.json()).filter((item) => item.clientId === log.clientId);
console.log(`Theo's logs with this clientId on the server: ${saved.length}`);
Theo, first try: 201
Theo, same clientId again: 200, same log: true
Amara: 403 Only volunteers with a spot at this cleanup can log for it.
No sign-in: 401 Sign in first.
Theo's logs with this clientId on the server: 1

What each task shows: (1) the store needs storage: createJSONStorage(() => AsyncStorage) and partialize: ({ reports }) => ({ reports }); the saved JSON is { state: { reports }, version: 0 }, and merge drops a saved item in an old shape; (2) the catch block sorts: ApiError 401 → onSignInEnded() and break; other ApiError under 500 → change(clientId, { status: 'refused', problem: error.message }) and go on; anything else → attempts + 1 and break. Watch for status >= 400 (swallows the 401) and for break after a refusal (the next report never goes); (3) useShallow around the filtered selector; the remove action is a plain selector (a function stays the same).

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 5.5 · Offline First
  1. B The queue is read back at startup and the log is sent right away: Send saved the log in the queue, which persist wrote to AsyncStorage at once. At startup the root layout reads the queue back behind the splash screen, and the Hook sends the signed-in volunteer's waiting logs as soon as everything is loaded.
  2. D 200 with the log the server already has, because the clientId is the same: The clientId was made once and saved with the log, so the second try carries the same one. shoreline-api finds the log it already has for that volunteer and clientId and answers 200 with it. The queue treats 200 like 201: into the logbook, out of the queue, one log on the server.
  3. A Mark it refused, keep the server's message, and stop sending it by itself: Asking again, unchanged, would get the same answer, so retrying only wastes battery and hides the problem. Deleting it silently loses the volunteer's work. Keeping it as refused, with the server's words and a Remove button, tells the volunteer and lets them decide.
  4. C It waits, unseen by Maya, until Theo signs in on this phone again: The server records the log for whoever's token sends it, so sending Theo's log with Maya's sign-in makes it Maya's (measured). The queue sends only the signed-in volunteer's logs, Maya's Logbook shows none of Theo's, and his log goes out the next time he signs in on this phone.
  5. B The first change fails, because a phone has no localStorage: persist defaults to window.localStorage. The web preview has it; a phone has a window without localStorage, so saving fails on the first change (measured in Node in the same situation: Cannot read properties of undefined, reading setItem). Name the storage: createJSONStorage with AsyncStorage.
Lesson 6.1 · Permissions Done Right
  1. C After the volunteer presses Show my distance and reads the one-sentence reason: Asked in context, the question explains itself, and the panel's one-sentence reason comes first. Asked at launch or at sign-in, nobody knows why, and on an iPhone a no given then is final unless the volunteer digs into Settings.
  2. A The blocked face, with Open Settings: On an iPhone one Don't Allow sets canAskAgain to false, so permissionStep returns blocked as soon as the Hook reads the answer. A request would return the same no without showing anything, so the panel offers Open Settings instead.
  3. D Expo Go is its own app with its own purpose strings; yours appear in your own build: The purpose string lives in the app's Info.plist, which config plugins write when your own app is built. Expo Go is a different app with its own Info.plist and words, used for every project. Your string appears in a development build or a store build.
  4. B usePermission reads it again when the focus manager says the app is back in the foreground: Expo's Hook reads once when it mounts and after each request. usePermission subscribes to the focus manager, which AppState tells when the app is back in the foreground, and calls check() then. Without it (measured), the panel kept saying no.
  5. A The web answer always says true, but a browser never asks again after a block, so the Hook does not trust it there: expo-location's web code answers canAskAgain true for every answer, but a browser told Never allow never asks again: a request returns the no at once. Treating the web's no as blocked shows the site-settings note instead of an Ask me again button that could do nothing (measured).

The Lessons' Exercises: the Kit's Runs

  • 5.5 Exercise 1 (predict the queue): the lesson's solution was measured in the web preview; the kit's answers.mjs shows the server's side of cases 2 and 3 (200 with the saved log, the 403 message).
  • 6.1 Exercise 2 (Face ID string): the entry ["expo-secure-store", { "faceIDPermission": false }] and introspect give exactly the lesson's one line (Predict P2's explanation above).
  • 6.1 Exercise 3 (introspect with and without the entry): both runs above; each equals the lesson's card line for line (the closing }, of the dictionary aside).

Exit Ticket

  1. The two rules: save the log on the phone before anything is sent; remove it from the queue only after the server has it AND the logbook has a copy. Between the two steps, a crash means "send again", which the clientId makes harmless.
  2. A 403: mark it refused, keep the server's words ("Only volunteers with a spot at this cleanup can log for it."), stop sending it by itself, show it with Remove. Asking again unchanged gets the same answer: retrying drains the battery and hides the problem.
  3. Continue: the real Allow is the phone's own question, which Continue leads to (and Apple asks that a screen before it does not imitate it); Not now asks nothing. After an iPhone's first "Don't Allow" the phone never asks again: canAskAgain false, the blocked face, Open Settings (documentation-based: the course could not run a phone).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A or B. Confuses Lesson 5.4's paused mutation with today's queue. Draw the two columns again: memory versus AsyncStorage.
  • Q2 A or C. Expects the type checker or the web preview to catch a missing storage. Show the demo's two runs: tsc silent, the TypeError.
  • Q4 A or C. Has not connected the clientId to the server's lookup. Show the second line of answers.mjs.
  • Q7 C. Thinks mounting a screen is "in context". Ask what the volunteer pressed.
  • Q8 A. Treats every no as final (the iPhone rule) on Android. Back to the platform table.
  • Q10 A. Believes a plugin runs only when listed. Show the introspect run without the entry.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 15: Camera, Photos, Location and Maps

Answers and reasoning for every Session 15 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the demo scripts, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 15 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Warm-up

  1. W1: the blocked face with Open Settings: an iPhone asks once, and one Don't Allow is final (Lesson 6.1; documentation-based). He can still choose photos: the library needs no permission, so a photo taken with the phone's camera app can go on the log (tonight, Lesson 6.2).
  2. W2: the photos, each answered with an id; then the log listing those ids. The API refuses a log that names a photo it has never seen ("Upload each photo first and send the ids it returned."): bags first, then the tally sheet.
  3. W3: usually a few megabytes each, some bigger. Over one bar of signal that is slow at best, and over 5 MB the API refuses it (413). Tonight: quality: 0.6, no EXIF, JPEG, checked when added.

6.2 · Camera and Photos: self-check

Q1
On his iPhone Theo answered "Don't Allow" to Shoreline Crew's camera question. Which photo feature still works for him, and why?
A) None: photos need the camera permission
B) Take a photo, once he presses Continue again
C) Choose photos: the photo library needs no permission, so a picture he took with the camera app can still go on the log
D) Only uploading photos he chose before
Why: launchImageLibraryAsync opens the system picker, which runs outside the app: no permission on current iPhones and Android phones. That is Lesson 6.1's "a no is an answer": Not now brings the buttons back, and Choose photos is the other way round. (Documentation-based: the course could not run a phone.)
A is wrong: Only the camera needs a yes.
B is wrong: On an iPhone one no is final: the blocked face, Open Settings.
D is wrong: Uploading needs no permission at all; and nothing was uploaded when chosen.
Q2
The classic React Native upload from older answers, written in postPhoto. npx tsc --noEmit prints this (the kit ran it). What is the fix?
src/api/client.ts:452:8 - error TS2769: No overload matches this call.
  Overload 1 of 3, '(name: string, value: string | Blob): void', gave the following error.
    Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'uri' does not exist in type 'Blob'.
  Overload 2 of 3, '(name: string, value: string): void', gave the following error.
    Argument of type '{ uri: string; name: string; type: string; }' is not assignable to parameter of type 'string'.
  Overload 3 of 3, '(name: string, blobValue: Blob, filename?: string | undefined): void', gave the following error.
    Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'uri' does not exist in type 'Blob'.

452   body.append('photo', { uri: photo.uri, name: 'photo.jpg', type: 'image/jpeg' });
           ~~~~~~
A) Silence it: as unknown as Blob
B) Send the photo itself as the body (an expo-file-system File is a Blob), with its kind in Content-Type
C) Install a FormData polyfill
D) Set Content-Type: multipart/form-data by hand
Why: Read the overloads: append takes a string or a Blob, and { uri, name, type } is neither. SDK 58's fetch is Expo's own (expo/fetch) and sends Blobs, not file addresses. postPhoto sends the API's second form: the image as the body, Content-Type: image/jpeg.
A is wrong: The error is right: with the cast, Expo's fetch fails at run time ("Unsupported FormDataPart implementation").
C is wrong: FormData exists; the part is the problem.
D is wrong: The body is still not bytes; and a hand-written multipart type has no boundary.
Q3
Someone adds the cast and ships the old FormData part. What happens on a phone when the queue sends the log?
A) The photo is uploaded as before
B) shoreline-api answers 415
C) tsc catches it at the next build
D) Expo's fetch throws "Unsupported FormDataPart implementation" inside itself: the client reports a NetworkError, and the queue counts a try and waits for a signal that is already there
Why: The kit ran Expo's own convertFormDataAsync (copied from node_modules/expo, as the lesson did): old way: Error: Unsupported FormDataPart implementation, while a Blob part converts. The error never reaches the server, so it looks like no signal. The lesson's advice: when only uploads "cannot reach the server", suspect the body.
A is wrong: Not with SDK 58's fetch: it only turns text and Blobs into bytes.
B is wrong: Nothing reaches the server.
C is wrong: The cast silences tsc for good.
Q4
The kit sent one real JPEG to POST /api/photos labeled image/heic, as an iPhone photo can arrive without the picker's "compatible" option. What did shoreline-api answer, and what does the queue do with the log?
A) 415 "Photos must be JPEG, PNG or WebP images.": refused with the server's words, never sent again by itself
B) 201: the API converts HEIC
C) 413: HEIC files are too big
D) 500: try again later
Why: A 415 (and a 413 for a 9 MB PNG: "Photos must be 5 MB or smaller.") will not change on the next try, so it lands in the sender's 4xx branch, like Lesson 5.5's 403. That is why LogPhotos checks the type and size when a photo is ADDED, while the volunteer can still choose another.
B is wrong: The API takes JPEG, PNG and WebP only.
C is wrong: The bytes were 28,955: size was not the problem.
D is wrong: Nothing went wrong on the server; the answer is final.
Q5
Two decisions in Lesson 6.2's queue: why does each photo move from the cache into the app's documents, and why is each photo's id saved with setPhotoId the moment it arrives?
The phone may empty the cache when storage runs low, and a log can wait for days: in documents its photos survive until the server has them (then forgetPhoto deletes them). Photos have no clientId: shoreline-api stores every upload as a new photo, so if the photos went up and the log's answer was lost, the next try must send the log with the saved ids instead of uploading again.
Why: The four questions of "For your own app": how big, where it waits, when it goes, what a retry may repeat. Lab 6.2 Task 2 has a test for the lost answer: two uploads in all, the item sent twice with the same ids.
Q6
Lesson 6.2 adds photos to every queued log. Theo updates the app with two logs saved yesterday, without a photos list. What keeps them?
A) Nothing is needed: merge keeps everything
B) version: 1 and a migrate that gives each version-0 log photos: [] before the check
C) Clearing AsyncStorage at startup
D) skipHydration: true
Why: persist saves a version number with the data and calls migrate when the saved version is older. Without it, Lesson 5.5's merge check (which now requires photos) throws yesterday's logs away. Lab 6.2's Task 3 fails exactly that way until migrate is there.
A is wrong: merge checks the shape: logs without photos fail it.
C is wrong: That throws the logs away on purpose.
D is wrong: That decides WHEN the queue is read back, not what survives.

6.2 · Camera and Photos: predict, then check

P1
One real JPEG (28,955 bytes) is sent to POST /api/photos twice more: labeled image/heic, and then a 9 MB PNG. Predict each status code and message, and what the queue would do with a log that carried each photo.
// What shoreline-api answers to a HEIC photo and to a 9 MB PNG
const api = process.env.API_URL ?? 'http://localhost:4000';
async function send(label, body, type) {
  const response = await fetch(`${api}/api/photos`, {
    method: 'POST',
    headers: { Authorization: 'Bearer dev-volunteer', 'Content-Type': type },
    body,
  });
  console.log(`# ${label}`);
  console.log(`HTTP/1.1 ${response.status} ${response.statusText}`);
  console.log(JSON.stringify(await response.json(), null, 2));
}
const jpeg = await (await fetch(`${api}/images/sites/otter-creek.jpg`)).blob();
await send('Content-Type: image/heic', jpeg, 'image/heic');
console.log('');
await send('a 9 MB PNG', new Blob([new Uint8Array(9 * 1024 * 1024)]), 'image/png');
# Content-Type: image/heic
HTTP/1.1 415 Unsupported Media Type
{
  "error": "unsupported_media_type",
  "message": "Photos must be JPEG, PNG or WebP images."
}

# a 9 MB PNG
HTTP/1.1 413 Payload Too Large
{
  "error": "payload_too_large",
  "message": "Photos must be 5 MB or smaller."
}
Why: Run against the course API (a private copy, sample data built around one fixed day): the lesson card of Common Mistakes, line for line (its ... lines aside). The bytes were a real JPEG of 28,955 bytes: only the label changed, so the API refuses by Content-Type. Both answers are final for that photo, and in the queue they make the whole log refused, like Lesson 5.5's 403. The same photo labeled image/jpeg gets 201 (the demo's first script).

6.3 · Location and Maps: self-check

Q7
Theo checks in at today's cleanup with the right code; the answer is lost and he presses Check in again. What does shoreline-api answer the second time? (The kit ran it.)
A) 200, still attended, with the same checkedInAt: a retry is safe
B) 409: already checked in
C) 400: the code was used
D) 201 and a second sign-up
Why: The kit's run: Theo at e17 with 6544: 200, status attended, then Again: 200, status attended, same checkedInAt: true. The check-in is idempotent, which is why a paused mutation may simply be sent again when the phone is back online.
B is wrong: 409s are the wrong day and no confirmed spot.
C is wrong: The code is the day's, not single-use.
D is wrong: The check-in changes the existing sign-up to attended.
Q8
Why does Shoreline Crew NOT put check-ins in Lesson 5.5's saved queue?
A) A check-in is too big for AsyncStorage
B) The API has no clientId for check-ins
C) TanStack Query cannot pause a mutation
D) A late check-in can never work (only on the day), a wrong code needs the volunteer now, and the organizer needs to see it now
Why: Offline, the check-in is a paused mutation in memory with the honest line "No connection: your check-in is sent when you are back online. Keep Shoreline Crew open until then." If the app closes, the volunteer checks in again or the organizer marks them present in Shoreline Crew HQ. A debris log, by contrast, is welcome hours later.
A is wrong: It is four digits.
B is wrong: Idempotency comes from the API's rule (attended stays attended), not a clientId.
C is wrong: Pausing is exactly what it does offline (Lesson 5.2).
Q9
You created src/app/check-in/[id].tsx and linked to it while Metro was stopped. npx tsc --noEmit prints (among others) this. What is the fix?
src/app/check-in/[id].tsx:34:39 - error TS2344: Type '"/check-in/[id]"' does not satisfy the constraint 'RoutePath'.

34   const { id } = useLocalSearchParams<'/check-in/[id]'>();
A) Change the type parameter to RoutePath
B) Add the route to .expo/types/router.d.ts by hand
C) Start Metro (npx expo start): Expo Router rewrites the route list, then tsc prints nothing
D) Remove the type parameter from useLocalSearchParams
Why: The code is right; the list of routes is old. The kit ran it: three errors with Metro stopped (the lesson's card), then npx expo start, then npx tsc --noEmit printed nothing. router.d.ts is rewritten every time: never edit it.
A is wrong: '/check-in/[id]' IS the right route: it is just not in the list yet.
B is wrong: It is rewritten each time Metro runs.
D is wrong: That hides the error and loses the typed id.
Q10
An organizer suggests: "Only allow check-in within 300 m of the meeting point." Under the cliff at Otter Creek, Theo's phone reports accuracy: 400. What is the problem?
A) None: 300 m is generous
B) The circle is 400 m wide, so the rule could refuse someone standing on the right spot; and a check on the phone can be changed by whoever holds it
C) accuracy 400 means the phone is 400 m from the pin
D) Accuracy.Highest always fixes it
Why: A position is a circle: accuracy is its radius in meters. Phones report 50 to 500 m under cliffs and in creek valleys, and mocked or edited positions exist. Rules belong on the server; the code is the proof, the location a hint (Exercise 3, Lab 6.3 Task 1).
A is wrong: Not when the circle is wider than the rule.
C is wrong: It is the uncertainty, not the distance.
D is wrong: More accuracy costs time and battery and still depends on the sky; it does not change who controls the phone.
Q11
What does npx expo export --platform web do when a route imports react-native-maps with no .web.tsx stand-in, why the WHOLE web build, and what is the fix?
It stops: "Metro error: (0 , r(...).codegenNativeComponent) is not a function". react-native-maps defines native components when it is loaded, and react-native-web has no codegenNativeComponent. Expo Router's web build loads every route to render the pages on the server, so one import stops all of them. Fix: SiteMap.web.tsx next to SiteMap.tsx, same props, never importing react-native-maps.
Why: The kit removed SiteMap.web.tsx from the finished code and ran the export: the lesson's card, line for line (the bundle sizes aside). Lab 6.3's web check shows the same message for a plain app.
Q12
In the web preview, Open in Maps opens OpenStreetMap. Why does the button use React Native's Linking.openURL and not expo-linking's?
A) expo-linking cannot open https links
B) React Native's Linking adds the map provider
C) expo-linking needs a permission on the web
D) On the web, expo-linking's openURL replaces the page (leaving the app and, with the memory-only session, signing the volunteer out); React Native's opens a new tab
Why: Measured in the lesson. On a phone both hand the link to the system: maps.apple.com on an iPhone, a geo: link on Android (documentation-based). Platform.select in mapLink picks the link.
A is wrong: It opens them: in the same tab.
B is wrong: The link itself names the map.
C is wrong: Opening a link needs no permission.

6.3 · Location and Maps: predict, then check

P2
Four check-ins and two reads: Theo at a cleanup three days away; Amara (no spot) at today's; Theo at today's with 6544, twice; then today's cleanup as the organizer and as Theo. Predict each answer.
// The check-in's answers: the wrong day, no spot, the right code twice, and who sees the code
const api = process.env.API_URL ?? 'http://localhost:4000';
async function checkIn(token, eventId, code) {
  const response = await fetch(`${api}/api/events/${eventId}/check-in`, {
    method: 'POST',
    headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}`, 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
    body: JSON.stringify({ code }),
  });
  return { status: response.status, body: await response.json() };
}
const signIn = await fetch(`${api}/api/auth/sign-in`, {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
  body: JSON.stringify({ email: 'amara.diallo@example.com', password: 'shoreline-demo' }),
});
const amara = (await signIn.json()).token;

const early = await checkIn('dev-volunteer', 'e08', '6544'); // Theo, Weekend Cove Cleanup: in three days
console.log(`Theo at e08 (not today): ${early.status} ${early.body.message}`);
const noSpot = await checkIn(amara, 'e17', '6544'); // Amara has no spot at today's cleanup
console.log(`Amara at e17 (no spot): ${noSpot.status} ${noSpot.body.message}`);

const first = await checkIn('dev-volunteer', 'e17', '6544');
console.log(`Theo at e17 with 6544: ${first.status}, status ${first.body.status}`);
const again = await checkIn('dev-volunteer', 'e17', '6544'); // the answer was lost: he presses again
console.log(`Again: ${again.status}, status ${again.body.status}, same checkedInAt: ${again.body.checkedInAt === first.body.checkedInAt}`);

const asMaya = await (await fetch(`${api}/api/events/e17`, { headers: { Authorization: 'Bearer dev-organizer' } })).json();
const asTheo = await (await fetch(`${api}/api/events/e17`, { headers: { Authorization: 'Bearer dev-volunteer' } })).json();
console.log(`GET /api/events/e17 as Maya (organizer): checkInCode ${asMaya.checkInCode}`);
console.log(`GET /api/events/e17 as Theo: checkInCode ${'checkInCode' in asTheo ? asTheo.checkInCode : '(not sent)'}`);
Theo at e08 (not today): 409 Check-in opens on the day of the cleanup.
Amara at e17 (no spot): 409 You need a confirmed spot to check in.
Theo at e17 with 6544: 200, status attended
Again: 200, status attended, same checkedInAt: true
GET /api/events/e17 as Maya (organizer): checkInCode 6544
GET /api/events/e17 as Theo: checkInCode (not sent)
Why: The wrong day and no confirmed spot are 409s in the API's words (the modal says them before anyone types). The right code twice is 200 both times, and the second keeps the first time: idempotent, so a paused mutation may simply be sent again. Only an organizer's sign-in gets the code. And with a wrong code, the 400 names the field:
HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request
cache-control: no-store
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "validation_failed",
  "message": "Some fields need attention.",
  "fieldErrors": {
    "code": [
      "That code does not match. Ask the organizer for today's code."
    ]
  }
}

Demo Scripts (for the projector)

The scripts of the two demos, exactly as the kit ran them (Node 24). Save the first three in the copy of the finished Lesson 6.2 code (lesson-21-end, with npm ci done) and run them from its shoreline-crew folder; formdata.mjs reads Expo's own code from that folder's node_modules and needs no API. Save checkins.mjs in the Lesson 6.3 copy. The others need the API running at http://localhost:4000 (or the address in API_URL); restart the API before the session, so Theo's check-in at today's cleanup is not already done (or curl -X POST http://localhost:4000/api/_reset, as the lesson says).

photo.mjs (one real photo, the way postPhoto sends it)
// A real JPEG (one of the API's own site pictures), sent the way postPhoto sends it: the photo is the body
const api = process.env.API_URL ?? 'http://localhost:4000';
const theo = { Authorization: 'Bearer dev-volunteer' };
const jpeg = await (await fetch(`${api}/images/sites/otter-creek.jpg`)).blob();

const upload = await fetch(`${api}/api/photos`, {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: { ...theo, 'Content-Type': 'image/jpeg' },
  body: jpeg,
});
const photo = await upload.json();
console.log(`POST /api/photos (${jpeg.size} bytes): ${upload.status} ${upload.statusText}`);
console.log(`  the answer's fields: ${Object.keys(photo).join(', ')}`);
console.log(`  contentType ${photo.contentType}, bytes ${photo.bytes}, uploadedBy ${photo.uploadedBy}`);

const back = await fetch(photo.url); // no sign-in needed
console.log(`GET its url: ${back.status}, ${back.headers.get('content-type')}, ${(await back.arrayBuffer()).byteLength} bytes`);

// The tally sheet names the bag: a debris log for today's cleanup, listing the photo's id
const log = await fetch(`${api}/api/events/e17/logs`, {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: { ...theo, 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
  body: JSON.stringify({
    clientId: '6f1d2c3b-8a47-4e0b-9b1e-5c2a7d9e4f10',
    items: [{ category: 'fishing-gear', count: 3 }],
    bags: 1,
    weightKg: 2.5,
    recordedAt: new Date().toISOString(),

  }),
});
const saved = await log.json();
console.log(`POST /api/events/e17/logs with photoIds [its id]: ${log.status}, photoIds kept: ${saved.photoIds?.[0] === photo.id}`);

formdata.mjs (Expo's own fetch code on the old FormData part: the lesson's measurement)
// Expo's own code from node_modules: SDK 58's fetch turns a FormData into bytes with convertFormDataAsync
import { readFileSync, writeFileSync } from 'node:fs';
const build = 'node_modules/expo/build';
writeFileSync('blobUtils.mjs', readFileSync(`${build}/utils/blobUtils.js`, 'utf8'));
writeFileSync('convertFormData.mjs', readFileSync(`${build}/winter/fetch/convertFormData.js`, 'utf8')
  .replace('../../utils/blobUtils', './blobUtils.mjs'));
const { convertFormDataAsync } = await import('./convertFormData.mjs');

function rnFormData() { // React Native's FormData keeps each part as it was appended
  const parts = [];
  return { append: (name, value) => parts.push([name, value]), entries: function* () { yield* parts; } };
}
const old = rnFormData();
old.append('photo', { uri: 'file:///data/user/0/host.exp.exponent/cache/ImagePicker/1234.jpeg', name: 'photo.jpg', type: 'image/jpeg' });
try {
  await convertFormDataAsync(old);
  console.log('old way: converted');
} catch (error) {
  console.log(`old way: ${error.name}: ${error.message}`);
}
const blobWay = rnFormData();
blobWay.append('photo', new Blob([new Uint8Array([0xff, 0xd8, 0xff])], { type: 'image/jpeg' }));
const { body } = await convertFormDataAsync(blobWay);
console.log(`a Blob part: converted, ${body.length} bytes`);
old way: Error: Unsupported FormDataPart implementation
a Blob part: converted, 171 bytes

What each task shows: (1) the file's kind is asset.mimeType; keep JPEG, PNG and WebP of 5 MB or less, slice(0, room), and one sentence counting the refused; (2) postPhoto(photo: Blob, contentType) sends the Blob as the body with Content-Type; uploadPhotos reads each file (readPhoto), skips a gone one, saves each id with setPhotoId before the next, reuses saved ids; after the item forgetPhoto each file; a 415 marks the item refused with the server's words; (3) version: 1 and migrate: (saved, version) => ({ items: ... .map(old => version === 0 ? { ...old, photos: [] } : old).filter(isQueuedItem) }). Watch for ids saved only after the item went (the lost-answer test then counts four uploads).

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 6.2 · Camera and Photos
  1. B Take a photo, which opens the camera: The camera needs the camera permission, so Take a photo goes through usePermission and PermissionGate. The library picker needs no permission on current phones, and uploading or showing your own files needs none either.
  2. D The log waits in the queue with its photos' file addresses; back online, the photos are uploaded first, then the log with their ids: The log and its photos' file addresses go into the queue, and the files wait in the app's documents. When the phone is back online, the sender uploads each photo, saves its id, then sends the log with those ids.
  3. A The phone may empty the cache when storage runs low, and a log can wait a long time to be sent: The phone may empty an app's cache when storage runs low, and a queued log can wait days for a signal. Documents are left alone, and Shoreline Crew deletes each photo itself once the server has it.
  4. C The photo's bytes as the body (an expo-file-system File is a Blob), with Content-Type such as image/jpeg: The body is the photo itself: an expo-file-system File, which is a Blob, with Content-Type set to the type the picker reported. shoreline-api accepts a raw image body as well as a multipart form, and SDK 58's fetch sends Blobs.
  5. A It sends the log again with the two saved photo ids, without uploading the photos again: setPhotoId saved each id in the queue the moment it arrived, so uploadPhotos skips both uploads and the log goes with the saved ids. The log's clientId then makes a resend safe, as in Lesson 5.5: the API answers 200 with the log it already has.
Lesson 6.3 · Location and Maps
  1. B The server checks the code; a position comes from the phone, can be faked or far off, and needs a yes to location: The server checks the code, and the app cannot change its answer. A position comes from the phone, can be faked by whoever holds it, can be hundreds of meters off under a cliff, and would lock out volunteers who said no to location.
  2. D It is gone: he checks in again, or the organizer marks him present: A paused mutation lives in memory only. Closing the app loses it, which the screen warned about. Theo checks in again when he has signal, or the organizer marks him present in Shoreline Crew HQ. Retrying is safe: the API's check-in is idempotent.
  3. A A SiteMap.web.tsx next to SiteMap.tsx that never imports react-native-maps: react-native-maps defines native components when it is imported, and the web has none. A SiteMap.web.tsx with the same props that never imports the package is what the web bundle uses instead; the phone keeps SiteMap.tsx.
  4. C The build has no Google Maps API key of its own (react-native-maps' androidGoogleMapsApiKey): Expo Go carries its own Google Maps key. Your own Android build needs yours, from a Google Cloud project, added with react-native-maps' config plugin (androidGoogleMapsApiKey) and restricted to your app.
  5. B On an iPhone, the prop alone makes the phone ask for location, with no reason given first: react-native-maps documents that showsUserLocation makes iOS ask for location. Always on, the phone would ask the moment the screen opens, with no explanation, and a Don't Allow there is final on an iPhone. Reading the answer asks nobody, so the dot appears only after an earlier yes.

The Lessons' Exercises: the Kit's Runs

  • 6.2 Exercise 1 (predict the requests): the lesson's solution was measured in the web preview; the kit's photo.mjs shows the server's side of case 1 (a photo up, then the log naming its id, 201) and refusals.mjs why case 4's PNG must never get into the log.
  • 6.2, Step 3's tsc: the kit's run is the lesson's TS2554 card, word for word (the form as Lesson 6.1 left it).
  • 6.3 Exercise 1 (predict the face): cases 1 and 2 are the API's 409 sentences, which the modal says before anyone types (checkins.mjs: "Check-in opens on the day of the cleanup." for Theo at e08); case 5 is the 400 above, under the field.
  • 6.3, the new route: three errors with Metro stopped (the lesson's card), then npx expo start and npx tsc --noEmit printed nothing (Self-check Q9).

Exit Ticket

  1. The order: each photo without an id is uploaded first (its id saved in the queue the moment it arrives), then the log with photoIds; then the logbook, then the files are deleted. The API refuses a log that names a photo it has never seen; a saved id means a retry never uploads twice.
  2. The code: the server checks it (the app cannot change the answer), it works under a cliff and after a no to location, and it proves the volunteer heard the organizer. A position comes from the phone (mock locations, edited code), can be hundreds of meters off, and needs a yes to location: a hint, never a rule.
  3. A stand-in: SiteMap.web.tsx next to SiteMap.tsx, with the same props, never importing react-native-maps; otherwise npx expo export --platform web stops with "codegenNativeComponent is not a function" (the web preview shows it full screen).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Thinks every photo needs the camera permission. Back to the two ways to a photo.
  • Q2 A. Trusts the online answers' cast. Show the demo's formdata.mjs run.
  • Q4 B or C. Expects the API to convert or misreads the size. Show refusals.mjs: same bytes, only the label changed.
  • Q8 A. Puts everything offline in the queue. Ask when a late check-in could ever succeed.
  • Q9 B. Edits generated files. Show the run: start Metro, then tsc prints nothing.
  • Q10 A or C. Reads accuracy as a distance. Draw the circle around the dot.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 16: Notifications, Haptics, Sharing and Files

Answers and reasoning for every Session 16 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the demo scripts, the lab reference outputs, the Part 2 kickoff reference, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 16 12 self-check items Confidential

Start Here · Warm-up

  1. W1: answers vary. Listen for the lesson's three: the good one speaks when it has something useful to say, at a sensible time, and can be turned off; the silenced one interrupted once too often.
  2. W2: blocked, with Open Settings: an iPhone asks once, so after one "Don't Allow" canAskAgain is false and only Settings can change it (Lesson 6.1). Tonight's notification permission goes through the same gate.
  3. W3: the debris-log queue: the same log sent again with the same clientId gets a 200 with the log the server already has. Tonight's updateReminders is safe to run twice in the same way: the same truth gives the same reminders, and the same identifier replaces.

6.4 · Notifications: self-check

Q1
At 7 am on a Saturday, an organizer cancels Weekend Cove Cleanup because of a storm warning. Can a local reminder tell the volunteers?
A) Yes: updateReminders cancels the reminder and the phone shows a cancellation
B) No: only the server knows, and when; that is a push notification, which needs a development build, an EAS project, credentials, push tokens and a server
C) Yes, if the app was opened that morning
D) No: local notifications cannot carry text
Why: A local reminder is the tide table on the dashboard: written ahead, from what the phone knew. News that only a server knows, at the moment it happens, is the harbor radio: push. Lesson 6.4's table lists what it would take; shoreline-api has none of it, and the course does not build it.
A is wrong: Cancelling a reminder makes the phone show NOTHING; it cannot announce anything by itself.
C is wrong: Opening the app would quietly cancel the reminder (the sign-up is gone); still no message unless the screen shows one.
D is wrong: Local notifications carry a title, a body and data, as the lesson's code shows.
Q2
Expo's example creates the Android channel with Notifications.AndroidImportance.DEFAULT. npx tsc --noEmit prints nothing; npx expo lint prints this (the kit ran it). What is the fix?
env: load .env.local
env: export EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL

~/shoreline/shoreline-crew/src/notifications/reminders.ts
  42:33  error  'AndroidImportance' not found in imported namespace 'Notifications'  import/namespace

✖ 1 problem (1 error, 0 warnings)
A) Import the enum by name: import { AndroidImportance } from 'expo-notifications';
B) Upgrade expo-notifications: AndroidImportance is missing from version 58
C) Add // eslint-disable-next-line above the line
D) Use the number 3 instead of the enum
Why: The enum is there (tsc finds it, the app runs); ESLint's import/namespace rule cannot follow it through the library's re-exports when it is reached as Notifications.AndroidImportance. A named import satisfies both. The kit's run is the lesson's card, word for word.
B is wrong: tsc found it: the name exists in the installed version.
C is wrong: That hides the message instead of reading it; the named import is cleaner and changes nothing at run time.
D is wrong: A magic number loses the meaning, and TypeScript would reject a plain number where the enum is expected.
Q3
An older tutorial writes trigger: reminderTime(event). What does npx tsc --noEmit say, and what is the fix? (The kit ran it on the finished code.)
src/notifications/reminders.ts:120:9 - error TS2322: Type 'Date' is not assignable to type 'NotificationTriggerInput'.

120         trigger: reminderTime(event),
            ~~~~~~~

  node_modules/expo-notifications/build/Notifications.types.d.ts:638:5
    638     trigger: NotificationTriggerInput;
            ~~~~~~~
    The expected type comes from property 'trigger' which is declared here on type 'NotificationRequestInput'


Found 1 error in src/notifications/reminders.ts:120
A) Nothing: a Date is a valid trigger
B) Wrap it: trigger: new Date(reminderTime(event))
C) An object with a type: { type: Notifications.SchedulableTriggerInputTypes.DATE, date: reminderTime(event) }
D) Cast it: reminderTime(event) as NotificationTriggerInput
Why: In expo-notifications 58 a trigger is an object whose type says what kind of time it is (DATE, TIME_INTERVAL, DAILY, WEEKLY). The first line says it all; the second part points at the declaration. Plain JavaScript would get through with a deprecation warning; TypeScript catches it first.
A is wrong: The run above is the TS2322 the lesson shows.
B is wrong: Still a Date.
D is wrong: A cast hides the mismatch and keeps the old call shape the library has deprecated.
Q4
Theo's phone holds reminders for three cleanups. updateReminders runs twice in a row with nothing changed. What happens the second time?
A) Three more reminders: six in all
B) All three are cancelled, then nothing is set
C) It throws, because the identifiers already exist
D) Nothing changes: none is unwanted, and scheduling reminder-<id> again replaces the same reminder
Why: Read the truth, compute what should exist, make it so: the same truth gives the same three ids, scheduling an id the phone holds replaces it, and nothing is cancelled. That is why the app can call it at start, sign-in, the switch, every return to the foreground and after every sign-up, without bookkeeping (Lesson 5.5's clientIds, again).
A is wrong: The identifier is the cleanup's: the same id replaces, it never adds.
B is wrong: Only Shoreline Crew's reminders that are no longer wanted are cancelled.
C is wrong: Same id = replace, never an error.
Q5
A tap on a reminder reaches the app in two ways. Name both, say when each applies, and say what the app does with the notification's data before it opens anything.
App running (foreground or background): addNotificationResponseReceivedListener hears the tap. App not running (the tap started it): getLastNotificationResponse(), read once when the app is ready, then cleared. Either way content.data.url is outside input: isCleanupPath checks it, and only a cleanup's path is ever pushed; a notification only opens a screen.
Why: Quiz Q3 on the lesson page asks the closed-app case. The guard matters because tomorrow's push could put anything in data: Lab 6.4's Task 3 tries a web address, a .. path and a number.
Q6
Replacing the guard with router.push(response.notification.request.content.data.url) gives this (the kit ran it). Why not silence it with as Href?
src/hooks/useReminderTaps.ts:18:19 - error TS18048: 'response.notification.request.content.data' is possibly 'undefined'.

18       router.push(response.notification.request.content.data.url);
                     ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

src/hooks/useReminderTaps.ts:18:19 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'unknown' is not assignable to parameter of type '`/cleanups/${string}` | ... 35 more ... | { ...; }'.

18       router.push(response.notification.request.content.data.url);
                     ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/hooks/useReminderTaps.ts:18
A) as Href would not compile either
B) Because the data is outside input: a cast opens whatever a future push or a bug put there, while a type guard turns unknown into a cleanup's path only after checking it
C) Because router.push cannot take a variable
D) Because data is always undefined on a phone
Why: Two errors, both right: data may be missing, and its values are unknown, while router.push wants one of the app's typed routes (Lesson 4.1). The type guard isCleanupPath is the API type guards' move since Lesson 1.3. (The long list of routes in the second error is shortened here.)
A is wrong: A cast compiles; that is the danger.
C is wrong: It takes any of the app's routes, from a variable too.
D is wrong: The reminders put { url } there; another notification might not.

6.5 · Haptics, Sharing and Files: self-check

Q7
Where does the sign-up's buzz go?
A) In onPress, before signUp.mutate(...), so it feels instant
B) In a useEffect that watches the button's pressed state
C) In the callbacks of this mutate call: onSuccess: () => feel('success'), onError: () => feel('error')
D) In the queue's sender, when the sign-up reaches the server
Why: Buzz for outcomes, not presses: the button's pressed look answers the press. Offline, the sign-up waits (Lesson 5.2); a buzz on the press would say "done" before anything is done, and lie if the API refuses. Callbacks passed to mutate run only while the panel is on screen, which is what a buzz needs.
A is wrong: The lesson's last Common Mistake: no tool catches it; it is a design error.
B is wrong: Still the press, not the result.
D is wrong: Sign-ups are not queued (Lesson 5.5), and a background send never buzzes: the volunteer may be driving.
Q8
The note is Bottles, cans and 2 "lost" flip-flops. followed by a second line, Café cups too. Which is the row logbookCsv writes for it? (The kit ran the finished code's logbookCsv in Node.)
A) Weekend Cove Cleanup,2026-11-14,63,2,4.1,Bottles, cans and 2 "lost" flip-flops. and the second line as a new row
B) "Weekend Cove Cleanup","2026-11-14","63","2","4.1","Bottles, cans and 2 \"lost\" flip-flops. Café cups too."
C) 'Weekend Cove Cleanup','2026-11-14',63,2,4.1,'Bottles, cans and 2 "lost" flip-flops.'
D) "Weekend Cove Cleanup","2026-11-14",63,2,4.1,"Bottles, cans and 2 ""lost"" flip-flops. with Café cups too." on the next line
Why: Text in double quotes with every inner quote doubled (RFC 4180); the line break stays inside the quotes, so the spreadsheet keeps one cell; numbers stay bare so they add up. The kit's run is the lesson's "Shoreline Crew's version" card, line for line. (The file also starts with a byte order mark and ends every row with \r\n.)
A is wrong: That is the naive join: 7 cells, then a 1-cell row.
B is wrong: Numbers are not quoted, and CSV escapes a quote by doubling it, not with a backslash.
C is wrong: CSV quotes with double quotes; a single quote is only the formula guard.
Q9
Sharing.shareAsync('https://rays-reactnative.netlify.app/', ...) in place of file.uri: npx tsc --noEmit prints nothing (the kit ran it). What happens on an Android phone?
A) expo-sharing refuses: only local file: addresses inside the app's own folders ("Only local file URLs are supported")
B) The share sheet opens with the link
C) The browser opens the page
D) The file is downloaded first, then shared
Why: The first argument is a string, so tsc is satisfied; the library's Android code refuses anything but a file: address in the app's folders (read in its source by the lesson; the course could not run a phone). Words and links go through React Native's Share.share (Lesson 4.4); files through expo-sharing.
B is wrong: That is Share.share's job.
C is wrong: Nothing opens a browser here.
D is wrong: expo-sharing downloads nothing.
Q10
const exportsFolder = new Directory(Paths.cache, 'exports'); moved to the top of logbookExport.ts. tsc prints nothing; expo lint prints only this (the kit ran both). What happens in the web preview?
env: load .env.local
env: export EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL
