✖ 1 problem (0 errors, 1 warning)
A) Nothing: the linter would have said so
B) The Logbook tab goes blank: importing the module reads Paths.cache, which throws on the web (this.validatePath is not a function)
C) Share my logbook shows the rows, as before
D) The file is written to the browser's downloads
Why: Nobody pressed anything: importing was enough. The linter only noticed the variable was unused; nothing names the real problem. The lesson measured the blank tab and Metro's call stack (read it from the bottom: ShareLogbook.tsx imported logbookExport.ts, whose line read Paths.cache). Create files and folders inside the functions that use them.
A is wrong: Neither tool sees platforms: the warning above is about an unused name.
C is wrong: The module never finishes loading.
D is wrong: The web has no files for the app; expo-file-system's web stand-in only warns and throws.
Q11
shareLogbook checks Platform.OS === 'web' BEFORE Sharing.isAvailableAsync(). Why is isAvailableAsync() alone not enough?
isAvailableAsync() answers "is there a share sheet?" (on the web: does the browser have navigator.share), not "can this app write a file?". Desktop Chrome on Windows and macOS and Safari have navigator.share, so it says yes, the app writes a file, and expo-file-system throws on the web: "Could not share your logbook". The platform check first returns 'unavailable' and the screen shows the rows instead.
Why: The lesson measured it by giving Chromium a navigator.share. Lab 6.5's Task 3 has a test for exactly that case.
Q12
Maya keeps Shoreline Crew's vibration switch ON, but has turned off System Haptics in her iPhone's Settings. She signs up for a cleanup. What does she get, according to Apple's documentation?
A) A buzz: the app's switch wins
B) An error message saying haptics are off
C) No buzz, and the words "You are signed up. See you on the beach!" as usual
D) A long vibration instead of the short tap
Why: Two layers of off: the in-app switch stops Shoreline Crew's buzzes on every platform; the phone's setting stops UIKit's feedback generators for every app (Apple's documentation: they do not play). The words never depend on either. The course could not feel a phone: Step 7 is where you check yours.
A is wrong: The phone's setting has the last word.
B is wrong: feel() swallows failures; nothing is shown, and nothing should be.
D is wrong: expo-haptics plays the generators' patterns or nothing; it never falls back to a long vibration.

Demo Scripts (for the projector)

The two Node scripts of the demos, exactly as the kit ran them (Node 24 strips the types and runs the file). Save reminder-time.ts in the copy of the finished Lesson 6.4 code and csv-demo.ts in the 6.5 copy, and run them from shoreline-crew with node reminder-time.ts and node csv-demo.ts. Both copy their functions from the finished code, so a learner can compare them line for line.

reminder-time.ts (reminderTime from src/notifications/reminders.ts)
// reminderTime from src/notifications/reminders.ts, run in Node with three cleanups
type CleanupEvent = { title: string; date: string };

/** When to remind: 6 pm the evening before the cleanup, by the phone's clock */
function reminderTime(event: CleanupEvent): Date {
  const time = new Date(`${event.date}T18:00`);
  time.setDate(time.getDate() - 1);
  return time;
}

const show = (d: Date) =>
  `${d.toDateString()} ${String(d.getHours()).padStart(2, '0')}:${String(d.getMinutes()).padStart(2, '0')}`;
for (const event of [
  { title: 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', date: '2026-12-05' },
  { title: 'First of the Month Sweep', date: '2026-12-01' },
  { title: "Harbor Steps Cleanup", date: '2027-01-01' },
]) {
  console.log(`${event.title} (${event.date}): remind at ${show(reminderTime(event))}`);
}
Weekend Cove Cleanup (2026-12-05): remind at Fri Dec 04 2026 18:00
First of the Month Sweep (2026-12-01): remind at Mon Nov 30 2026 18:00
Harbor Steps Cleanup (2027-01-01): remind at Thu Dec 31 2026 18:00
csv-demo.ts (columns, cell and logbookCsv from src/storage/logbookExport.ts)
// columns, cell and logbookCsv from src/storage/logbookExport.ts, run in Node with three logbook rows
type LogbookEntry = {
  eventTitle: string;
  eventDate: string;
  pieces: number;
  bags: number;
  weightKg: number;
  note: string;
};

const columns = ['Cleanup', 'Date', 'Pieces', 'Bags', 'Weight (kg)', 'Note'];

// One cell. Numbers as they are; text in quotes with every quote doubled, so a comma, a quote or a
// line break in a note stays inside its cell. Text that starts like a formula (= + - @) gets a '
// in front: a spreadsheet would otherwise run it as one.
function cell(value: string | number): string {
  if (typeof value === 'number') {
    return String(value);
  }
  const text = /^[=+\-@\t\r]/.test(value) ? `'${value}` : value;
  return `"${text.replaceAll('"', '""')}"`;
}

/** The logbook as CSV text: a row of column names, then one row per log */
export function logbookCsv(entries: LogbookEntry[]): string {
  const rows = entries.map((entry) => [
    entry.eventTitle,
    entry.eventDate,
    entry.pieces,
    entry.bags,
    entry.weightKg,
    entry.note,
  ]);
  const lines = [columns, ...rows].map((row) => row.map(cell).join(','));
  // A byte order mark (\uFEFF) first tells spreadsheet apps the text is UTF-8: without it,
  // "Café" may open as "CafÃ©"
  return `\uFEFF${lines.join('\r\n')}\r\n`;
}

const entries: LogbookEntry[] = [
  { eventTitle: 'Sunset Stairs Cleanup', eventDate: '2026-11-07', pieces: 42, bags: 1, weightKg: 2.5,
    note: 'Lots of wrappers by the stairs.' },
  { eventTitle: 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', eventDate: '2026-11-14', pieces: 63, bags: 2, weightKg: 4.1,
    note: 'Bottles, cans and 2 "lost" flip-flops.\nCafé cups too.' },
  { eventTitle: 'Tide Pool Morning Cleanup', eventDate: '2026-11-15', pieces: 12, bags: 1, weightKg: 0.8,
    note: '=HYPERLINK("http://example.com","Open")' },
];

// The obvious first try: join the fields with commas
const naive = [columns, ...entries.map((e) => [e.eventTitle, e.eventDate, e.pieces, e.bags, e.weightKg, e.note])];
console.log(naive.map((row) => row.join(',')).join('\n'));
console.log('---');
const csv = logbookCsv(entries);
console.log(`First character: U+${csv.codePointAt(0)?.toString(16).toUpperCase()}`);
console.log(`Rows ending in \\r\\n: ${csv.split('\r\n').length - 1}`);
console.log('---');
console.log(csv.replace('\uFEFF', '').replaceAll('\r\n', '\n').trimEnd());
Cleanup,Date,Pieces,Bags,Weight (kg),Note
Sunset Stairs Cleanup,2026-11-07,42,1,2.5,Lots of wrappers by the stairs.
Weekend Cove Cleanup,2026-11-14,63,2,4.1,Bottles, cans and 2 "lost" flip-flops.
Café cups too.
Tide Pool Morning Cleanup,2026-11-15,12,1,0.8,=HYPERLINK("http://example.com","Open")

First character: U+FEFF
Rows ending in \r\n: 4

"Cleanup","Date","Pieces","Bags","Weight (kg)","Note"
"Sunset Stairs Cleanup","2026-11-07",42,1,2.5,"Lots of wrappers by the stairs."
"Weekend Cove Cleanup","2026-11-14",63,2,4.1,"Bottles, cans and 2 ""lost"" flip-flops.
Café cups too."
"Tide Pool Morning Cleanup","2026-11-15",12,1,0.8,"'=HYPERLINK(""http://example.com"",""Open"")"

The first block is the lesson's naive card; the last is its "Shoreline Crew's version" card (both line for line). The middle two lines show what the box cannot: the first character is the byte order mark, and the four rows end with \r\n (the note's own line break is a plain \n inside its quotes).

● Task 3: on a phone, one file in cache/exports, shared as a CSV
  expect(jest.fn()).toHaveBeenCalledWith(...expected)
● Task 3: the previous export is deleted before the next one is written
  expect(received).toEqual(expected) // deep equality

The solution: npm test; npm run typecheck and npm run lint print nothing after their two > lines:

 PASS  src/lab.test.ts
  ✓ Task 1: a byte order mark first, and \r\n after every row
  ✓ Task 1: a note with a comma, quotes and a line break stays in one cell
  ✓ Task 1: counts stay numbers, and a missing date is an empty cell
  ✓ Task 1: text that starts like a formula is shown as text
  ✓ Task 2: switched off, nothing plays
  ✓ Task 2: an iPhone feels Success and Error
  ✓ Task 2: Android plays its own Confirm and Reject
  ✓ Task 2: a buzz that cannot play is never an error
  ✓ Task 3: importing the module reads no folder (the web preview imports it too)
  ✓ Task 3: the web preview never writes a file, even where the browser can share
  ✓ Task 3: on a phone, one file in cache/exports, shared as a CSV
  ✓ Task 3: the previous export is deleted before the next one is written
Tests:       12 passed, 12 total

What each task shows: (1) cell() returns '' for null, the number as it is, and text in quotes with replaceAll('"', '""'), after a ' when it matches /^[=+\-@\t\r]/; the text starts with the byte order mark and every row ends with \r\n (the test reads it with a real CSV reader); (2) buzz returns at once when hapticsOn is false, plays performAndroidHapticsAsync(AndroidHaptics.Confirm | Reject) on Android and notificationAsync(Success | Error) elsewhere, and calls .catch(() => {}) on the Promise; (3) the exports folder is made INSIDE shareInventory (the stand-in counts every read of Paths.cache, and the starter read it once at import), os === 'web' returns 'unavailable' before isAvailableAsync(), the previous export is deleted, and shareAsync gets mimeType: 'text/csv' and UTI: 'public.comma-separated-values-text'.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 6.4 · Notifications
  1. D The reminder time is known when the volunteer signs up, so the phone can schedule it without a server, an account or a signal: The cleanup's date and time are in the sign-up, so the phone can schedule the reminder itself. No server, no push credentials, no token, and no signal at 6 pm. Push is for things only a server knows, when they happen.
  2. B After the sign-ups are refetched, updateReminders cancels it: the sign-up is no longer confirmed: cancelSignupMutation settles through refreshSignups, which fetches the sign-ups again and then runs updateReminders. The cancelled sign-up is no longer confirmed, so its reminder is not wanted, and the phone cancels it.
  3. C useReminderTaps reads getLastNotificationResponse() once the app is ready, checks the path and calls router.push: The tap started the app before any listener existed, so useReminderTaps reads it once with getLastNotificationResponse when the app is ready, checks content.data.url with isCleanupPath, and calls router.push. The listener handles taps while the app runs.
  4. A Nothing: expo-notifications has no scheduler on the web, which is why the preview says reminders work on a phone: expo-notifications uses the browser's permission on the web, but its web scheduler is empty, so nothing was ever scheduled. remindersWork is false there, and the Profile tab says reminders work in the app on a phone.
  5. C Before then nobody seems signed in, so updateReminders would cancel every reminder at each launch: Before loadSession finishes, readSession() returns null, so updateReminders would decide that nobody is signed in and cancel every reminder at each launch, then set them again, or not at all when the app starts offline.
Lesson 6.5 · Haptics, Sharing and Files
  1. C Many people cannot feel or have turned off haptics, so a buzz may confirm something but must never be the only way to learn it: Some people cannot feel haptics or turn them off, some phones have no suitable motor, and screen readers announce words, not buzzes. A haptic confirms what the screen already says; it never carries information alone.
  2. A The file is only made to be shared, and if the phone clears it, the logbook in SQLite can write it again: The file exists only to be handed to the share sheet. The receiving app keeps its own copy, the logbook itself stays in SQLite, and the next press writes the file again, so losing it to a cache cleanup costs nothing.
  3. D The screen says files cannot be shared here and shows the file's rows as text to copy: shareLogbook checks Platform.OS first and returns unavailable on the web, without touching expo-file-system. ShareLogbook then explains that files cannot be shared there and shows the CSV rows as selectable text.
  4. B "Bottles, cans and 2 ""lost"" flip-flops.": Text cells are wrapped in double quotes and every quote inside is doubled, so the comma and the quotes stay inside one cell when a spreadsheet reads the file.
  5. A No buzz, and the words "You are signed up. See you on the beach!" as usual: feel() reads the setting with readSettings() at the moment of the buzz and returns at once when it is off. The words You are signed up. See you on the beach! still appear, as they do for everyone.

The Lessons' Exercises: the Kit's Runs

  • 6.4 Step 1 (introspect with and without the entry): both runs above (Predict P1); each equals the lesson's card.
  • 6.5 A File a Spreadsheet Trusts: csv-demo.ts above gives both of the lesson's cards from the finished code.

Exit Ticket

  1. Local: the reminder's time is in the sign-up, so the phone schedules it with no server, account or signal. A cancellation is known only to a server, at the moment it happens: a push, which needs a development build, an EAS project, FCM and APNs credentials, a push token per device, a server that stores tokens and a sender (none of it in this course).
  2. Haptics: outcomes not presses; the same meaning every time; never the only feedback; less is more; optional. CSV: text quoted with inner quotes doubled; a byte order mark first; a ' before a formula; \r\n after every row; numbers bare.
  3. The project: Bookmobile Stops for Tamarack County Library Bookmobile (Odile Varga). Complete = no rubric row (R1 to R6) at 0. Part 2 met = the Bookmobile Stops project complete under its rubric (no row at 0) AND the Part 2 assessment met (14 or more of 20 points, 70 %).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Thinks cancelling a reminder can say something. Back to the tide table and the harbor radio.
  • Q2 B or C. Reads a lint error as "the name is missing" or silences it. Point at tsc's silence: the name exists.
  • Q4 A or B. Thinks in "schedule here, cancel there". Read updateReminders' three steps again.
  • Q7 A. Buzzes on the press. Ask what has happened when the thumb lifts, offline.
  • Q8 B. Escapes quotes with a backslash, as in JavaScript. CSV doubles them.
  • Q10 A. Trusts the tools to see platforms. Show the lint warning: it is about an unused name.

A1
In the Lesson 4.1 app (before Lesson 4.4's anchor), the cleanup screen's leave() runs router.canGoBack() ? router.back() : router.replace('/'). When does the replace branch run? (1 point)
A) When the volunteer presses the back arrow in the header
B) When the screen was opened straight from its address (or a link from outside): it is the only screen in the stack, so there is nothing to go back to
C) Whenever the API answers 404 for the id
D) Only on Android, which has a system back button
Why: router.canGoBack() says whether anything is underneath. A screen opened from its address has no pile under it (the crew leader's single clipboard sheet), so the app sends the volunteer home with replace instead (Lesson 4.1).
A is wrong: The header's back arrow pops the stack itself; it does not call leave().
C is wrong: A 404 shows the empty state; whether its button goes back or replaces still depends on the stack.
D is wrong: canGoBack() is about the stack, not the platform.
A2
Moving the Cleanups screen into the tab group, a learner copies src/app/index.tsx to src/app/(tabs)/index.tsx and forgets to delete the original. What does the app show? (1 point)
A) Metro stops with an error: two routes claim the address /
B) The tab bar, with the Cleanups tab listed twice
C) The root file wins: the Cleanups screen opens with no tab bar at all and a header titled index, and Metro prints no warning
D) npx tsc --noEmit reports a duplicate route
Why: Both files claim /; the course measured that the root file wins, silently. If the tab bar is missing on the first screen, look for a leftover index.tsx; moving with git mv avoids it (Lesson 4.2).
A is wrong: Nothing stops and nothing is printed: that is what makes this mistake slow to find.
B is wrong: The (tabs) group never renders at all.
D is wrong: tsc is silent: both are valid route files.
A3
Shoreline Crew's sign-in modal calls router.back() and only then setSession(session) when the API says yes. Why that order? (1 point)
A) The moment the session is set, Stack.Protected takes the sign-in screen away; if it were still open, Expo Router would send the volunteer to the first tab instead of back to the screen that opened sign-in
B) setSession throws while a modal is open
C) router.back() is what saves the token in SecureStore
D) The order does not matter: it is a style choice
Why: Close first, then sign in: the guard session === null stops being true the moment the session is set. Opened from its address (canGoBack() false), the guard's redirect to the Cleanups tab is exactly right (Lesson 4.3, measured).
B is wrong: Nothing throws: the guard redirects, which is the bug.
C is wrong: Saving is session.ts's listener, which runs on every session change.
D is wrong: The other order was measured: the volunteer lands on the first tab.
A4
With unstable_settings = { anchor: '(tabs)' } in the root layout, the not-found screen still uses Lesson 4.1's <Link href="/" replace asChild>. A volunteer opens a mistyped address and presses Back to the cleanups. What happens? (1 point)
A) Metro prints an error about the anchor
B) The app closes, because nothing is left in the stack
C) The Cleanups list appears with one tab bar, exactly as intended
D) The list looks right, but the page now holds two tab bars: a copy of the tabs replaced the not-found screen on top of the tabs the anchor put underneath. dismissTo closes screens back to the existing tabs instead
Why: An anchor changes what replace means: the tabs are already underneath, so replacing the top screen with / adds a second copy (measured: two tab bars, no warning). <Link href="/" dismissTo> closes screens until it reaches /, and replaces only when there is nothing to go back to (Lesson 4.4).
A is wrong: Nothing warns about it.
B is wrong: With the anchor, the tabs are underneath: there is always something left.
C is wrong: That is what dismissTo gives; replace measured two tab bars.
A5
The school's Wi-Fi keeps devices apart, so you start Metro with npx expo start --tunnel. Expo Go now loads Shoreline Crew on your phone, but the Cleanups list says it could not reach the server. Why? (1 point)
A) The tunnel accepts only https addresses, and the API uses http
B) --tunnel switches off every EXPO_PUBLIC_ variable
C) The tunnel carries only Metro's traffic: the app's requests still go to EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, an address the phone cannot reach, and shoreline-api has no tunnel
D) shoreline-api must be restarted with --tunnel as well
Why: --tunnel gets the app's code to the phone through a service on the internet, but the API still needs an address the phone can reach. On a network with client isolation: your home network, a phone hotspot, the Android emulator or the web preview (Lesson 5.1).
A is wrong: The tunnel is not the problem: the API's requests never go through it.
B is wrong: EXPO_PUBLIC_ variables are built into the app as usual.
D is wrong: shoreline-api has --host, not a tunnel.
A6
Theo spends ten minutes on the Cleanups tab, then switches back to My cleanups. In a version WITHOUT useRefreshOnFocus('signups'), what happens to his sign-ups list? (1 point)
A) Nothing asks the API: the tab stayed mounted, and neither the app's focus nor the network changed, so the list shows what was fetched ten minutes ago
B) It is fetched again, because the screen mounts again
C) Query fetches it again, because the window gained focus
D) The list is empty until he pulls to refresh
Why: On the web, a new page mounts and its queries start; on a phone, visited tabs stay mounted, and the focus and online managers stay quiet. useRefreshOnFocus refetches the key's ACTIVE and STALE queries each time the screen comes into view (Lesson 5.2).
B is wrong: Tabs you have visited stay mounted (Lesson 4.2).
C is wrong: Switching tabs is not an app focus change: the app never left the foreground.
D is wrong: The cached list stays on screen; it is old, not gone.
A7
The settings saved on a phone are {"theme":"sepia"} (written by an older version, or by a bug). Shoreline Crew starts. What happens? (1 point)
A) TypeScript rejects the saved value before the app starts
B) useTheme meets an unknown theme and the app shows a red error screen
C) The app clears everything in AsyncStorage
D) The settings store keeps a theme only if it is one of the three it knows, so it falls back to the default: the app opens with Phone setting chosen
Why: What comes out of storage was written by some earlier version of your app: check it like data from the API. Measured: sepia, and even text that is not JSON, opened with Phone setting chosen (Lesson 5.3).
A is wrong: Types are gone when the app runs; AsyncStorage hands back a string.
B is wrong: The store never hands useTheme a theme it does not know.
C is wrong: Nothing is deleted: only the unknown value is ignored.
A8
Why does the sign-in screen write const send = handleSubmit(submit) and onPress={() => send()} instead of the web course's habit, onPress={handleSubmit(submit)}? (1 point)
A) handleSubmit works only inside a <form>, which React Native has only on the web
B) handleSubmit(submit) returns a function typed to take a web form event; a press event and the return key's submit event are different types, so tsc rejects it. Called with no event at all, it type-checks
C) onPress cannot take a function that another function returned
D) Calling handleSubmit while rendering sends the form twice
Why: The seam between React Hook Form and the phone: tsc reports it once for the button and once for the return key (onSubmitEditing). Make the function once and call it with no arguments (Lesson 5.4).
A is wrong: There is no <form> on a phone at all; handleSubmit itself works anywhere.
C is wrong: It can: () => send() is exactly that. The problem is the event type.
D is wrong: handleSubmit(submit) only builds a function; nothing is sent until it is called.
A9
Shoreline Crew saves debris logs in a persisted queue that survives the app, but leaves sign-ups as Lesson 5.2's mutations, which wait in memory. Why? (1 point)
A) A sign-up is decided by the server (a spot or the waiting list), so the volunteer needs the answer now; the queue is for things a person creates (a log, a note, a photo)
B) Sign-ups are too big for AsyncStorage
C) Zustand can persist only one kind of item
D) Sign-ups never fail when the phone is offline
Why: Queue what people create, and let what the server decides wait for the server: "you are signed up" later, somehow, is no answer (Lesson 5.5).
B is wrong: A sign-up is a few bytes.
C is wrong: A store can hold any state; this is a design choice.
D is wrong: Offline, a sign-up pauses in memory (Lesson 5.2): it is lost if the app closes.
A10
Lesson 6.1's app.json entry for expo-location sets locationAlwaysAndWhenInUsePermission, locationAlwaysPermission, motionUsagePermission and locationFullAccuracyPermission to false. Why? (1 point)
A) To turn location off in the web preview
B) So that Expo Go skips the location question
C) expo-location's plugin runs even when it is not listed, with default words for every string; false removes what Shoreline Crew never uses (Always location, motion, temporary precise location), so the app asks only for what it uses
D) Without them, npx expo install expo-location fails
Why: The entry exists to pass options: your own words for when-in-use location, and false for the rest. Vague or unused strings make people say no, and app reviewers ask about access an app cannot justify (Lesson 6.1).
A is wrong: The web preview asks through the browser; app.json does not change that.
B is wrong: Expo Go asks as itself, with its own words, and ignores your plugins.
D is wrong: The install worked without any entry: it printed no "Added config plugin" line.
A11
Shoreline Crew's picker options include preferredAssetRepresentationMode: Compatible. What is it for? (1 point)
A) It asks for the photo-library permission before the picker opens
B) iPhones save photos as HEIC; this option asks iOS for its most compatible version, a JPEG, because shoreline-api answers HEIC with 415 Photos must be JPEG, PNG or WebP images
C) It makes Android photos smaller
D) It removes the location data (EXIF) from the photo
Why: From expo-image-picker's iOS code and Apple's and Expo's documentation (the course could not check it on an iPhone): without the option, a HEIC photo comes back as HEIC. quality: 0.6 and exif: false are the other two size settings (Lesson 6.2).
A is wrong: Picking from the library generally needs no permission; this option changes the file, not the question.
C is wrong: Android ignores the option.
D is wrong: That is exif: false.
A12
A teammate writes checkInMutation with only a mutationFn. Theo types the right code; shoreline-api answers 200 and marks his sign-up attended. What does the screen do? (1 point)
A) It shows You are checked in at once
B) It shows an error, because the answer carries no new data
C) It goes back to the cleanup screen
D) Nothing changes: still the form, still the code, still Check in. The screen draws from the cached sign-ups, and nothing marked them out of date: onSettled must invalidate ['signups']
Why: Measured in the web preview: the server said attended, the screen did not move, and the volunteer pressed again and again. Invalidating ['signups'] refetches the active query, and the You are checked in face appears by itself (Lesson 6.3).
A is wrong: Only after the sign-ups are fetched again.
B is wrong: The answer is the sign-up, now attended: there is no error.
C is wrong: Nothing navigates: the screen stays where it is.
A13
When a volunteer taps a reminder, useReminderTaps reads url from the notification's content.data. Why does it pass the value through isCleanupPath() before router.push? (1 point)
A) router.push accepts only addresses that start with https://
B) A notification's data is outside input: it may be missing and its values are typed unknown; the type guard accepts only a cleanup's path, so anything else (a web address, /sign-in, a number) opens nothing
C) isCleanupPath() schedules the next reminder
D) Without it, Android would not show the notification
Why: Today the app writes the data, but the same handler would read a server's push tomorrow. A cast (as Href) would silence tsc's TS18048 and TS2345 and open whatever arrived; the guard checks first, like the API's type guards since Lesson 1.3 (Lesson 6.4).
A is wrong: router.push takes the app's own routes, not web addresses.
C is wrong: Scheduling is updateReminders' job.
D is wrong: The guard runs after the tap; showing the notification is the phone's job.
A14
A teammate adds const folder = new Directory(Paths.cache, 'exports') at the top level of src/storage/logbookExport.ts. On a phone it works. What happens in the web preview? (1 point)
A) The Logbook tab fails to load: importing the module is enough to throw this.validatePath is not a function (a blank tab; the exported preview answers /logbook with a 500). Create files and folders inside the functions that use them
B) Only the Share my logbook button is disabled
C) The file is written to the browser's downloads folder
D) tsc reports that Paths does not exist on the web
Why: The web has no file system: expo-file-system's web stand-in throws as soon as Paths.cache is read. Nobody pressed anything; importing the module was enough, and the whole tab imports it (Lesson 6.5, measured).
B is wrong: The error happens on import, before any button exists.
C is wrong: There is no file system in the web preview; sharing there goes through navigator.share with a URL.
D is wrong: tsc type-checks one code base for every platform: it is silent.
A15
A teammate says: "My cleanups shows guests a sign-in face, so GET /api/me/signups does not need to check the token." In two or three sentences: why is that wrong, and what does Shoreline Crew do when the API answers 401 to a request that carried a token? (2 points)
A screen's guard or guest face only hides screens: anyone can call the API without the app (curl, an old version, another program), so the API must check the token on every request, and it does (401). When a request that carried a token gets a 401, the client clears the session (setSession(null)), and the query and mutation caches' onError marks the sign-in as ended, so the app shows "Your sign-in has ended. Sign in again." and its guest face.
Marking (2 points: 2 / 1 / 0): 1 point: the API can be reached without the screen, so hiding is not protection ("the guard hides screens; the API protects data"). 1 point: the 401 path: the session is cleared (the client's rule) and/or the sign-in-ended message through the caches' onError, back to the guest face. "The API is secure anyway" without a reason: 0 for the first point.
Source: Lesson 4.3 (the guard and the 401 rule) and Lesson 5.2 (one place for 401: the caches' onError).
A16
This is the debris-log form after Lesson 5.5. Why is clientId made once, when the form opens, and not inside send (for example toNewLog(data, randomUUID(), new Date()))? And what does shoreline-api do when the same log arrives twice? (2 points)
shoreline-crew/src/app/log/[id].tsx
function DebrisLogForm({ event, viewer }: { event: CleanupEvent; viewer: string }) {
  // One id per log, made on the phone the moment the form opens: if the same log is sent twice
  // (a retry after a lost answer), the API recognizes it and keeps one
  const [clientId] = useState(() => randomUUID());
  ...
  // Checked by the schema first; the time is when Send was pressed, not when the form opened.
  // The log goes into the queue (saved on the phone at once), and the queue sends it if it can.
  const send = handleSubmit((data) => {
    const log = toNewLog(data, clientId, new Date());
    addLog(viewer, { event, log });
    setHandedOver(log);
    void sendWaitingLogs(viewer);
  });
On a bad connection the first send can reach the server while its answer is lost, so the queue (or the volunteer) sends the log again. Made once per log, the clientId is the same on every try. shoreline-api recognizes it: the first send answers 201 with the saved log, a resend with the same clientId answers 200 with the log it already has, so there is exactly one log. An id made inside send would be new on every try: two logs, and the bottles counted twice.
Marking (2 points: 2 / 1 / 0): 1 point: the reason: a retry after a lost answer must carry the same id (the phone cannot know whether the first try arrived). 1 point: the API's behavior: 201 the first time, 200 with the saved log for the same clientId, never a duplicate ("it recognizes it and keeps one" is enough).
Source: Lesson 5.4 made the id once per log; Lesson 5.5's queue saves it with the log, which is what makes every retry safe.
A17
Read permissionStep (Lesson 6.1). On an Android phone, Maya answered no to the location question once, and the Hook reads { status: 'denied', granted: false, canAskAgain: true }. Which step does the function return, and what does the panel offer her? After a second no, canAskAgain is false: which step then, and what can the panel offer? (2 points)
shoreline-crew/src/hooks/usePermission.ts
export function permissionStep(response: PermissionResponse | null): PermissionStep {
  if (response === null) {
    return 'checking';
  }
  if (response.granted) {
    return 'granted';
  }
  if (response.status === 'undetermined') {
    return 'not-asked';
  }
  // The web preview's answers always say canAskAgain: true, but a browser never asks again after
  // a "Block": treat a no there as blocked
  if (!response.canAskAgain || Platform.OS === 'web') {
    return 'blocked';
  }
  return 'denied';
}
'denied' (not granted, not undetermined, the phone may still ask, and it is not the web): the panel says that everything else works without it and offers Ask me again (Android allows one more question). After the second no: 'blocked': the phone will not ask again, so asking is useless; the panel says so and offers Open Settings (Linking.openSettings()), and the answer is read again when she comes back to the app.
Marking (2 points: 2 / 1 / 0): 1 point: 'denied' with a way to ask again (and the app still usable). 1 point: 'blocked' with Open Settings, not another request. Naming only the steps, without what the panel offers: 1 point in all.
Source: Lesson 6.1's five rules: a no is an answer, and say when only Settings can help.

labs/projects/part2-tamarack-bookmobile

R3 Data from the mock API5.1, 5.2a typed client with guards; TanStack Query with the phone's managers; loading, error and empty states; pull to refresh
R4 Offline-safe storage5.3, 5.5hold requests in a persisted queue with clientIds that syncs when back online; the My stop setting in AsyncStorage
R5 The form and the device feature5.4, 6.1, 6.3the place-hold form (React Hook Form + Zod); Find my nearest stop: the location question at the right moment, a no handled
R6 Qualityalltypes, accessibility labels, npx tsc --noEmit, npx expo lint 0 problems, Jest, npx expo export for web, android and ios, NOTES.md

Rubric, scoring guidance by row, the scoring sheet and the instructor notes: final-assessment.html (Part 2 section) and the project folder (RUBRIC.md, INSTRUCTOR_NOTES.md). The model solution's checks are its lab.json runs (python3 labs/check_labs.py projects/part2-tamarack-bookmobile).

Part 2 met = the Bookmobile Stops project complete under its rubric (no row at 0) AND the Part 2 assessment met (14 or more of 20 points, 70 %).

Self-check: Part 2 Review

Item 11 quotes Lesson 6.4's own tsc card (the first line). Every other fact is the lessons' own, most of them measured in the web preview.

Q1
Lesson 4.1 gives a rule for choosing between <Link> and router. Which navigation belongs to router?
A) The Open button on a cleanup card
B) The tab bar's buttons
C) Navigation the code decides, such as leave(): back when something is underneath, home when not
D) None: router exists only in the web preview
Why: Link (with asChild on your own button) for plain "go there"; router.push, replace, back and canGoBack when the code decides where to go (Lesson 4.1).
A is wrong: Open is a plain "go there": a Link.
B is wrong: The tab navigator draws and handles its own buttons.
D is wrong: router works on every platform; Expo Router is the app's navigation.
Q2
How does the web preview show a route with presentation: 'modal', and why does every Shoreline Crew modal have a visible Done button?
A) As an ordinary screen with a back arrow (measured); the ways to close a modal differ by platform and swiping a card down is not obvious to everyone
B) As a browser pop-up window; Done closes the window
C) Not at all: modal routes run only on phones
D) As a sheet from the bottom; Done is required by Expo Router
Why: A modal is an ordinary stack route presented differently: a card on an iPhone, over the whole screen on Android, an ordinary screen on the web. Done uses canGoBack(): back, or home when it was opened from its address (Lesson 4.2).
B is wrong: Nothing opens a new window.
C is wrong: It opens in every place; only the presentation differs.
D is wrong: The sheet look is a phone's; nothing requires a Done button, people need one.
Q3
useSession() calls useSyncExternalStore(onSessionChange, readSession) with no third argument. tsc and npx expo lint are quiet. What happens in the web preview?
A) Nothing: the third argument is optional
B) The session is lost on every render
C) tsc reports a missing argument after a restart
D) Every page load logs an SSR error, Missing getServerSnapshot, and the page switches to client rendering: the third argument (() => null) is what the server render uses
Why: The web preview renders pages on the server first (web.output: "server"), and there is no session there. () => null says "signed out" for that first render (Lesson 4.3, measured).
A is wrong: Optional for the type checker, required for server rendering.
B is wrong: The store keeps the session; only the server render fails.
C is wrong: tsc stays quiet: the parameter is optional in the types.
Q4
With anchor: '(tabs)', opening /cleanups/e08 directly sends more requests than without it. Why?
A) The anchor sends every request twice, to be safe
B) The tabs underneath are real screens: the Cleanups tab also loads its first page of cleanups and the sites (three requests instead of one)
C) Deep links always reload the whole app
D) The anchor turns off TanStack Query's cache
Why: The price of a back button that leads somewhere. The cleanup's own request comes first, so the screen the volunteer asked for is not kept waiting (Lesson 4.4, measured).
A is wrong: No request is repeated; two more screens load.
C is wrong: Only the anchor's screens mount; nothing reloads.
D is wrong: The cache works as before; there is simply more on screen.
Q5
You want ten minutes on the Android emulator without editing .env.local. Which command does Lesson 5.1 use?
A) EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL=http://10.0.2.2:4000 npx expo start --clear: a variable set in the terminal wins over the file, and 10.0.2.2 is the emulator's name for your computer
B) npx expo start --android --host localhost
C) node server.ts --emulator
D) npx expo start --tunnel
Why: Measured: an export built with .env.local saying localhost and the shell variable set to 10.0.2.2 contained only the 10.0.2.2 address. --clear because the address is built into the bundle (Lesson 5.1).
B is wrong: localhost on the emulator is the emulator itself.
C is wrong: shoreline-api has no such option.
D is wrong: The tunnel carries Metro only, never the API.
Q6
useOnlineManager feeds TanStack Query from NetInfo. Why does it read isConnected and not isInternetReachable?
A) isInternetReachable does not exist in NetInfo 12
B) isConnected is faster
C) shoreline-api is on your Wi-Fi: a network without internet still reaches it, and "reachable" means NetInfo could reach a test address on the internet
D) Expo Go blocks isInternetReachable
Why: On a beach both mean the same thing; at your desk only isConnected is right. null counts as online (Lesson 5.2).
A is wrong: NetInfo reports both.
B is wrong: Speed is not the question; meaning is.
D is wrong: Nothing is blocked.
Q7
A volunteer's phone is set to German, and the decimal pad types 2,5 in the weight field. What does Shoreline Crew's schema do?
A) Rejects it: only 2.5 is a number
B) Turns the comma into a point before it checks the value, so the log says 2.5 kg
C) Sends 2,5 to the API, which rejects it
D) Stores 25 kg
Why: Accept what the phone's own keyboard can type: a rule of /^\d+(\.\d+)?$/ alone rejects what a German, French or Spanish decimal pad types, and the volunteer cannot fix it from that keyboard (Lesson 5.4).
A is wrong: That is the mistake the lesson measured.
C is wrong: The schema has already turned it into a number.
D is wrong: The comma is a decimal separator, not ignored.
Q8
The Logbook selects "my waiting logs" with useLogQueue((state) => state.logs.filter(...)), without useShallow. What happens?
A) The list is right but one render behind
B) tsc reports the selector
C) Nothing: Zustand compares arrays by their items
D) filter builds a new array on every call, so the store never sees the same answer: a blank page and React's error #185, Maximum update depth exceeded
Why: Wrap selectors that build new arrays in useShallow, or select something that stays the same, such as the .length the tab badge uses (Lesson 5.5, measured; the web course's Lesson 7.3 mistake, unchanged on a phone).
A is wrong: It never settles at all.
B is wrong: The types are fine.
C is wrong: Without useShallow it compares with Object.is.
Q9
Before the phone's own location question, Shoreline Crew shows its own explanation with Continue and Not now. Why not ask straight away?
The phone's question comes with the app's reason, at the moment the feature is wanted (ask in context, explain first). On an iPhone a no is final after one answer (only Settings can change it), so the app explains first, and Not now leaves the phone's question unasked for a better moment
Why: Lesson 6.1's rules: ask in context, explain first, a no is an answer, say when only Settings can help, check before every use.
Q10
In the web preview, a volunteer adds a photo to a log, then reloads the page before it is sent. What happened to the photo, and why?
A) It is gone: in the web preview a photo is a blob: address in the browser's memory that lasts until the page reloads (photoFiles.web.ts); on a phone it was moved into the app's documents
B) It is safe in the app's documents folder, as on a phone
C) It was uploaded when it was added
D) The browser saved it to the downloads folder
Why: The web preview has no file system for an app, so photoFiles.web.ts keeps the browser's own address. On a phone, keepPhoto moves the photo out of the cache into the documents folder (Lesson 6.2).
B is wrong: There is no file system in the web preview.
C is wrong: Photos go with the log, when the queue sends it.
D is wrong: Nothing writes to the browser's downloads.
Q11
tsc prints this for an older reminder example. What does trigger need in expo-notifications 58?
src/notifications/reminders.ts:120:9 - error TS2322: Type 'Date' is not assignable to type 'NotificationTriggerInput'.
A) trigger: reminderTime(event).getTime()
B) An object with a type: { type: Notifications.SchedulableTriggerInputTypes.DATE, date: reminderTime(event) }
C) trigger: null, then set the time in content
D) trigger: reminderTime(event) as NotificationTriggerInput
Why: A trigger is an object with a type (DATE, TIME_INTERVAL, DAILY, WEEKLY), and null means "now". Plain JavaScript would get through with a deprecation warning; TypeScript catches it first (Lesson 6.4).
A is wrong: A number is not a trigger either.
C is wrong: null shows the notification at once.
D is wrong: A cast silences the message and keeps the wrong shape.
Q12
When does Shoreline Crew use React Native's Share.share, and when expo-sharing's shareAsync? Why not pass a link to shareAsync?
Share.share for words and links (the cleanup's link, Lesson 4.4); shareAsync for one local file (the logbook CSV). shareAsync wants a local file address: given https://… it type-checks, but on Android it refuses ("Only local file URLs are supported")
Why: Lesson 6.5: React Native's Share takes a message and a title (no file on Android); expo-sharing shares one file from the app's own folders.

Exit Ticket

  1. Any idea with a place in their code. Weak answers name an idea without a file: ask "where?" at the hand-in.
  2. Their own; it should match the check-in's next step. If it does not, the check-in did not land: follow up in writing.
  3. Their own; collect them for the course feedback.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • A3 D (the order does not matter). Has not seen the guard redirect: Lesson 4.3's third Common Mistake, measured.
  • A6 B or C (the screen mounts again, or the window focus refetches). Still thinks in web pages: Lesson 4.2's tabs stay mounted; Lesson 5.2's table of browser and phone signals.
  • A9 C (sign-ups never fail offline). Mixes up "paused" and "safe": a paused mutation lives in memory only (Lesson 5.2), the queue on the phone (Lesson 5.5).
  • A14 D (tsc reports it). Believes the type checker knows the platform: one code base is type-checked for every platform; the web stand-in throws at run time.
  • Self-check 3 A (optional argument). Treats "tsc is quiet" as "it works": the server render needs the third argument.
React Native & Expo: Build and Ship a Mobile App · Answer Key

Part 3: Motion, Quality and Shipping

Sessions 18 to 26. Modules 7 to 10: animation with Reanimated 4 and gestures, motion polish, accessible mobile apps, testing the app's logic and its screens with Jest, React Native Testing Library and MSW, performance and debugging, app config and environments, development builds and EAS Build, updates and monitoring, the app stores, then the final project and real mobile projects. Opens with a Parts 1 and 2 refresher and catch-up, and ends with the final project showcase and final assessment.

Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 18: Animation and Gestures

Answers and reasoning for every Session 18 item: the refresher, the catch-up check's real output, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 18 12 self-check items Confidential

Refresher

  1. Lesson 5.5's queue: a debris log is saved with no signal. Where does it wait, and what sends it later? **Answer:** In the log queue (a Zustand store saved on the phone), shown under "Not sent yet" in the Logbook; the queue sender sends waiting logs when the phone is online again (or with Send now). Lesson 7.1's tally counts what arrives; Lesson 7.2 lets a waiting log be swiped away.
  2. Lesson 4.3's useFocusEffect: when does its function run, compared with useEffect? **Answer:** When the screen comes into view (and again when its dependencies change while it is in view), not only when the component mounts: a tab you are not looking at stays still. Tally starts its count with it.
  3. Lesson 2.5's createThemedStyles: what does it give a component? **Answer:** A useStyles() Hook whose StyleSheet is built from the current theme's colors (light or dark). Tally and SwipeToRemove take their colors from it (colors.pressed, badges.danger).
  4. Lesson 3.2's FlashList: what does it do with a row that scrolls off the screen? **Answer:** Recycles it: the same row component is reused for another item, with whatever state it holds. Lesson 7.2 builds on that: swipe state lives in the parent, keyed by id.

7.1 · Animation with Reanimated 4: self-check

Q1
While the tally counts from 42 to 44, TanStack Query parses a long logbook answer. Which version stays smooth on a phone, and why?
A) A setInterval that calls setShown 30 times a second: timers run on their own thread
B) A shared value animated with withTiming: the UI thread works out every frame without the JavaScript thread
C) A useEffect that calls setShown once per frame: React batches the updates
D) Neither: animations on a phone always stutter while data loads
Why: React says once where the value should go (the skipper); the UI thread steps it frame by frame (the helm). A busy JavaScript thread cannot make it late. Timers and setState per frame both need the JavaScript thread for every frame (Lesson 7.1, Two Threads, One Frame).
A is wrong: JavaScript timers fire on the JavaScript thread: late whenever it is busy (the lesson's setInterval question).
C is wrong: Every frame would still need React and the JavaScript thread.
D is wrong: That is exactly the problem Reanimated solves.
Q2
A teammate writes count.value = withTiming(...) in Tally.tsx. npx expo lint prints this. Which change fixes it?
~/shoreline/shoreline-crew/src/components/Tally.tsx
  63:7  error  Error: This value cannot be modified
> 63 |       count.value = withTiming(value, counted, (finished) => {
     |       ^^^^^ `count` cannot be modified
  ...  react-hooks/immutability
✖ 1 problem (1 error, 0 warnings)
A) Add // eslint-disable-next-line above the line
B) Make count a let instead of a const
C) Write count.set(withTiming(...)), and read with count.get()
D) Switch the React Compiler off in app.json
Why: The React Compiler's lint rules treat what a Hook returns as unchangeable. set() and get() do the same thing as a method call, which the compiler leaves alone, and Reanimated recommends them for compiled code (Lesson 7.1, Common Mistakes; the kit's run is the lesson's card).
A is wrong: Hiding the rule leaves code the compiler may optimize wrongly.
B is wrong: count is never reassigned; the rule is about changing a Hook's value.
D is wrong: The project relies on the compiler; one method call fixes it.
Q3
A progress number counts from 0 to 42 pieces. Which animation fits it?
A) withTiming(42, { duration: 900, easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic) })
B) withSpring(42, { mass: 1, stiffness: 100, damping: 10 })
C) withSpring(42, { dampingRatio: 0.35, duration: 500 })
D) withRepeat(withTiming(42), -1)
Why: A number must land exactly on its target: withTiming ends on time and on the value. The course measured Reanimated 3's old default spring showing 49, then 41, before settling on 42: totals that were never true. Springs are for the bounce, not the count.
B is wrong: Overshoots: the measured 0 … 49 … 41 … 42.
C is wrong: A dampingRatio below 1 overshoots on purpose: right for the bounce, wrong for the number.
D is wrong: Repeats forever: a count is not a loop.
Q4
Reduce Motion is on and the app was restarted. Which of these still moves in the web preview, as the lesson measured?
A) The tally's count (withTiming)
B) The tally's bounce (withSequence of a timing and a spring)
C) FadeIn's opacity (withTiming)
D) A breathing dot with a CSS-style keyframe animation and no useReducedMotion() check
Why: Reanimated's animation functions default to ReduceMotion.System and jump to the end. The CSS-style transitions and keyframe animations do not look at the setting (measured), so the code must check useReducedMotion() itself, as SendingDot and Lab 7.1's LiveBadge do.
A is wrong: Jumps to the end: the totals appear at once.
B is wrong: Jumps to the end too: no bounce.
C is wrong: Was 1 at the first sample.
Q5
Inside useAnimatedReaction, why does Tally call scheduleOnRN(setShown, step / steps), and only when the rounded step changes? Two reasons.
The reaction runs on the UI thread, which cannot call a React setter (on a phone: the Worklets red screen); and React should draw once per visible step, not once per frame
Why: Anything that is not a worklet goes through scheduleOnRN to the JavaScript thread. The step check keeps React's work to about one render per number shown (42 renders for 42 pieces). The web preview runs worklets on its one thread, so the direct call works there: the trap.
Q6
Maya turns on Reduce Motion while Shoreline Crew (at the end of Lesson 7.1) is open, and opens her Logbook. What happens?
A) The tallies stop counting at once
B) The tallies still count: the setting was read when the app started
C) The app crashes: the setting changed underneath it
D) Only the background highlight stops
Why: Reanimated reads the setting once, at start (its documentation: changing it "doesn't cause your components to rerender"). Restart the app, or reload the web preview. Lesson 7.3 makes the app follow it live.
A is wrong: That is Lesson 7.3's code, not 7.1's.
C is wrong: Nothing crashes; the old value is simply used.
D is wrong: The highlight is a CSS-style transition: it is kept on purpose, and does not follow the setting anyway.

7.1 · Animation with Reanimated 4: predict, then check

7.2 · Gestures: self-check

Q7
A pan copied from a Gesture Handler 2 tutorial. npx tsc --noEmit prints this. What is the fix?
src/components/SwipeToRemove.tsx:42:5 - error TS2353: Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'onEnd' does not exist in type 'PanGestureConfig'.
src/components/SwipeToRemove.tsx:42:13 - error TS7006: Parameter 'event' implicitly has an 'any' type.
Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/components/SwipeToRemove.tsx:42
A) Rename onEnd to onDeactivate (Gesture Handler 3's name); the second error goes with it
B) Type the parameter: (event: any) =>
C) Use Gesture.Pan().onEnd(...) instead of the Hook
D) Install Gesture Handler 2
Why: The config object is typed: for a property it does not know, TypeScript cannot tell what event is, hence TS7006. Fix the first error and both go (Lesson 7.2, Common Mistakes; the kit's run is the lesson's card). The names are onBegin, onActivate, onUpdate, onDeactivate, onFinalize.
B is wrong: Silences the second error; the callback still never runs.
C is wrong: The builder API is deprecated in version 3.
D is wrong: SDK 58 pins 3.3.
Q8
The root layout's GestureHandlerRootView is taken out. Where does the missing root view show up?
A) In npx tsc --noEmit
B) In the web preview, as soon as a card is dragged
C) Nowhere: gestures just stop working everywhere
D) On a phone in development (a red screen), and in Jest, which renders as iOS; not in the web preview
Why: Gesture Handler checks for the root view only when __DEV__ and the platform is not web. The lesson measured the web preview carrying on; the kit rendered Lab 7.2's list without it in Jest (jest-expo renders as iOS) and got the phone's message word for word.
A is wrong: Types are fine: nothing in the code is wrong for tsc.
B is wrong: The web preview skips the check: the card still followed the mouse.
C is wrong: In development a phone stops with the error message.
Q9
In SwipeToRemove, what do activeOffsetX: [-12, 12] and failOffsetY: [-10, 10] do together?
A) Limit how far the card can move: 12 points sideways, 10 up or down
B) Make the swipe win only after 12 points sideways, and give up if the finger first moves 10 points up or down
C) Slow the card down to 12 points per frame
D) Make the swipe and the scroll run at the same time
Why: Whichever threshold the finger crosses first wins the touch: sideways first is a swipe, vertical first is a scroll. Without them, a crooked scroll moved the card 54 points (measured).
A is wrong: They are thresholds for starting, not limits on movement.
C is wrong: Nothing about speed.
D is wrong: That would be useSimultaneousGestures.
Q10
SwipeToRemove asks when offset.get() < -reach || event.velocityX < flick (reach 120, flick -800). Theo flicks a card 70 points to the left at 1,200 points per second. What happens?
A) Nothing: 70 points is short of the reach
B) It asks: a quick flick counts even when it is short
C) The card is removed at once
D) The card stays where he left it
Why: Far OR fast: -1,200 points per second is below -800. The card springs back either way, and the screen asks; nothing is removed without "Yes, remove it" (Lesson 7.2's Exercise 1; Lab 7.2 Task 1).
A is wrong: Distance is one of two ways in.
C is wrong: A swipe only asks.
D is wrong: withSpring(0) brings it back.
Q11
Why does every waiting card also have a visible Remove button, and when would accessibilityActions be the better choice?
A gesture is a shortcut, never the only way (WCAG 2.5.1): the button reaches screen readers, keyboards, switch and voice control. accessibilityActions fit a row that is ONE accessible element with no controls inside
Why: The queue card has its own buttons, so it is not one accessible element: a visible Remove is the way in for everyone (Lesson 7.2, Every Gesture Needs Another Way). Lesson 8.1 audits it with a screen reader.
Q12
In a FlashList of 300 swipe rows, each row keeps const [asking, setAsking] = useState(false). Item 2 is swiped, then the list is scrolled far down. What can happen?
A) Nothing: every row has its own state
B) The app crashes when the row is recycled
C) FlashList refuses to recycle rows that have state
D) A row nobody touched asks "Item 95: remove it?": the recycled row kept Item 2's state
Why: FlashList reuses the row component for another item, state included (measured). Keep "which item is asking" in the parent, keyed by id, and pass extraData so the rows draw again (Lesson 7.2, Swipe Rows in Long Lists).
A is wrong: Recycling is exactly what shares the state.
B is wrong: No crash: a wrong question, which is worse.
C is wrong: It recycles every row.

✖ 1 problem (1 error, 0 warnings)

The solution: npm test; npm run typecheck and npm run lint print nothing after their two > lines; npx expo export --platform web bundles it:

 PASS  src/lab.test.tsx
  ✓ Task 1: 12 of 40 gloves is 30 %, and the fill is that wide
  ✓ Task 1: never more than 100 %, never a division by 0
  ✓ Task 1: the fill glides to its new width (a CSS-style transition, 400 ms)
  ✓ Task 2: with full motion, the dot pulses forever

  ✓ Task 2: screen readers skip the dot and read the words

  ✓ Task 3: the count lands exactly on its target: withTiming, never a spring
Tests:       8 passed, 8 total

What each task shows: (1) filledPercent clamps with Math.min(100, Math.max(0, ...)) and returns 0 for a stock of 0; the fill is an Animated.View with transitionProperty: 'width', transitionDuration: 400, transitionTimingFunction: 'ease-out' (accept the props on a plain View only with the question "what moves it on a phone?": Jest keeps any style); (2) useReducedMotion() and !reduceMotion && styles.pulsing, plus accessibilityElementsHidden and importantForAccessibility="no" (accept aria-hidden, Session 19's choice); (3) count.set(withTiming(bags, { duration: 900, easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic) })): the lint rule react-hooks/immutability and the overshooting spring are the lesson's first and third Common Mistakes.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 7.1 · Animation with Reanimated 4
  1. B So the animation stays smooth even when the JavaScript thread is busy with React, the network or the queue: React says once where the count should go and how; the UI thread works out every frame by itself. Whatever else the JavaScript thread is doing (parsing an answer, drawing a list) cannot make the animation late.
  2. D So React draws the number once per visible step, not once per frame: The count changes every frame, but the text only needs to change when it reaches a new step. Sending only new steps to React draws the component about 42 times for 42 pieces, not once per frame for a whole second.
  3. A It shows her total at once, without counting or bouncing: withTiming and withSpring default to ReduceMotion.System: with the setting on, they jump to the end. The tally shows the total at once, with no count and no bounce, and the words are the same as for everyone.
  4. C A CSS-style transition: transitionProperty: 'width' with a duration on an Animated.View: The width is a style that changes because state changed, which is exactly what a CSS-style transition is for: React draws the new width, and transitionProperty plus transitionDuration make it glide, with no shared value and no Hook.
  5. B A red screen: the UI thread cannot call a React setter, which must go through scheduleOnRN: On a phone the reaction runs on the UI thread, where a React state setter is not a worklet. react-native-worklets stops with an error about synchronously calling a Remote Function on the UI Runtime. scheduleOnRN runs the setter on the JavaScript thread instead.
Lesson 7.2 · Gestures
  1. D So the card follows the finger on the UI thread, even while the JavaScript thread is busy: Gesture Handler recognizes the gesture on the UI thread and its callbacks are worklets, so the card can follow the finger through a shared value every frame, however busy the JavaScript thread is. React only hears about the result, through scheduleOnRN.
  2. B There is no GestureHandlerRootView around the app: Gesture Handler checks for the root view in development on every platform except the web, so the web preview works while a phone stops with GestureDetector must be used as a descendant of GestureHandlerRootView. One root view around the app in the root layout fixes it.
  3. C activeOffsetX: [-12, 12] and failOffsetY: [-10, 10] on the pan gesture: activeOffsetX makes the pan wait for 12 points of sideways movement, and failOffsetY makes it give up after 10 points up or down, so a vertical drag goes to the scroll view and a sideways one to the swipe.
  4. A She moves to the card's Remove button, double taps it, then answers Yes, remove it: Every card has a Remove button, labeled with the cleanup's name for screen readers; it leads to the same question as the swipe, which is announced as a live region. The swipe is a shortcut, never the only way.
  5. B A different item, one nobody swiped, shows the question: FlashList reuses Item 2's row for an item coming on screen, and the row's own state comes with it: the course measured Item 95 asking to be removed. Keep the asking id in the parent, keyed by id, or use useRecyclingState.

Exit Ticket

  1. Timing or spring: a number must land exactly on its target, so withTiming; a spring overshoots (the measured 0 … 49 … 41 … 42), which suits a scale bounce and lies on a total.
  2. Setter in a worklet: on a phone the callback runs on the UI thread, which cannot call a React setter: a red screen (per the library's code). scheduleOnRN(setAsking, id). The web preview runs worklets on its one thread, so it hides the mistake.
  3. Two doors: a swipe far (120 points) or fast (800 points per second) enough, and the Remove button; both lead to the question. Neither while the queue is sending.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Thinks timers live on their own thread: draw the two lanes again.
  • Q2 A or D. Fixes the tool instead of the code: the rule protects the compiler's assumptions.
  • Q3 B or C. Likes springs everywhere: run the measured spring count from Common Mistakes.
  • Q4 A, B or C. Has not separated Reanimated's functions from CSS-style animations: Lab 7.1 Task 2.
  • Q8 B. Trusts the web preview as a phone: the kit's Jest run (P2) and the lesson's card.
  • Q12 A. Has not met recycling: Lesson 3.2, then the lesson's measured "Item 95".
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 19: Motion Polish and Accessible Apps

Answers and reasoning for every Session 19 item: the warm-up, both self-checks, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson pages' own quizzes and the exit ticket.

Session 19 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

  1. Tally counts every frame on the UI thread. Why is its number a shared value and not React state? **Answer:** State would re-render the component on the JS thread every frame (60 times a second or more), competing with everything else React does; a shared value changes on the UI thread, and only the number to show reaches React, through scheduleOnRN (Lesson 7.1, the skipper and the helm).
  2. A gesture callback calls a React setter directly. Why does the web preview not catch it? **Answer:** The web preview runs worklets on the JS thread, so the call works there; on a phone the callback runs on the UI thread and needs scheduleOnRN (Lesson 7.1's mistakes; 7.2's callbacks are worklets too).
  3. Why does every waiting card have a visible Remove button when the swipe already removes it? **Answer:** Every gesture needs another way (WCAG 2.5.1): screen-reader users, switch users and anyone who never discovers the swipe press the button (Lesson 7.2). Today's 8.1 audit relies on that.

7.3 · Motion Polish: self-check

Q1
Every time Theo opens the Logbook, all ten cards fade in. Which change stops that and still lets a log that arrives while he watches fade in?
A) Remove entering={FadeIn} from the cards
B) Wrap the content in <LayoutAnimationConfig skipEntering>
C) Render <ReducedMotionConfig mode={ReduceMotion.Always} /> on the Logbook
D) Give each card a new key on every render
Why: skipEntering skips the entering animations of everything inside while the wrapper itself first mounts; views that mount later still animate (Lesson 7.3, The first render is not news).
A is wrong: Then a real arrival does not fade in either.
C is wrong: That stops all motion, for everyone, everywhere.
D is wrong: A new key makes React mount a new view every render: more fading, not less.
Q2
Only lottie-react-native was installed. The phone is fine, tsc and the linter are silent, and npx expo export --platform web ends like this. What fixes it?
Error: Unable to resolve module @lottiefiles/dotlottie-react from ~/shoreline/shoreline-crew/node_modules/lottie-react-native/lib/module/LottieView/index.web.js: @lottiefiles/dotlottie-react could not be found …
...
Import stack:
 ...
 src/components/LogSentMark.tsx
 | import "lottie-react-native"

 src/app/log/[id].tsx
 | import "@/components/LogSentMark"
A) npx expo install @lottiefiles/dotlottie-react
B) A development build: Lottie is not in Expo Go
C) Import the JSON file with require() instead of import
D) npx expo start --clear
Why: Read the import stack from the bottom up: the screen imports LogSentMark, which imports lottie-react-native, whose web file needs the dotLottie player, an optional companion npm does not install for you. The kit removed it from a copy of the finished app and ran the lesson's command for real.
B is wrong: lottie-react-native is part of Expo Go, and the phone was fine: only the web bundle fails.
C is wrong: The JSON is not in the stack; the missing package is.
D is wrong: Nothing is cached wrong: the package is not there.
Q3
Maya's phone has Reduce Motion on. In Profile she turns "Reduce motion in this app" off, then sends a log. What does she see, with Lesson 7.3's code?
A) The check mark draws itself: the switch is off
B) Nothing at all: no mark and no heading
C) A still check mark: useReduceMotion() is phone || reduceMotion
D) The animation, at half speed
Why: The app's switch only ADDS: the phone's setting is the person's word for every app and always wins. LogSentMark reads useReduceMotion() and shows the still sent icon.
A is wrong: That would be the three-way choice the lesson rejects: never turn motion back on against the phone.
B is wrong: The heading always says it in words; only the mark changes.
D is wrong: No setting slows Lottie down.
Q4
Why does the root Stack get animation: reduceMotion ? 'fade' : 'default', when <ReducedMotionConfig> is already rendered?
A) ReducedMotionConfig only works in development
B) The web preview needs it to slide screens
C) Fades are faster to bundle than slides
D) Screens slide with the phone's native navigation, not with Reanimated, so ReducedMotionConfig never reaches them
Why: The lesson's motion inventory: Reanimated functions and layout animations follow ReducedMotionConfig; Lottie and native screen transitions do not, so useReduceMotion() covers them. Expo Router's types say the option works only on iOS and Android: check it on a phone.
A is wrong: It works in any build; only its warning is development-only.
B is wrong: The web preview has no slide to begin with.
C is wrong: Bundling has nothing to do with it.
Q5
Lab 7.3's starter decides reduceMotionFrom(phoneReduces, appSwitch) with return appSwitch. Who does that hurt, and what is the one-line fix?
A volunteer whose PHONE asks for less motion and whose app switch is off gets full motion back; return phoneReduces || appSwitch
Why: The switch may only add to the phone's setting (Lesson 7.3, An In-App Switch: The Decision). The lab's first test fails on exactly that case: reduceMotionFrom(true, false) must be true.
Q6
A teammate found a lovely free confetti animation on a marketplace for the final project. What do you check before it ships in a store app?
A) Who made it, and that its license allows your use, commercial use included
B) Only that it is a JSON file and not a GIF
C) That it loops, so the celebration lasts
D) Nothing: free files on marketplaces are free to use
Why: A Lottie file is artwork, and artwork has an owner. Each file has its own license (LottieFiles shows one on every animation: read it); keep the source and license next to the file (Lesson 7.3, Where did this animation come from?).
B is wrong: Being JSON says nothing about who owns it.
C is wrong: Play once at the moment that matters, and stop (battery, and motion with a job).
D is wrong: Some "free" files on other sites were simply copied from someone else.

8.1 · Accessible Mobile Apps: self-check

Q7
Lesson 8.1's mental model is the radio call. Name the parts a screen reader says for one element, in order, and the prop (or props) React Native gives you for each.
Label (accessibilityLabel, or the text inside), role (accessibilityRole), state (aria-checked, aria-expanded, aria-disabled…), value (accessibilityValue) and sometimes a hint (accessibilityHint)
Why: A good call is short, never repeats itself, and never makes the listener guess; decoration is radio silence (aria-hidden). The exact words and order belong to each screen reader.
Q8
npx tsc --noEmit prints this after a change in Icon.tsx. What fixes it?
src/components/Icon.tsx:35:6 - error TS2322: Type '{ name: …; size: …; tintColor: …; "aria-hi...' is not assignable to type 'ViewProps'.
  Types of property '"aria-hidden"' are incompatible.
    Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'boolean | undefined'.

35     <SymbolView
        ~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/components/Icon.tsx:35
A) Change SymbolView to View
B) Write aria-hidden on its own (it means true), not aria-hidden="true"
C) Add // @ts-ignore above the line
D) Remove accessibilityElementsHidden
Why: In HTML, ARIA attributes are strings; in React Native, aria-hidden is a boolean. The kit's run is Lesson 8.1's card word for word; Lab 8.1 shows the same error on a Text.
A is wrong: SymbolView is right; only the value's type is wrong.
C is wrong: That hides the error and keeps a string where a boolean belongs.
D is wrong: Icon keeps that prop on purpose: SymbolView on iPhone is Expo's own native view (Lesson 8.1, Step 3).
Q9
Before Lesson 8.1, Maya (VoiceOver) heard nothing when her waiting log was sent; Theo (TalkBack) heard "Log sent. Thank you!". Which change made the iPhone speak?
A) aria-live="polite" instead of accessibilityLiveRegion
B) accessibilityRole="alert" on every text
C) A bigger font for the heading
D) On iOS, AppText calls AccessibilityInfo.announceForAccessibilityWithOptions(words, { queue: true })
Why: accessibilityLiveRegion is Android only (React Native's documentation); iPhone needs to be asked. Because all 21 live texts go through AppText, one effect there covers them; queue: true lets VoiceOver finish what it is saying (Lab 8.1, Task 2 does the same on a new component).
A is wrong: That is the web's attribute: react-native-web makes it from accessibilityLiveRegion; VoiceOver on a phone never sees it.
B is wrong: An alert role on everything would make every text urgent.
C is wrong: Size has nothing to do with being announced.
Q10
At the largest text size, two tide times side by side run into each other. Which fix comes FIRST?
A) allowFontScaling={false} on both times
B) A fixed width and height for each tile
C) When useWindowDimensions().fontScale is large, put the tiles on top of each other
D) A smaller font size for everyone
Why: Layouts that let text grow first, a different layout at a large fontScale second, maxFontSizeMultiplier only on text that is already big, allowFontScaling={false} never (Lesson 8.1, The Phone's Text Size; Lab 8.1, Task 3).
A is wrong: It switches the person's setting off, for exactly the person who needed it.
B is wrong: A fixed size for text is the problem, not the fix ("M ee t").
D is wrong: Everyone loses so that nothing changes shape.
Q11
In Lab 8.1's solution, a test does screen.getByText('☑') on a packed row. Jest prints this. Why?
● the picture of the state, found like a screen reader would
  Unable to find an element with text: ☑
A) The ☑ is aria-hidden, and Testing Library, like a screen reader, skips hidden elements unless a query asks for them
B) Jest cannot render emoji
C) The row was never rendered
D) getByText only finds buttons
Why: React Native Testing Library finds elements the way a screen reader meets them. The picture of the state is decoration: the checkbox's role and aria-checked already say it. { includeHiddenElements: true } finds it anyway, which is how the lab's tests check that it is there.
B is wrong: It is a plain Unicode character, rendered like any text.
C is wrong: The checkbox called Gloves is on the screen in the same test.
D is wrong: getByText finds any text a screen reader could reach.
Q12
The audit found every Profile setting read twice ("Reduce motion in this app", then "…, switch, off"), and only the 40 by 20 switch answered a finger. What did Lesson 8.1 do?
A) Wrapped each row in a View with accessible, so the row and the switch are one element
B) The row became a Pressable that flips the value (not a stop of its own); the words are aria-hidden; the switch keeps its label and takes no touches
C) Removed the words and kept only the switch
D) Made the switch bigger with transform scale
Why: One row, one target, one stop: accessible={false} and tabIndex={-1} on the row, aria-hidden words, the Switch with accessibilityLabel={label} and pointerEvents: 'none' (measured: a tap on the words, the gap or the switch flips it once).
A is wrong: Grouping a control away: an accessible view hides the switch inside it from screen readers as a control of its own.
C is wrong: People need the words; voice control users say them.
D is wrong: A scaled switch is still a small target with words that do nothing.

The Lesson Pages' Own Quick Quizzes

Lesson 7.3 · Motion Polish
  1. B layout={LinearTransition} on the cards that move: A layout transition animates a view whose position or size changed because something else changed. The second card moved up, and LinearTransition on it turned the jump into a glide.
  2. D The first screenful fades in once; rows scrolled into view and removed rows do not animate: FlashList recycles rows instead of mounting and unmounting them. Only the first screenful mounts, so it fades in; rows scrolled into view and removed rows do not animate at all (measured: 38, 0 and 0 animations).
  3. A A still check mark above Log sent. Thank you!: loadMotion subscribed to reduceMotionChanged, so the store already knows. useReduceMotion returns true, and LogSentMark shows the still check mark. No restart is needed, unlike Lesson 7.1.
  4. C Lottie's own player draws the check mark, and it never hears Reanimated's setting: ReducedMotionConfig changes what ReduceMotion.System means for Reanimated's animations. The check mark is drawn by Lottie's own player, which knows nothing about Reanimated, so the component has to decide itself, with the same hook.
  5. A They still show their totals at once: the phone's setting wins: The app's switch only adds: useReduceMotion returns phone OR switch, so the phone's setting still wins, and the note under the switch says so. An app should never turn motion back on against an accessibility setting.
Lesson 8.1 · Accessible Mobile Apps
  1. C The words and the switch were two elements; the words are now hidden and the row presses the switch: The words were a text element and the switch, with the same words as its label, was a second one. Hiding the words with aria-hidden leaves one stop, the switch, and the row as a Pressable makes the whole row the touch target.
  2. A accessibilityLiveRegion is Android only; iPhone needs announceForAccessibility: React Native's documentation lists accessibilityLiveRegion as Android only. On iPhone an app asks VoiceOver to speak with AccessibilityInfo.announceForAccessibility, which AppText now does for every live text.
  3. D A different layout when fontScale is large: two tallies to a row: Change the layout, not the text: with fontScale at 1.5 or more, each tally takes at least 40 percent of the row and the row wraps, so they sit two to a row. A cap like maxFontSizeMultiplier is for text that is already large, and only after the layout works.
  4. B The mechanical problems axe can find are fixed; a screen reader and large text on a phone are still needed: Automated checks catch part of the mechanical problems, and on React Native they check the web translation of the props. Reading order, what the screen readers really say, announcements and large text need a person with a phone.
  5. A "Logbook (2), not sent yet": Label in Name: the accessible name contains the visible words, at the start, so speaking what you see matches. The extra not sent yet helps screen-reader users without getting in the way.

Exit Ticket

  1. The phone wins: no, the tally does not count. useReduceMotion() is phone || reduceMotion, true; ReducedMotionConfig switches to Always and the tally's withTiming jumps to its end. The app's switch can only add.
  2. Beyond Reanimated: the Lottie log-sent mark (LogSentMark shows the still sent icon from useReduceMotion()) or the native screen transitions (the root Stack's animation: 'fade', phones only). The tally's CSS color highlight is kept on purpose (a dissolve in place).
  3. Only a phone: what VoiceOver or TalkBack actually say and in which order; whether announcements arrive (iPhone); how the largest text lays out (the web preview's text size is always 1); whether targets are easy to hit with a thumb; native headers and tab bars (any two).

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A. Removes motion instead of choosing when it plays. Show the demo: open the Logbook (nothing moves), then Send now (the new entry fades in).
  • Q2 B. Thinks every new package needs a development build. The phone was fine: the stack names a .web.js file.
  • Q3 A. Expects the app's switch to override the phone. Back to the decision table: never turn motion back on against the phone.
  • Q8 C. Silences the checker instead of reading it. Read the LAST line of the error first.
  • Q10 A. The most common real-world "fix". Ask whose setting it switches off.
  • Q12 A. Groups the control away: the lesson's Elements section and the 4.1 decision to keep two buttons on the card.
