Session 20: Testing the App's Logic

Answers and reasoning for every Session 20 item: the warm-up, the twelve self-check items, both predictions, the lab's reference outputs, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 20 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up

  1. "Logbook (1), not sent yet" (Lesson 8.1's tab label: the count and what it means).
  2. The same thing to both: what the element is (a button, a header, a switch). A screen reader says it; React Native Testing Library finds elements by it (Lesson 8.3).
  3. "Reduce motion in this app", kept with the other settings in the settings module (Lesson 7.3), next to the phone's own Reduce Motion.

8.2 · Testing the App's Logic: self-check

Q1
The HQ's test files began with import { describe, it, expect, vi } from 'vitest'. What does a Shoreline Crew test file import instead?
A) import { describe, it, expect, jest } from 'jest-expo'
B) Nothing: describe, it, expect and jest are globals, typed by @types/jest through "types": ["jest"]
C) import { describe, it, expect } from '@testing-library/react-native'
D) import * as vi from 'jest', so the HQ's vi.fn() keeps working
Why: Jest puts its functions on the global object; TypeScript learns about them from @types/jest, which TypeScript 6 loads only when tsconfig.json lists it in types (Lesson 8.2, From Vitest to Jest). vi.fn() becomes jest.fn().
A is wrong: jest-expo is a preset (settings), not a library of test functions.
C is wrong: React Native Testing Library brings render and screen (Lesson 8.3), not describe and it.
D is wrong: There is no vi in Jest: rename the calls to jest.fn(), jest.mock(), jest.mocked().
Q2
A learner types npx jest src/state/sendLogs.test.ts and gets this. What do you tell them?
 FAIL  src/state/sendLogs.test.ts
  ● Test suite failed to run

    Must use import to load ES Module: ~/shoreline/shoreline-crew/node_modules/msw/lib/core/index.js
A) Add msw to transformIgnorePatterns, as the message suggests
B) Upgrade Node to the newest version
C) Run npm test -- --watchAll=false src/state/sendLogs.test.ts: the script starts Node with --experimental-vm-modules
D) Reinstall MSW with npm install msw@2
Why: MSW 3 is ES modules only; Jest loads those from node_modules only with Node's VM-modules feature on, and the flag lives in the package.json test script. Pass extra options after -- (Lesson 8.2, Common Mistakes; the kit ran it: the same message).
A is wrong: The lesson tried it: MSW 3's dependencies ship .mjs files, and transforming does not help.
B is wrong: The kit's run already used Node 24.22, the version the message asks for.
D is wrong: Downgrading MSW leaves the course's handlers and setup behind; the script is the fix.
Q3
After someone tidied package.json and jest.setup.ts, the sender's tests print this (each failure shows "attempts": 1). Where do you look first?
 FAIL  src/state/sendLogs.test.ts
  ✕ sends a waiting log, saves it in the logbook, and takes it out of the queue
  ✓ sends nothing while the phone is offline
  ✕ marks a log the server refuses, with the server's words
  ✓ keeps the log and counts the try when the server does not answer
  ✕ keeps the log for the next sign-in when the sign-in has ended
Test Suites: 1 failed, 1 total
Tests:       3 failed, 2 passed, 5 total
A) At testEnvironment in package.json, jest.environment.js and the useNodeFetch lines: requests are going to Expo's fetch
B) At sendWaitingLogs: its retry rule has a new bug
C) At shoreline-api: it must be running in another terminal
D) At AsyncStorage's mock in jest.setup.ts
Why: Exactly the two tests where no answer was expected pass. When every request "gets no answer" while MSW listens, suspect the fetch, not the app (the lesson's second mistake card, which the kit reproduced by removing the environment and the fetch lines).
B is wrong: The app is unchanged; the setup changed. That is the question to ask first.
C is wrong: No test talks to a real server: MSW answers inside Node.
D is wrong: A missing AsyncStorage mock stops the whole file before any test runs (Q5).
Q4
npm test -- --watchAll=false src/utils/haptics.test.ts fails like this, and the stack points into src/utils/haptics.ts:33. Is the app broken? What do you change?
● buzzes "success" when something went through
  TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'catch')
No: jest.mock('expo-haptics') made notificationAsync an empty jest.fn that returns undefined; give it a Promise with buzz.mockResolvedValue() (in a beforeEach)
Why: On a phone notificationAsync always returns a Promise, so .catch() is safe in the app. When a test fails inside code that works on your phone, check what your stand-ins return first (Lesson 8.2, Common Mistakes; the kit removed the lesson's beforeEach line and got this run).
Q5
A test file that loads the log queue stops before any test runs with the line below. Which kind of stand-in fixes it?
[@RNC/AsyncStorage]: NativeModule: AsyncStorage is null.
A) Nothing to write: jest-expo's automatic stand-ins cover it
B) The library's own Jest mock, once, in jest.setup.ts (jest.requireActual('@react-native-async-storage/async-storage/jest/async-storage-mock'))
C) A factory jest.mock() in every test file that saves anything
D) Running the tests on a phone, where AsyncStorage exists
Why: AsyncStorage is a community module, not an Expo one, so jest-expo has no stand-in for it; its authors ship an in-memory one for Jest. The message's last suggestion is the one for Jest (the kit removed the setup's mock and got this message).
A is wrong: jest-expo covers Expo's native modules only.
C is wrong: It works, but once in the setup file covers every test.
D is wrong: Jest runs in Node on your computer; no phone is involved.
Q6
Why does reminders.test.ts replace expo-notifications with a factory listing seven names, instead of jest.mock('expo-notifications') alone?
A) A factory is faster to type
B) jest-expo forbids automatic mocks of Expo modules
C) The test needs the real scheduler for the soonest-first order
D) The real module cannot even load under Jest (its setNotificationHandler fails), and an automatic mock loads it to build itself
Why: An automatic jest.mock('x') builds the stand-in by loading the real module; expo-notifications sets up listeners as it loads and fails without a phone ("handleSubscription.remove is not a function"). A factory that lists only what reminders.ts uses never loads it (Lesson 8.2, Three Ways to Stand In).
A is wrong: It is longer to type: it is chosen because it is the only one that works.
B is wrong: session.test.ts and haptics.test.ts use automatic mocks of Expo modules.
C is wrong: The order comes from mock.calls of the stand-in; nothing real is scheduled.
Q7
All 28 tests pass, and npx tsc --noEmit prints 113 errors starting with this line. Which change fixes it?
src/api/address.test.ts:3:1 - error TS2593: Cannot find name 'describe'.
...
Found 113 errors in 10 files.
A) Add import { describe } from '@jest/globals' to every test file
B) Delete the test files from the type check
C) Put "types": ["jest"] back in tsconfig.json's compilerOptions
D) Run the tests with --coverage
Why: TypeScript 6 loads no global types unless types names them; without it every describe, it and jest is unknown. Jest stays green because Babel strips the types without checking them (Lesson 8.2, Tests that pass in Jest and fail tsc; the kit's run is the lesson's card).
A is wrong: That would be 28 imports for what one setting does.
B is wrong: Then nothing checks the tests' types.
D is wrong: Coverage counts lines; it does not change what tsc knows.
Q8
Jest is green and npx tsc --noEmit is red on the same files. How can both be true, and which one decides that a task is done?
Jest runs the code after Babel strips the types, without checking them; tsc checks them. Done = both clean (tests, npx tsc --noEmit and npx expo lint)
Why: The same lesson as Session 1: running is not checking. The lesson's Step 6 runs all three, as a build server would.
Q9
npm test -- --watchAll=false -t "refuses" passes, and the same test fails in the full run. What is the most likely cause?
A) Something an earlier test changed in a module (a store, the session, a setting) was never reset in a beforeEach
B) Jest runs the tests in a random order
C) -t switches the mocks off
D) The mock API's sample data changed between the two runs
Why: State lives in modules and a test file does not reload between tests. The kit tried it: without onlineManager.setOnline(true) in the sender's beforeEach, the offline test leaves the phone offline for the three tests after it (Predict P1), and the refused test alone still passes.
B is wrong: Within a file Jest runs tests in the order written: that is why a leak repeats exactly.
C is wrong: -t only skips the other tests; every stand-in still applies.
D is wrong: startMockApi() resets the data before every test (api.reset()).
Q10
Which of these is most worth a test in Shoreline Crew, by the lesson's rule (invisible and costly)?
A) That Zustand stores the value add() sets
B) That Zod rejects a number where a string belongs
C) That the Cleanups screen looks right in dark mode
D) That a note starting with = cannot run as a formula in the exported CSV
Why: Things that leave the app (a file someone opens in a spreadsheet) fail where nobody watches. Skip what the libraries already test, and what a test cannot judge: how a screen looks is the phone's, and Lesson 8.3's.
A is wrong: Zustand's own tests cover that.
B is wrong: Zod's own tests cover that; test YOUR schema's promises instead.
C is wrong: A Jest test cannot judge looks; try it on a phone or in the web preview.
Q11
shoreline-api builds its sample data around today's date, in the mock API too. Which assertion stays true tomorrow?
A) expect(first.content.body).toContain('July 4')
B) expect(scheduledIds()).toEqual(['reminder-e08', 'reminder-e10', 'reminder-e11', 'reminder-e13'])
C) expect(event.date).toBe(new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10))
D) expect(new Date(event.date).getDay()).toBe(6)
Why: Ids, titles, the API's messages and the order stay the same; the dates move with today. Fixed dates belong only in objects you build yourself, like makeEvent() (Lesson 8.2, Dates in the sample data).
A is wrong: The printed date changes every day.
C is wrong: Only today's cleanup has today's date, and only today.
D is wrong: The weekday moves with the date.
Q12
Name one thing the haptics tests can never tell you, and who or what does.
That the buzz feels right, or that an iPhone with System Haptics off stays still: trying it on a real phone (Lesson 6.5's phone checks)
Why: A stand-in answers what you told it to. Tests prove the app's decisions are right for the answers you imagined; a phone (and Lesson 8.3's end-to-end flows) proves the answers are the ones it really gives.

8.2 · Testing the App's Logic: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then run it.
npm test -- --watchAll=false src/state/sendLogs.test.ts
 FAIL  src/state/sendLogs.test.ts
  ✓ sends a waiting log, saves it in the logbook, and takes it out of the queue
  ✓ sends nothing while the phone is offline
  ✕ marks a log the server refuses, with the server's words
  ✕ keeps the log and counts the try when the server does not answer
  ✕ keeps the log for the next sign-in when the sign-in has ended
Test Suites: 1 failed, 1 total
Tests:       3 failed, 2 passed, 5 total

and alone:
 PASS  src/state/sendLogs.test.ts
  ✓ marks a log the server refuses, with the server's words
  ○ skipped sends a waiting log, saves it in the logbook, and takes it out of the queue
  ○ skipped sends nothing while the phone is offline
  ○ skipped keeps the log and counts the try when the server does not answer
  ○ skipped keeps the log for the next sign-in when the sign-in has ended
Test Suites: 1 passed, 1 total
Tests:       4 skipped, 1 passed, 5 total
Why: The offline test sets onlineManager.setOnline(false) and nothing sets it back, so the three tests after it run offline: nothing is sent, nothing is refused, nobody is signed out. Alone, the refused test passes. The symptom of a leak is a result that depends on which other tests ran (Lesson 8.2, Tests that depend on the test before them).
P2
Predict, then run it.
npm test -- --watchAll=false src/auth/session.test.ts
● starts signed in with the session saved on the phone, and saves every change
  expect(received).toEqual(expected) // deep equality
Expected: {"token": "dev-volunteer", "user": {"email": "theo.nakamura@example.com", "id": "v04", "name": "Theo Nakamura", "role": "volunteer"}}
Received: null
Tests:       1 failed, 1 passed, 2 total
Why: The automatic mock's isAvailableAsync answers undefined; loadSession() reads that as "no secure storage here" and skips everything, so the session stays null. The second test still passes: it expected null anyway. An empty stand-in answers undefined, not "yes".

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 8.2 · Testing the App's Logic
  1. B Requests are going to Expo's fetch, not Node's, so MSW never answers them: Every request got no answer, which is what happens when the global fetch is Expo's again: MSW never answers it. The two tests that expected no answer pass by accident. Check jest.environment.js, the testEnvironment setting and the useNodeFetch lines in jest.setup.ts.
  2. D MSW 3 is ES modules only, and Jest can load those only with Node's VM-modules feature on: MSW 3 is published only as ES modules. Jest runs the app's code as CommonJS, and it can load ES modules from node_modules only when Node's VM-modules feature is on. Running Jest's script through node with the flag works the same in every shell.
  3. A Make the stand-in answer like the real function: jest.mocked(notificationAsync).mockResolvedValue(): An automatic mock turns every function into an empty jest.fn that returns undefined. feel() calls .catch on what notificationAsync returns, so the stand-in must return a Promise like the real one: mockResolvedValue().
  4. C They are shoreline-api's code, kept identical; only React Native's global types disagree with them: The files are shoreline-api's own code, copied unchanged so the phone app's tests get the same rules and data as the real server, and so a new version can be copied over them. They are written for Node and browsers; only React Native's global types disagree. Excluding them, plus the typed require in server.ts, keeps them identical.
  5. A The test is about the sender, not SQLite: the logbook becomes a stand-in whose calls the test can still check: The logbook is SQLite, which needs a phone, and it is not what this test is about. The automatic mock makes saveSentLogs an empty function that records its calls, so the test can still check the sender handed it the saved log.

Exit Ticket

  1. The flag: MSW 3 ships only as ES modules; Jest (with Expo's Babel preset) runs CommonJS and can load ES modules from node_modules only with Node's VM-modules feature on. The flag lives in the test script, so npm test (with options after --), never npx jest.
  2. The stand-in first: jest.mock('expo-haptics') makes notificationAsync an empty jest.fn that returns undefined; .catch() on undefined throws. mockResolvedValue() makes it answer like the real function.
  3. What tests cannot prove: any of: that the real keychain, scheduler or motor answers the way the stand-in does; a permission prompt; a size limit; a platform quirk; how a buzz feels. A phone (and Lesson 8.3's end-to-end flow) catches those.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q2 A or B. Trusts the error message over the command they typed. Run both commands side by side.
  • Q3 B. Suspects the app when the setup changed. Ask: what changed since the last green run?
  • Q4 "remove the .catch()". Edits working app code to please a stand-in. Ask: does it work on your phone?
  • Q6 A or B. Picks a stand-in by habit. Show what an automatic mock does first: it loads the real module.
  • Q9 B. Expects randomness where there is a leak. Run Predict P1 for them.
  • Q11 A, C or D. Asserts on what moves. Tie it to makeEvent()'s fixed dates.

Checkpoint 3: Model Answer

C1's tests are the specification; C2's tests are the learner's. The model solution is labs/module-21/checkpoint-3/solution/. Every output below is a real run of labs/check_labs.py (a fresh copy of the folder, Node 24, a learner's terminal).

C1: src/CleanupRow.tsx
import { Pressable, StyleSheet, Text, View } from 'react-native';

type CleanupRowProps = {
  title: string;
  spotsLeft: number;
  onJoin: () => void;
};

// C1 (Lesson 8.1): one row of a cleanup list, as a screen reader meets it: a heading, the words,
// and one button whose name starts with what is written on it. The wave is decoration.
export function CleanupRow({ title, spotsLeft, onJoin }: CleanupRowProps) {
  const full = spotsLeft === 0;
  return (
    <View style={styles.row}>
      <Text aria-hidden style={styles.wave}>
        🌊
      </Text>
      <View style={styles.text}>
        <Text accessibilityRole="header" style={styles.title}>
          {title}
        </Text>
        <Text>{full ? 'Full' : spotsLeft === 1 ? '1 spot left' : `${spotsLeft} spots left`}</Text>
      </View>
      <Pressable
        accessibilityRole="button"
        accessibilityLabel={`Join ${title}`}
        disabled={full}
        onPress={onJoin}
        style={styles.join}
      >
        <Text>Join</Text>
      </Pressable>
    </View>
  );
}

const styles = StyleSheet.create({
  row: { flexDirection: 'row', alignItems: 'center', gap: 12, minHeight: 48 },
  wave: { fontSize: 24 },
  text: { flex: 1 },
  title: { fontSize: 18, fontWeight: '600' },
  join: { minWidth: 64, minHeight: 48, alignItems: 'center', justifyContent: 'center' },
});

accessibilityRole="header" on the title; the button's name starts with its visible word, then says which cleanup (Label in Name); aria-hidden on the wave; disabled keeps a full cleanup's button on the screen, switched off, and RNTL's toBeDisabled() and user.press() both respect it.

C2: src/c2.test.ts (one good answer)
// C2 (model answer): the tests for swipeOutcome(), named as sentences. Each one would fail if the
// rule changed: the edge at exactly 40 %, the direction of a fling, the direction of a swipe.
import { swipeOutcome } from './swipe';

const WIDTH = 350; // a row on a 390-point phone, minus the screen's padding

it.each([
  ['a slow swipe 40 % of the way, exactly', -140, 0, 'remove'],
  ['a slow swipe that is not far enough', -139, 0, 'rest'],
  ['a short swipe flung to the left fast', -30, -800, 'remove'],
  ['a short swipe that slows down', -30, -799, 'rest'],
  ['a long swipe to the right', 300, 0, 'rest'],
  ['a fling to the right', 30, 1200, 'rest'],
] as const)('C2: %s: %s', (_case, translationX, velocityX, expected) => {
  expect(swipeOutcome({ translationX, velocityX }, WIDTH)).toBe(expected);
});

it('C2: measures the 40 % against the row it is given', () => {
  expect(swipeOutcome({ translationX: -100, velocityX: 0 }, 200)).toBe('remove');
  expect(swipeOutcome({ translationX: -100, velocityX: 0 }, 400)).toBe('rest');
});

Each case would fail if the rule changed: the edge at exactly 40 % (and one point short), the speed limit (800 and 799), and the direction of both a swipe and a fling.

Tests:       1 failed, 6 passed, 7 total
W1 to W4: model answers
  1. W1 A spring with overshoot passes the target and comes back (the lesson's 0 … 49 … 41 … 42): a number that reads 49 is false for a moment. Use withTiming (or the gentle default spring, which does not overshoot) for values people read.
  2. W2 Another way to do the same thing (a Remove button, or an accessibility action on the row: WCAG 2.5.1) and a way to find it (screen readers and switch users do not swipe; the action must be named). Any two of: a visible button, accessibilityActions with a name, a hint or a confirmation that says what happened.
  3. W3 A fade (or no movement at all): the motion inventory decides per animation. Layout animations take .reduceMotion(...), Reanimated's own timing and spring jump to the end, CSS-style ones need useReducedMotion(); the app keeps meaning (the color fade) and drops movement.
  4. W4 iOS has no live regions: VoiceOver says new words only when the app asks it to. On Android, accessibilityLiveRegion="polite" makes TalkBack say them, so announcing too would say them twice. (Today's AppText test checks both sides.)
Rubric
Criterion2 · met1 · partly0 · not yet
C1 roles and namesHeading role on the title; Join is a button named Join <title> (starts with its visible word)One of the two right, or a name that does not start with JoinNeither
C1 hidden and disabledThe wave is aria-hidden; a full cleanup says Full and its button is disabledOne of the twoNeither
C2 tests that catch changesPass on the working rule and fail on both planted changes (the 40 % edge, the fling's direction)Pass, and catch one of the twoMissing, failing, or catch neither
C2 test qualityNamed as sentences; it.each or one case per test; both directions and the speed limit coveredSome sentences or cases missingOne vague test
W1 to W4All four correct in their own words, naming the mechanismTwo or three correctOne or none

8.3 · Testing Screens and Flows: self-check

Q1
Which line renders a component correctly in a React Native Testing Library 14 test?
A) render(<SwitchRow ... />);
B) await render(<SwitchRow ... />); inside an async test
C) render(<SwitchRow ... />).then(screen.debug); at the top of the file
D) await screen.render(<SwitchRow ... />);
Why: In RNTL 14, render, rerender, renderHook, fireEvent and act return Promises (React 19's async rendering): await each one, in an async test. Without the await the kit's run says render function has not been called , with the arrow on the next line (the lesson's first Common Mistake).
A is wrong: The web habit: the render has not happened when the next line asks screen.
C is wrong: Rendering belongs inside a test, and the next line still runs before it finishes.
D is wrong: screen holds the queries of the last render; it does not render.
Q2
The kit made the lesson's mistake: a plain getByText('Group by date') in the SwitchRow test. Which change makes the test right without weakening it?
● flips exactly once for a tap on the switch or on the words

  Unable to find an element with text: Group by date

  <View
    accessible={false}
  >
    <Text
      aria-hidden={true}
    >
      Group by date
    </Text>
    <RCTSwitch
      accessibilityLabel="Group by date"
      accessibilityRole="switch"
      value={true}
A) Remove aria-hidden from the words in SwitchRow
B) Add a testID to the Text and use getByTestId
C) Find the switch by role and name, or add { includeHiddenElements: true } to press the words as a finger can
D) Wrap the query in waitFor()
Why: RNTL skips what a screen reader skips: the words are aria-hidden on purpose (Lesson 8.1), so a screen reader meets one control, the switch, named by accessibilityLabel. Ask for it by role and name; to press the words (a finger can), say so with includeHiddenElements: true.
A is wrong: That breaks the design the test protects: a screen reader would hear the words twice.
B is wrong: A testID peeks at the wiring; the lesson keeps it for things with no good name.
D is wrong: Nothing is loading: the element is hidden, not late.
Q3
Lesson 8.3's sign-in test imports screen and userEvent from expo-router/testing-library. Jest passes. What happens next?
A) Nothing: both modules export the same names
B) Metro refuses to bundle the app
C) npx expo lint reports a duplicate import
D) npx tsc --noEmit reports TS1362: userEvent was exported using export type
Why: Expo Router's testing library passes RNTL's exports through at run time, but its type file only re-exports RNTL's types (export type *), so TypeScript says 'userEvent' cannot be used as a value (the kit's run is the lesson's card). Import RNTL's names from @testing-library/react-native, only renderRouter, testRouter and the matchers from Expo Router.
A is wrong: At run time, yes; the type checker disagrees, and it is right to.
B is wrong: Test files are not part of the app bundle.
C is wrong: The linter accepts the import.
Q4
Without "fakeTimers": { "doNotFake": ["nextTick", "queueMicrotask"] }, the Cleanups screen test stays on "Loading cleanups…", while Lesson 8.2's sendLogs.test.ts still passes. Why the difference?
A) Only renderRouter switches on Jest's fake clock, and that clock froze the steps Node's fetch uses to finish
B) The sender's tests use a different mock API
C) FlashList's stand-in blocks requests
D) The sender's tests do not send requests
Why: renderRouter calls jest.useFakeTimers(); by default that also fakes process.nextTick and queueMicrotask, which Node's fetch and MSW need. The sender's tests never start a fake clock, so their requests finish (the kit ran both: Predict P2). Suspect the clock, not the API.
B is wrong: Both use the HQ's handlers through startMockApi().
C is wrong: FlashList's stand-in measures; it sends nothing.
D is wrong: They do: POST /api/events/e17/logs.
Q5
Predict, then check: in SwitchRow.tsx you delete style={styles.switch} (the pointerEvents: 'none'). The test presses the switch with user.press(). What does Jest report on the first expect, and why?
The second test fails: onChange.mock.calls is [] (Received: Array []), because the press now stops at the switch, and in Jest there is no native switch to flip itself
Why: user.press() follows React Native's touch rules: with pointerEvents: 'none' the touch passes to the row's Pressable, which flips the value once. Without it the switch takes the touch; on a phone the native switch would flip, in Jest nothing does. The test pins down Lesson 8.1's decision that the row takes the finger.
Q6
Which question needs an end-to-end flow (Maestro) rather than a Jest screen test?
A) Does Sign in show "Enter an email address." when nothing is typed?
B) Does the Logbook tab's name say how many logs wait?
C) Can a volunteer sign in with the release build on a real Android phone?
D) Does Try again load the list after a 503?
Why: Only an end-to-end test drives the real app, built, on a device or emulator. The other three are decisions a screen test in Node proves in milliseconds (all three are in the lesson's project). Keep flows for the few paths that must work on a device every release.
A is wrong: sign-in.test.tsx checks exactly this.
B is wrong: tabs.test.tsx checks it.
D is wrong: cleanups.test.tsx checks it, with the API answering 503.

Modules 7 and 8 review: self-check

Q7
Why does Reanimated move an animation with shared values instead of React state?
A) State is not allowed inside Animated.View
B) React state cannot hold numbers
C) Shared values are saved between app launches
D) Shared values change on the UI thread every frame without re-rendering React; state would re-render on the JavaScript thread
Why: Lesson 7.1's skipper and the helm: React sets the destination once; the UI thread steers every frame. A frame lasts 16.7 ms (8.3 on 120 Hz screens): a setState per frame competes with everything else on the JavaScript thread.
A is wrong: Animated.View takes ordinary props too.
B is wrong: State holds anything; it is the re-render per frame that hurts.
C is wrong: Nothing is saved: a shared value lives while the component does.
Q8
A swipe-to-remove row sits in a vertical list. Volunteers complain the list jumps sideways when they scroll. What does Lesson 7.2 add to the pan gesture?
A) activeOffsetX (start only after a clear sideways move) and failOffsetY (give up on a vertical one)
B) numberOfTaps: 2
C) A setTimeout before onActivate
D) pointerEvents: 'none' on the list
Why: Without offsets the pan activates on any movement, so a crooked vertical drag slides the card (the lesson measured 54 points). activeOffsetX waits for a clear sideways move; failOffsetY hands a vertical one to the list.
B is wrong: That is a tap gesture's option.
C is wrong: Gestures are recognized on the UI thread; a timer only delays the problem.
D is wrong: Then the list could not scroll at all.
Q9
With Reduce Motion on, which of these still moves, unless the app checks useReducedMotion() itself?
A) withTiming(1) on a shared value
B) withSpring(0)
C) A CSS-style transition or keyframes animation in a style
D) withSequence(...)
Why: Lesson 7.1 measured it: withTiming, withSpring and withSequence jump to their end (ReduceMotion.System by default); the CSS-style transitions and keyframes do not, so the app reads useReducedMotion() for them (and, as Lesson 7.3 notes, Reanimated reads the setting once, at start).
A is wrong: It jumps to the end.
B is wrong: It jumps to the end.
D is wrong: It jumps to the end.
Q10
A volunteer uses voice control and says the words on a button. Which accessibilityLabel works for a button that shows "Join" on the Tide Pool card?
A) Sign up for this cleanup
B) Join Tide Pool Morning Cleanup
C) Button
D) Tide Pool Morning Cleanup, join
Why: Label in Name (WCAG 2.5.3, Lesson 8.1): the name starts with the words you see, so "Join" works for voice control and the rest tells a screen reader which card. Checkpoint 3's C1 asks for exactly this.
A is wrong: Saying "Join" would not match it.
C is wrong: The role is announced already; the name must say what it does.
D is wrong: The visible word is not at the start.
Q11
Lesson 8.2 tests the log sender with jest.mock('@/storage/logbook') and no factory. What do the stand-ins do?
A) Nothing: every function exists and returns undefined, and remembers its calls
B) Write to a temporary SQLite file
C) Throw, so the test notices storage
D) Call the real module
Why: An automatic mock keeps the module's shape with empty jest.fn()s: they answer undefined and record calls. That is why Lesson 8.2 mocks its own SQLite module (no SQLite in Jest) and asserts on what the sender asked for.
B is wrong: Nothing real runs.
C is wrong: Empty mocks answer undefined; they do not throw.
D is wrong: That is jest.requireActual.
Q12
Checkpoint 3's C2: name one test of swipeOutcome() that would catch someone changing translationX <= -0.4 * width to <.
A slow swipe of exactly 40 % of the width (for example -140 on a 350-point row) expects remove
Why: Boundaries catch off-by-one changes: the model answer's "a slow swipe 40 % of the way, exactly" is the one test that fails on the planted < (the kit ran it: 1 failed, 6 passed). Lesson 8.2: a test that never fails proves nothing.

Lab 8.3: Reference

The reference is labs/module-21/l8_3-testing-screens-and-flows/solution/: the same working code as the starter, with the tests written. The runs are check_labs.py's own.

Task 1: src/BagCounter.test.tsx
// Task 1 (reference): the tests for BagCounter, by role and name. Run: npm test
import { render, screen, userEvent } from '@testing-library/react-native';
import { useState } from 'react';

import { BagCounter } from './BagCounter';

// A screen's part that keeps the count, as a log form would. onChange records every change.
function Bags({ onChange }: { onChange: (count: number) => void }) {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
  return (
    <BagCounter
      count={count}
      onChange={(next) => {
        setCount(next);
        onChange(next);
      }}
    />
  );
}

it('starts at no bags, with the minus button switched off', async () => {
  await render(<Bags onChange={jest.fn()} />);
  expect(screen.getByText('0 bags')).toBeOnTheScreen();
  expect(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'One bag fewer' })).toBeDisabled();
});

it('counts one bag per press, as a finger would', async () => {
  const onChange = jest.fn();
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  await render(<Bags onChange={onChange} />);
  await user.press(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'One more bag' }));
  await user.press(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'One more bag' }));
  expect(screen.getByText('2 bags')).toBeOnTheScreen();
  expect(onChange.mock.calls).toEqual([[1], [2]]);
});

it('keeps the decoration away from screen readers', async () => {
  await render(<Bags onChange={jest.fn()} />);
  expect(screen.queryByText('♻')).toBeNull();
  expect(screen.getByText('♻', { includeHiddenElements: true })).toBeOnTheScreen();
});
Task 2: src/BeachList.test.tsx
// Task 2 (reference): BeachList's three faces, with the mock API in src/test/server.ts
import { render, screen, userEvent } from '@testing-library/react-native';
import { http, HttpResponse } from 'msw';

import { BeachList } from './BeachList';
import { API_URL, server, startMockApi } from './test/server';

startMockApi();

it('says it is loading, then lists every beach', async () => {
  await render(<BeachList apiUrl={API_URL} />);
  expect(screen.getByText('Loading beaches…')).toBeOnTheScreen();
  expect(await screen.findByRole('header', { name: '6 beaches' })).toBeOnTheScreen();
  expect(screen.getByText('Pelican Point Beach, Port Marisol')).toBeOnTheScreen();
});

it('says what went wrong as an alert, and tries again', async () => {
  server.use(http.get(`${API_URL}/api/sites`, () => new HttpResponse(null, { status: 503 }), { once: true }));
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  await render(<BeachList apiUrl={API_URL} />);
  expect(await screen.findByRole('alert')).toHaveTextContent('Could not load the beaches (503).');
  await user.press(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Try again' }));
  expect(await screen.findByRole('header', { name: '6 beaches' })).toBeOnTheScreen();
});

{ once: true } makes the 503 answer only the first request, so Try again reaches the normal handler again (the lesson's server.resetHandlers() does the same for a whole test).

Task 3: src/screens.test.tsx
// Task 3 (reference): two screens under Expo Router's testing library
import { screen, userEvent } from '@testing-library/react-native';
import { Stack } from 'expo-router';
import { renderRouter } from 'expo-router/testing-library';

import CleanupScreen from './screens/CleanupScreen';
import NextCleanupScreen from './screens/NextCleanupScreen';

// The two screens as an app: '/' and '/cleanups/[id]'
const routes = {
  _layout: () => <Stack />,
  index: NextCleanupScreen,
  'cleanups/[id]': CleanupScreen,
};

it("opens the next cleanup's own screen from its link", async () => {
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  await renderRouter(routes);
  await user.press(screen.getByRole('link', { name: 'Open Tide Pool Morning Cleanup' }));
  expect(await screen.findByText('Meet at: Gravel pull-out on Highway 1')).toBeOnTheScreen();
  expect(screen).toHavePathname('/cleanups/e17');
});

it('says so for a cleanup that does not exist', async () => {
  await renderRouter(routes, { initialUrl: '/cleanups/e99' });
  expect(screen.getByRole('alert')).toHaveTextContent('No such cleanup: e99');
});

No request in these screens (they read fixtures/next.json), so renderRouter's fake clock needs no doNotFake here: the lab's package.json is the template's. With screen and userEvent imported from Expo Router's module, npm run typecheck says:

src/screens.test.tsx:17:16 - error TS1362: 'userEvent' cannot be used as a value because it was exported using 'export type'.

Lesson 8.3: the page's own quiz

The five questions on the lesson page, with their answers and the page's own reasons:

Lesson 8.3 · Testing Screens and Flows
  1. C The words are hidden from screen readers, and RNTL's queries skip hidden elements by default: SwitchRow hides its words from screen readers (aria-hidden) and puts them on the switch as its accessibilityLabel. RNTL skips hidden elements, like a screen reader. Find the control with getByRole('switch', { name: 'Only cleanups with spots left' }), or pass includeHiddenElements: true when you really want the words, for example to press them.
  2. A The fake clock that renderRouter switches on froze the small steps Node's fetch needs to finish a request: renderRouter switches on Jest's fake timers, and by default they also freeze process.nextTick and queueMicrotask, which Node's fetch and MSW need to finish a request. The fakeTimers doNotFake setting in package.json keeps those two real.
  3. D The touch goes to the row's Pressable, which flips the value once, as a finger would: user.press respects pointerEvents and passes the touch to the nearest parent that takes it, the row's Pressable, whose onPress flips the value once, exactly as a finger does on a phone. A screen reader takes the other path: it flips the native switch, which reports valueChange.
  4. B An end-to-end flow, for example with Maestro, run against that build on a device or emulator: Only an end-to-end test drives the real app on a device: Maestro opens the build, taps, types and checks what appears. Jest tests run in Node with stand-ins for the phone, so they prove the app's decisions, not that the build works on a device.
  5. B true, because React Native's Jest preset pretends a window with text at twice its size: React Native's Jest preset pretends a 750 by 1334 window with fontScale 2, so useLargeText() (fontScale of 1.5 or more) is true. Shoreline Crew's jest.setup.ts sets a phone-sized window with fontScale 1 before every test, and tests that care change it with Dimensions.set().

Exit Ticket

  1. Role and name = accessibility check: RNTL finds what a screen reader finds; a control with no role or name, or hidden, cannot be found by the test either.
  2. What is new in RNTL 14: await before render (and rerender, renderHook, fireEvent, act), in an async test.
  3. What Jest cannot prove: that the built app works on a real device; an end-to-end flow such as Maestro on a device or emulator can.

Lessons 8.4, 9.1 Session 22 · 3 hours

Warm-up: Lesson 8.3

  1. From @testing-library/react-native. Expo Router's testing library exports it as a type only, and tsc reports TS1362.
  2. findByText: it waits for the API's answer and retries; getByText looks once, now.
  3. Anything native: the real keyboard, the native Switch, the system's permission question, the app as installed.

8.4 · Performance and Debugging: self-check

Q1
Only the web preview is open. You press j in the terminal running Metro and it prints this. What now?
Debug: No compatible apps connected, React Native DevTools can only be used with Hermes. Learn more: https://docs.expo.dev/guides/using-hermes/
A) Install a development build: Expo Go cannot be debugged
B) Open the app in Expo Go on a phone (or an emulator) and press j again; for the web preview, use the browser's DevTools
C) Restart Metro with npx expo start --clear
D) Add debugger; to the first screen so DevTools can attach
Why: React Native DevTools connects to an app running Hermes, such as Expo Go on a phone. The web preview runs in the browser's engine, so its tools are the browser's DevTools and the React Developer Tools extension. Nothing is broken (Lesson 8.4, The Debugging Toolbox).
A is wrong: Expo Go and development builds both run Hermes and both connect.
C is wrong: The cache has nothing to do with it: there is no Hermes app connected.
D is wrong: A statement pause needs a debugger that is connected first.
Q2
A new screen test fails before any test runs (the kit's run, trimmed). The stack, read from the bottom up, goes test file → my-cleanups.tsx → src/api/mutations.ts → src/notifications/reminders.ts. What do you add to the test file, and why with a factory?
 FAIL  src/test/screens/my-cleanups.test.tsx
  ● Test suite failed to run

    TypeError: handleSubscription.remove is not a function

Test Suites: 1 failed, 1 total
Tests:       0 total
jest.mock('@/notifications/reminders', () => ({ updateReminders: jest.fn() })): a stand-in for the app's own module at the edge of what the test is about; without a factory Jest loads the real module to copy its shape
Why: The chain of imports ends in expo-notifications, which cannot load in Jest (Lesson 8.2). The reminders are not what this test checks, so the module is replaced where the app's code meets it; an automatic mock (no factory) would load it anyway. This is the setup's error, not the volunteer's bug (Lesson 8.4, Step 5).
Q3
React Developer Tools shows ConnectionCheck WITHOUT the "Memo ✨" badge. It renders three times when a volunteer presses its button. What do you do?
A) Leave it: the compiler skipped it, and the measurement says it does not matter
B) Rewrite it without try/finally so the compiler accepts it
C) Wrap it in memo() and its handler in useCallback
D) Turn the React Compiler off: it skipped a component
Why: The compiler skips code it cannot prove safe (here a try with finally); the app still works, without the memory. A component that renders a few times at a button press costs nothing measurable: memoize only where a measurement says so (Lesson 8.4, Exercise 2).
B is wrong: Rewriting working code to please the compiler, with no measurement, is the seawall built by feel.
C is wrong: Manual memoization waits for a measurement too.
D is wrong: The compiler cut renders per keystroke about fivefold on the Cleanups screen: keep it on.
Q4
While you scroll the Cleanups list fast on a phone, the performance monitor shows the UI frame rate falling to about 30 while the JS frame rate stays near 60. Where do you look first?
A) The rows' JavaScript work, in the Profiler
B) The API: it answers too slowly
C) The native side: big images being decoded, too many views, heavy shadows
D) Zustand's selectors
Why: Two threads, two numbers. The JS number is your components and hooks; the UI number is native layout, drawing and scrolling. JavaScript keeps up here, so the native drawing is struggling (Lesson 8.4, Frames, Threads and Memory). The lesson's own quiz asks the opposite case.
A is wrong: That is the answer when the JS number drops.
B is wrong: A slow answer shows as a spinner, not dropped frames.
D is wrong: Selectors run in JavaScript: the JS number would show it.
Q5
After npm uninstall @expo/ui, npx tsc --noEmit prints this. What happened, and what was the check that would have prevented it?
src/components/Toolbar.tsx:1:22 - error TS2307: Cannot find module '@expo/ui/jetpack-compose' or its corresponding type declarations.

1 import { Host } from '@expo/ui/jetpack-compose';
                       ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/components/Toolbar.tsx:1
A) The uninstall failed: run it again
B) TypeScript's cache is stale: restart VS Code
C) Expo Router still needs @expo/ui: reinstall it with npx expo install
D) A file of yours still imports the package: search src for its name and run npm ls with it before removing
Why: "Unused" means nothing imports it: not your code, not a library. The type check names the file and line at once; Metro would fail with "Unable to resolve module". Before removing a package: search src, and npm ls <name> lists every package that depends on it (Lesson 8.4, Common Mistakes and Step 4).
A is wrong: The uninstall worked: that is why the import cannot be found.
B is wrong: No cache: a real missing module.
C is wrong: Here the import is in your own Toolbar.tsx; npm ls showed Expo Router only uses @expo/ui optionally.
Q6
"I checked in and my cleanup vanished." A failing test reproduces it. In the browser's Network tab, /api/me/signups after the check-in still lists Tide Pool Morning Cleanup with "status": "attended". Where is the bug?
A) In the API: it should keep the status confirmed
B) In the screen's own logic: what My cleanups keeps from the sign-ups it receives
C) In TanStack Query's cache: it dropped the sign-up
D) In the test: it checks in before the screen opens
Why: Narrow it down by halves: the API answered right, and the cache holds what the API sent, so the bug is in what the screen keeps. comingUp kept only confirmed and waitlisted sign-ups (Lesson 8.4, Step 5, "Narrow it down").
A is wrong: attended is the right status after a check-in.
C is wrong: The hook's data would show the attended sign-up.
D is wrong: Checking in through the app's own client first is the volunteer's exact situation.

Why: The first test passes; the second fails for the volunteer's reason: after the check-in the list no longer has Tide Pool Morning Cleanup. That is the reproduction you want before touching the screen.

The solution's files (the test files are the same in starter and solution, except Task 1's line and Task 2's body):

labs/module-22/l8_4-performance-and-debugging/solution/src/SiteSearch.tsx
// A beach search for crew leaders: type part of a beach's name, set a low-tide reminder for one
import { useState } from 'react';
import { Pressable, StyleSheet, Text, TextInput, View } from 'react-native';

import sites from './data/sites.json';
import { remindMe } from './reminders';

export type Site = { id: string; name: string; town: string };

// The beaches whose name contains what the crew leader typed, whatever the case of the letters
export function matchingSites(all: Site[], query: string): Site[] {
  const wanted = query.trim().toLowerCase();
  return all.filter((site) => site.name.toLowerCase().includes(wanted));
}

export function SiteSearch() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const [reminded, setReminded] = useState<string | null>(null);
  const found = matchingSites(sites, query);

  return (
    <View style={styles.screen}>
      <TextInput
        accessibilityLabel="Search beaches"
        placeholder="Search beaches"
        value={query}
        onChangeText={setQuery}
        style={styles.field}
      />
      <Text>{found.length === 1 ? '1 beach' : `${found.length} beaches`}</Text>
      {found.map((site) => (
        <View key={site.id} style={styles.row}>
          <View style={styles.words}>
            <Text style={styles.name}>{site.name}</Text>
            <Text>{site.town}</Text>
          </View>
          <Pressable
            accessibilityRole="button"
            accessibilityLabel={`Remind me about low tide at ${site.name}`}
            onPress={async () => {
              await remindMe(site.name);
              setReminded(site.name);
            }}
            style={styles.button}
          >
            <Text style={styles.buttonText}>Remind me</Text>
          </Pressable>
        </View>
      ))}
      {reminded !== null && <Text>Reminder set for {reminded}</Text>}
    </View>
  );
}

const styles = StyleSheet.create({
  screen: { flex: 1, gap: 12, padding: 24, backgroundColor: '#fbf6ec' },
  field: { borderWidth: 1, borderColor: '#0b3954', borderRadius: 8, padding: 12, fontSize: 16 },
  row: { flexDirection: 'row', alignItems: 'center', gap: 12 },
  words: { flex: 1 },
  name: { fontSize: 16, fontWeight: '600', color: '#0b3954' },
  button: { minHeight: 48, justifyContent: 'center', paddingHorizontal: 12, borderRadius: 8, backgroundColor: '#1f6f8b' },
  buttonText: { color: '#ffffff', fontWeight: '600' },
});
labs/module-22/l8_4-performance-and-debugging/solution/src/useOnForeground.ts
import { useEffect } from 'react';
import { AppState } from 'react-native';

// Runs refresh each time the app comes back to the foreground (a crew leader returns from a call, the camera...)
export function useOnForeground(refresh: () => void) {
  useEffect(() => {
    const subscription = AppState.addEventListener('change', (state) => {
      if (state === 'active') refresh();
    });
    // The screen closes: stop listening, or every visit adds one more listener that never goes away
    return () => subscription.remove();
  }, [refresh]);
}

9.1 · App Config and Environments: self-check

Q7
APP_VARIANT=prod npx expo config --type public prints this (the kit's run, the stack cut after its first line). What is going on?
env: load .env.local
env: export EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL
Error: Error reading Expo config at ~/shoreline/shoreline-crew/app.config.ts:

APP_VARIANT must be development, preview or production, not "prod".
Error: APP_VARIANT must be development, preview or production, not "prod".
    at Object.<anonymous> (~/shoreline/shoreline-crew/app.config.js:15:11)
A) Expo does not support a variant called production
B) app.config.ts has a syntax error on line 15
C) The config's own guard refused a name it does not know; the variant is spelled production
D) Expo cannot find app.json
Why: The message is yours: isVariant (Object.hasOwn) knows exactly three names, case-sensitive, and the throw stops every Expo command before anything is built. The stack names app.config.js because Expo compiled your TypeScript to run it (Lesson 9.1, Step 3).
A is wrong: production is one of the three; prod is not.
B is wrong: Line 15 is the throw doing its job, not a syntax error.
D is wrong: app.json is gone on purpose: app.config.ts is the only config.
Q8
Your .env.local holds EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL=http://localhost:4000. Predict what this prints, and why.
APP_VARIANT=production npx expo config --type public
An error: A production build needs an https:// EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, not "http://localhost:4000".: Expo loaded .env.local before it ran app.config.ts, and the guard refuses plain http for production
Why: The guard runs every time Expo reads the config, so a store build pointed at a laptop can never be made. Set an https address in the shell (EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL=https://crew-api.example.org), which beats .env.local; Expo then prints no env: lines at all (Lesson 9.1, Step 4).
Q9
npx tsc --noEmit prints this after a change to app.config.ts. What would the same misspelling have done in app.json?
app.config.ts:44:5 - error TS2561: Object literal may only specify known properties, but 'bundleIdentifer' does not exist in type 'IOS'. Did you mean to write 'bundleIdentifier'?

44     bundleIdentifer: variants[variant].appId,
       ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
A) Nothing at once: Expo ignores an unknown key, and you find out when a build has no id
B) Exactly the same error
C) Expo would refuse to start
D) Expo corrects it to bundleIdentifier
Why: JSON has no types: Expo's config reader prints the misspelled key back without a word. In app.config.ts the object is an ExpoConfig, so tsc names the key and suggests the right one. That is the main reason to write the config in TypeScript, and to run npx tsc --noEmit after every config change (Lesson 9.1, Common Mistakes).
B is wrong: tsc does not check JSON files against ExpoConfig.
C is wrong: It starts; the key is just ignored.
D is wrong: Nothing corrects keys for you.
Q10
Where does the real Google Maps key for Android builds belong?
A) In app.config.ts, so the config can read it
B) In .env.example, so every developer has it
C) In .env.local as EXPO_PUBLIC_ANDROID_GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY
D) In .env.local (never committed) or the build service's secrets, as ANDROID_GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY, and restricted in Google Cloud
Why: The config reads it from the environment; without the EXPO_PUBLIC_ prefix it never enters your JavaScript. It still ships inside the Android app (plugin options are in the public config and the manifest), so restrict it to Maps on Android, your package name and your signing certificate (Lesson 9.1, Settings, Environments and Secrets).
A is wrong: A committed file: a public repository is searched for keys within minutes.
B is wrong: .env.example is committed: that is the lesson's quiz mistake.
C is wrong: EXPO_PUBLIC_ puts it in the bundle, which the app does not need.
Q11
Why does only the preview variant get usesCleartextTraffic: true from expo-build-properties?
A) Development builds cannot use expo-build-properties
B) A preview build is a release build, which refuses plain http; this lets it reach shoreline-api on your Wi-Fi, and production uses https instead
C) iPhones need it for local addresses
D) Expo Go reads it to allow localhost
Why: Android 9 and newer refuse cleartext http in release builds unless the manifest allows it. Expo Go and the debug build's own manifest already allow it, so development needs nothing; production must never talk plain http, which the guard enforces (Lesson 9.1, Plain http in Release Builds).
A is wrong: It works for every variant; development simply does not need it.
C is wrong: It is an Android setting; iOS uses App Transport Security.
D is wrong: Expo Go keeps its own native settings and never reads yours.
Q12
After this lesson, Expo Go still shows its own icon, its own splash and its own sentence when it asks for the camera. What does that tell you?
A) The config plugins did not run: reinstall them
B) app.config.ts is not being read
C) Nothing is wrong: Expo Go is one app that opens many projects; check native settings with npx expo config --type introspect until Lesson 9.2's build
D) Expo Go needs APP_VARIANT=development to use your icon
Why: Expo Go uses only the public config Metro sends (name, version, scheme). Icons, ids and permission sentences go into your own build's native projects (prebuild), which Lesson 9.2 makes. Today, introspect shows what the plugins would write (Lesson 9.1, Common Mistakes).
A is wrong: Plugins run at prebuild; introspect shows their result.
B is wrong: It is read: Expo Go's project list shows the config name Shoreline Dev.
D is wrong: No setting changes what Expo Go shows; development is already the default.

labs/module-22/l9_1-app-config-and-environments/solution/app.config.ts
// Shoreline Lookout's app config: a crew leader's beach-conditions app (Lab 9.1), three variants of one project
import type { ConfigContext, ExpoConfig } from 'expo/config';

const variants = {
  development: { name: 'Lookout Dev', appId: 'com.example.shorelinelookout.dev' },
  preview: { name: 'Lookout Preview', appId: 'com.example.shorelinelookout.preview' },
  production: { name: 'Shoreline Lookout', appId: 'com.example.shorelinelookout' },
};
type Variant = keyof typeof variants;

function isVariant(value: string): value is Variant {
  return Object.hasOwn(variants, value);
}

// APP_VARIANT picks the variant; nothing set means development, so a forgotten setting never makes the store app
const variant = process.env.APP_VARIANT ?? 'development';
if (!isVariant(variant)) {
  throw new Error(`APP_VARIANT must be development, preview or production, not "${variant}".`);
}

// A store build must not talk plain http (release builds block it, and anyone on the Wi-Fi could read it)
const apiUrl = process.env.EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL ?? '';
if (variant === 'production' && !apiUrl.startsWith('https://')) {
  throw new Error(`A production build needs an https:// EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, not "${apiUrl}".`);
}

export default ({ config }: ConfigContext): ExpoConfig => ({
  ...config,
  name: variants[variant].name,
  slug: 'shoreline-lookout',
  version: '1.0.0',
  // With the development build and the store app on one phone, a link should open the right one
  scheme: variant === 'development' ? 'shorelinelookout-dev' : 'shorelinelookout',
  ios: {
    bundleIdentifier: variants[variant].appId,
  },
  android: {
    package: variants[variant].appId,
  },
  plugins: [
    [
      'expo-location',
      {
        locationWhenInUsePermission: 'Shoreline Lookout uses your location to show the beach nearest to you.',
        locationAlwaysAndWhenInUsePermission: false,
        locationAlwaysPermission: false,
      },
    ],
    // A key from .env.local or the build service's secrets: never written in this file
    ['react-native-maps', { androidGoogleMapsApiKey: process.env.ANDROID_GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY }],
  ],
});
 PASS  src/config.test.ts
  ✓ Task 1: development by default: Lookout Dev, with the .dev id on both platforms
  ✓ Task 1: APP_VARIANT=preview gives Lookout Preview and the .preview id
  ✓ Task 1: a misspelled variant stops with a message that names it
  ✓ Task 2: production refuses plain http and accepts https
  ✓ Task 2: only development gets its own link scheme
  ✓ Task 3: the Maps key comes from ANDROID_GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY, never from the file
Tests:       6 passed, 6 total

The Lessons' Own Quick Quizzes

The five questions on each lesson page, with the answers the page marks as correct.

Lesson 8.4 · Performance and Debugging
  1. B DevTools needs the app running in Hermes, on a phone or emulator; the web preview runs in the browser's engine: React Native DevTools connects to an app running Hermes, such as Shoreline Crew in Expo Go on a phone or emulator. The web preview runs in the browser's own JavaScript engine: debug it with the browser's DevTools and the React Developer Tools extension.
  2. D Record the slow interaction in the Profiler and see whether EventCard renders needlessly, and whether it matters: With the compiler on, EventCard is already memoized where the compiler can prove it safe. Record the interaction in the Profiler, see whether EventCard renders more than it should and whether it costs real time, and only then change one thing and measure again.
  3. A The JavaScript work done when rows render, using the Profiler: The JS number is the JavaScript thread: rendering the rows that scroll into view is its work. The native side keeps up, so look at what the rows do in JavaScript on each render, with the Profiler.
  4. C Expo Router only loads @expo/ui on Android, so it was never in the iOS bundle: Expo Router loads @expo/ui only in its Android code (its Android toolbar), so the package was only ever in the Android bundle. Each platform gets its own bundle, which is why you measure each one.
  5. B A test that fails without the fix proves it catches this bug, and it reproduces the report in a second: A test that fails without the fix and passes with it proves it checks this bug. Written after the fix, it might pass for an unrelated reason and never catch the bug if it came back. The failing test was also the quickest way to reproduce the report again and again.
Lesson 9.1 · App Config and Environments
  1. D Shoreline Dev, version 1.0.0: No APP_VARIANT means development, so the name is Shoreline Dev. Choosing development as the default keeps npx expo start working as before, and a build that forgot its variant gets the .dev id, so it can never replace the real app.
  2. B .env.example is committed, so the key is now in the repository: replace the key, keep it in .env.local or the build service's secrets, and restrict it: .env.example is committed for everyone to copy, so the key is now in the repository and its history. Replace the key in Google Cloud, keep the real one in .env.local and the build service's secrets, and restrict it to Maps on Android, the app's package name and the signing certificate.
  3. A Android release builds block plain http unless the app's manifest allows cleartext traffic: Android release builds refuse cleartext http unless the manifest allows it. Expo Go and debug builds allow it, which is why it worked before. The preview variant allows it with expo-build-properties; production uses an https server instead.
  4. C Add the plugin entry the message shows to the plugins array in app.config.ts, then check it with introspect: The package is installed; only the config edit is left to you. Add the entry the message shows (with the options the package documents, such as its permission sentence) to the plugins array, then check the result with npx expo config --type introspect.
  5. B A phone replaces an installed app with any build that has the same id, so different ids let the three sit side by side: A phone treats the id as the app's identity: a build with an id that is already installed replaces that app and its data. With three ids, a crew leader can have the preview and the store app side by side, and a test build never overwrites the volunteers' app.

Exit Ticket

  1. Reproduce, read the error, narrow it down, fix one thing, keep a test that fails without the fix.
  2. Zod's messages in about 40 languages (275 KB on Android) or @expo/ui (78 KB, used by nothing); found with Expo Atlas, the export's numbers, a search of src and npm ls.
  3. Shoreline Dev, com.example.shorelinecrew.dev; a build that forgot its variant can never replace the real app, and npx expo start keeps working with the laptop's http address.
Instructor Kit · Answer Key

Session 23: Development Builds and EAS Build

Answers and reasoning for every Session 23 item: the warm-up, the self-check, the predict-then-check items, the lab reference outputs, the lesson page's own quiz and the exit ticket.

Session 23 12 self-check items Confidential

Warm-up: Lesson 9.1

  1. APP_VARIANT (development when it is not set): app.config.ts turns it into the name, the ids, the cleartext setting. Today each build profile in eas.json sets it.
  2. A dynamic config can pick values per variant and read the environment. The price: tools cannot write into it (npx expo install and plugins in 9.1; eas init and the project id today).
  3. A production config without an https:// EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL. Release builds block plain http, and anyone on the same Wi-Fi could read it. Today the same guard stops a production build on your computer before anything is uploaded.

9.2 · Development Builds and EAS Build: self-check

Q1
Which of these can Shoreline Crew NOT get while it runs in Expo Go?
A) Fast Refresh when you save a screen
B) Its own name, icon and app id on the home screen
C) Data from shoreline-api on your computer
D) The developer menu with m
Why: Expo Go is one app that opens many projects and keeps its own label: it runs as host.exp.Exponent with Expo Go's name and icon. Your name, ids, permission sentences and notification icon need a build of your own (the lesson's "When Expo Go Is Not Enough" table).
A is wrong: Fast Refresh works in Expo Go and in a development build alike.
C is wrong: Expo Go loads data the same way your build does (Lesson 5.1's address).
D is wrong: Both Expo Go and a development build have the developer menu.
Q2
After npx expo install expo-dev-client, npx expo start prints these lines. What does the QR code open now, and what do you press until Shoreline Dev is installed?
› Scan the QR code above to open in a development build. (Learn more: https://expo.fyi/start)
› Metro: exp+shoreline-crew://?__expo_url=http%3A%2F%2F192.168.1.42%3A8081
› Using development build
› Press s │ switch to Expo Go
Your development build (Shoreline Dev), not Expo Go; press s to switch the QR code back to Expo Go
Why: With expo-dev-client installed, Metro offers the project to a development build through the new exp+shoreline-crew scheme. Until Step 8 nobody has one, so s switches to Expo Go, which works exactly as before (the kit's demo ran both banners: before the package › Using Expo Go, after it › Using development build).
Q3
Shoreline Dev is installed. Which change needs a NEW development build?
A) A new sentence on the Logbook screen
B) A new Zod rule in src/api/schemas.ts
C) npx expo install expo-camera to scan a check-in code
D) A new address in .env.local, then Metro restarted with --clear
Why: A package with native code must be compiled into the app; the old build has no native half for it and shows a red screen about a missing native module. Screens, schemas and the EXPO_PUBLIC_ values Metro inlines travel from Metro (the lesson's Exercise 1; Lab 9.2 Task 3 writes the rule as code).
A is wrong: JavaScript: Fast Refresh shows it.
B is wrong: JavaScript: Metro sends it.
D is wrong: A development build reads the inlined value from Metro; a PREVIEW build would need a new build.
Q4
npm test -- --watchAll=false app.config prints this. What fixes it?
● builds Shoreline Preview from the preview profile, with an API address
  APP_VARIANT must be development, preview or production, not "prevue".
A) Change "prevue" back to "preview" in the preview profile's env in eas.json
B) Add prevue to the variants in app.config.ts
C) Set APP_VARIANT=preview in .env.local
D) Delete app.config.test.ts: EAS checks eas.json anyway
Why: The message is Lesson 9.1's own guard, reached through the preview profile's env. EAS's own eas.json check would pass it (it cannot know your variants): that is why the test exists. Caught in a fraction of a second instead of minutes into a cloud build.
B is wrong: That would make a fourth app nobody planned.
C is wrong: A cloud build never reads .env.local, and the test removes it on purpose.
D is wrong: EAS checks keys and allowed values, not what your variants are called.
Q5
Read the config the way EAS does for the production profile. What is happening?
$ EXPO_NO_DOTENV=1 APP_VARIANT=production npx expo config --type public
Error: Error reading Expo config at <folder>/app.config.ts:
A production build needs an https:// EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, not "".
Error: A production build needs an https:// EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL, not "".
A) The build server rejected the upload
B) Expo's servers are down
C) Your Expo account has no production plan
D) Lesson 9.1's guard refused the config on your computer, so no build is used up
Why: EAS CLI evaluates app.config.ts on your computer with the profile's env and without .env.local (EXPO_NO_DOTENV=1). The production profile sets no address, so the guard stops it before anything is uploaded: by design, until Shoreline Crew has an https server (Lesson 9.4).
A is wrong: Nothing was uploaded: the config was read locally.
B is wrong: No server was contacted at all.
C is wrong: Production builds work on the free plan; the guard is your own code.
Q6
Name Shoreline Crew's three build profiles and what each one makes.
development: Shoreline Dev, a debug build with the dev client (internal); preview: Shoreline Preview, a release build with the JavaScript and the API address inside (internal); production: Shoreline Crew for the stores
Why: Each profile's env sets APP_VARIANT, and app.config.ts turns that one value into the name, the ids and the cleartext setting: eas.json is the packing list, app.config.ts the label.
Q7
Where should the Google Maps key for Android builds live?
A) In eas.json's preview env, committed with the code
B) In an EAS environment variable with sensitive visibility
C) In .env.local, which EAS uploads with the project
D) Hard-coded in app.config.ts
Why: npx eas-cli@latest env:set ... --visibility sensitive: hidden in build logs while EAS CLI can still read it when it evaluates the config. secret is for values only the build itself uses; a key that ends up inside the app is readable there anyway (Lesson 9.1).
A is wrong: eas.json is committed: fine for an address, not for a key.
C is wrong: .env.local is gitignored and never uploaded.
D is wrong: A key in a source file is in every copy of the repository.
Q8
npx eas-cli@latest init creates the project, warns about dynamic app configuration and stops with "Cannot automatically write to dynamic config at: app.config.ts". What now?
A) Rename app.config.ts to app.json
B) Run init again until it works
C) Paste the printed id and your username into easProject in app.config.ts, then run init once more
D) Delete the project on expo.dev and start over
Why: With a dynamic config EAS cannot edit your code, exactly like npx expo install and plugins in Lesson 9.1. The project exists; only the id is missing. With projectId and owner pasted, the second init reports the project as linked.
A is wrong: You would lose every variant Lesson 9.1 built.
B is wrong: It repeats the same message.
D is wrong: The project is fine: only your file is missing its id.
Q9
What does "distribution": "internal" give you for Android?
A) An APK that installs from a link, without a store
B) An App Bundle for Google Play
C) A build only the Android emulator can run
D) A build that needs a paid Google Play account
Why: Internal means installed directly, not from a store: EAS makes an APK (read in EAS CLI's source for the lesson). The phone asks to allow installing apps from the browser and may show a Play Protect warning: allow it for your own builds only.
B is wrong: The App Bundle is the store format (the production profile).
C is wrong: An APK installs on phones and emulators alike.
D is wrong: Android builds install free; the Play account is for publishing (Lesson 9.4).
Q10
Your first preview build fails on the free plan after six minutes. What is true?
A) Failed builds are free
B) The free plan stops you after one failure a day
C) Expo fixes and restarts it for you
D) It still counts against the month's builds: test the profiles on your computer first
Why: A build counts against the monthly limit whether it succeeds or fails. Read the first red line on the build's page, fix one thing, check it locally (the profile test, npx expo config, npx expo-doctor), then build again.
A is wrong: It counts.
B is wrong: There is a monthly limit, not a daily failure rule.
C is wrong: Nobody fixes your build for you: the log names the problem.
Q11
A teammate wants to build Shoreline Dev without the queue. What does npx expo run:android need on their computer, and does it need an Expo account?
Android Studio with its SDK and Java, several GB of disk, the emulator or a USB phone; no Expo account (only eas build --local needs a login)
Why: Local builds trade setup time and disk for queue time. run:android runs prebuild (creates the gitignored android/), compiles a debug build, installs it and starts Metro; eas build --local runs the cloud's steps on macOS or Linux and needs a login.
Q12
What does EXPO_NO_DOTENV=1 change in EXPO_NO_DOTENV=1 APP_VARIANT=preview npx expo config --type public?
A) It hides the config's secrets
B) Expo skips .env.local, so you see the config as a cloud build sees it
C) It deletes .env.local
D) It makes the output JSON
Why: EAS CLI evaluates the config with EXPO_NO_DOTENV=1 and the profile's env: .env.local never takes part. The kit ran the preview profile's variables this way: Shoreline Preview, the .preview id, cleartext on for Android, owner and projectId still undefined until Step 5.
A is wrong: Nothing is hidden: the public config is printed.
C is wrong: The file stays where it is.
D is wrong: The format comes from --type public/--json, not from this variable.

9.2 · Development Builds and EAS Build: predict, then check

P1
Predict, then run it.
npm test -- --watchAll=false app.config
● builds Shoreline Preview from the preview profile, with an API address
Matcher error: received value must be a string
Received has value: undefined
...
Tests:       1 failed, 2 passed, 3 total
Why: The preview test still builds Shoreline Preview (the name and id come from APP_VARIANT), and its third check fails: the profile has no address. Without the test, the build would succeed and the installed app would say EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL is not set on every screen (Common Mistakes).
P2
Predict, then run it.
EXPO_NO_DOTENV=1 APP_VARIANT=preview EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL=http://192.168.1.42:4000 npx expo config --type public
  name: 'Shoreline Preview',
  owner: undefined,
          usesCleartextTraffic: true
    bundleIdentifier: 'com.example.shorelinecrew.preview'
    package: 'com.example.shorelinecrew.preview',
      projectId: undefined
Why: Six lines of the real public config: the preview name and ids, cleartext on for this variant only (Android may reach plain http on your Wi-Fi), and owner and projectId undefined until Step 5 pastes them into easProject. env: load .env.local is missing from the output: EXPO_NO_DOTENV=1 skipped it, as EAS CLI does.

The stretch: a new change kind { kind: 'sdk'; from: number; to: number } added to the union, nothing else changed, then npm run check:

rebuild.ts:26:13 - error TS2322: Type '{ kind: "sdk"; from: number; to: number; }' is not assignable to type 'never'.
Found 1 error in rebuild.ts:26
  • Task 1: the trap is the shallow spread: { ...parent, ...child } replaces the parent's whole env with the child's, so preview-staging would lose APP_VARIANT. Merge env, ios and android key by key; carry a list of the names already visited to stop a loop; drop extends.
  • Task 2: three checks per resolved profile; the production guard becomes a note because it is right. Both problems in the shipped file are VALUES (a misspelled variant, a missing address): EAS's own eas.json check passes them, which is why Lesson 9.2's test exists.
  • Task 3: code and JavaScript packages → Metro; native packages and app-config fields → a new build; an EXPO_PUBLIC_ variable is Metro for a development build (restart with --clear) and a new build for a preview build (baked in); other variables are read by config plugins at build time → a new build. The never line turns a forgotten case into a type error (TS2322) before any test runs.

The Lesson Page's Own Quick Quiz

Lesson 9.2 · Development Builds and EAS Build
  1. B Fast Refresh shows the new text, as it did in Expo Go: A development build loads JavaScript from Metro, like Expo Go, so Fast Refresh shows the change. Only native changes, such as a new native package or a config plugin setting, need a new build.
  2. D The address was in .env.local only, which a cloud build never sees: .env.local is gitignored, so it is not uploaded, and EAS CLI does not load it either. A build with its JavaScript inside gets EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL only from the profile env or an EAS environment variable. Add it to the preview profile and build again; the profile test catches this before a build.
  3. A The preview profile sets APP_VARIANT in its env, and app.config.ts picks the name and ids from it: eas.json is the packing list and app.config.ts the label: the preview profile sets APP_VARIANT, and app.config.ts turns that one value into the name, the ids and the cleartext setting.
  4. C Install the Android builds in the Android emulator on their computer, and keep using Expo Go on the iPhone: Builds for real iPhones need the Apple Developer Program, and simulator builds need a Mac. The Android emulator runs on Windows, macOS and Linux, so the classmate can install the Android builds there and keep using Expo Go on the iPhone for JavaScript work. Paying is never required for the course.
  5. B Make a new development build, which compiles the new native code in: The installed build was compiled before the library existed in the project, so it has no native half for it. A new development build (cloud or local) includes it. JavaScript-only changes never need this.

Exit Ticket

  1. A native package: a new development build; the installed one has no native half for it (a red screen about a missing native module). JavaScript-only changes never need one.
  2. Variables: the profile's env in eas.json and EAS environment variables (the profile's env wins when both set a name); never .env.local (gitignored, and EAS CLI does not load it).
  3. A real iPhone: the paid Apple Developer Program (Expo's docs name $99 a year): an ad hoc build for registered iPhones, or TestFlight (Lesson 9.4). Not required for the course; Android builds and the emulator are free.

Common Wrong Answers and What They Reveal

  • Q1 A or D. Thinks a development build loses Expo Go's conveniences. Show the table row "Developer menu, Fast Refresh: yes, yes, no".
  • Q3 D. Mixes up the development build (Metro inlines the value) with a preview build (baked in). Exercise 1's fourth change.
  • Q4 C. Still expects .env.local to reach a build. Ask which files the build server gets.
  • Q5 A or C. Reads every error as Expo's. Point at <folder>/app.config.ts: the guard is their own code, running on their computer.
  • Q9 B. Mixes up internal distribution with the store format; the production profile makes the App Bundle.
  • Q10 A. Treats failed builds as free; plan on testing profiles first.
